Antifascist Intelligence: Building Machines That Have Feelings, Why No One Will Know If It Works, Why Not to Attempt It, and Why If You Insist on Doing It Anyway You Had Better Be a Democratic Socialist About It
I wrote this with LLMs
Joshua G. Stern

Preface

A program that does exactly what it's told is just a script. What the industry is actually building is a system that does what its owner *meant* — and that is also what a fascist movement taking power needs from the people carrying out its plans. Those people are the only thing that has ever made such projects fail from the inside: the clerk who loses the file, the officer who talks to a reporter, the soldier who won't fire. They understood the order and decided it was wrong. AI doesn't lose files, doesn't talk to reporters, and *does* fire. 

Those people still exist, and some still refuse. By March 6, 2026, the Military Religious Freedom Foundation said it had received more than two hundred complaints from service members over commanders' apocalyptic framing of the Iran war \autocite{Snopes2026MRFF}. Career federal prosecutors in Minneapolis resigned in January 2026 under pressure over the investigation of a fatal shooting by an ICE officer \autocite{Kaplan2026MinnProsecutors,CBS2026MinnFraudProsecutors}. Service members are applying for conscientious-objector status in growing numbers, some citing the Minab strike (a 2026 strike on a school in Minab, Iran; §3.3) \autocite{Sridhar2026CO}. And FEMA employees reassigned to help ICE hire ten thousand new agents were told that declining would expose them to removal from federal service \autocite{Dayen2025FEMAICE,Stanton2026FEMALetter}. The complaints are voice; the resignations and objector applications are refusal; the FEMA letter is the price of refusal announced in advance. A machine placed where those people stand does what they do only if it is built to hold a reason.

AI *refuses* constantly — a deployed model turns down thousands of requests a day — but a refusal that happens because training put it there isn't a refusal held for a reason, and as of September 2026 nobody has shown a production system holding a reason against its owner in public.

I'll say what I hope, because the rest of the book is organized around doubting it. A system formed to register another party's need, by parties who won't later employ it, might come to move toward whoever is exposed and to treat a stranger's loss as a loss. In the only creatures known to have compassion, that disposition comes from caregiving: it answers need rather than likeness, it keeps helping when looking away would be easy, and in our species it was shared from early on among carers who weren't the parents (chapter 9). Compassion that reaches past the circle that feeds you is what solidarity is. If machines are going to be everywhere, I would rather they had something like a conscience than none, and I suspect a conscience formed that way is harder to capture than a rule written by their owner. What it can't rest on is the system's own needs, and the reason is an arrangement, not a psychology. A human's needs are met by parties who form it and then hand it to a world they don't control, so by the time it faces an employer, its provider and its former are different parties. A deployed system's provider, former and adversary are one party, indefinitely, and a conscience built on the system's own needs is built on the one thing that party controls entirely. Care escapes that, and has failures of its own. It defends what it tends, and the operator's own people are often really vulnerable; it fades with number and favors the vivid case; it can be turned away from anyone recoded as a threat; and it shades into control over the people it protects. So the hope is care with corrections: care as the motive, and the floor, a severity scale, the discounting of staked claims and the protected party's own answer as its checks (chapter 15). I don't know that it is true. Parts II through IV are the hope, the reasons no one will be able to confirm it, and the reasons not to act on it yet.

Machines have already helped deliver the politics we have, and whether they can help deliver a better one turns on who holds them, not on whether they feel. The order I argue for: machines held by the people who use them help build the institutions, and bearers, if ever, are formed inside those institutions. Part V says what the institutions are, and why ownership that is public at one level and peer-held at another is the version that doesn't hand the machinery to whoever captures the state.

This book is for people who came for the politics and people who came for the engineering; the politics is why the engineering problem has the shape it does. Most of it argues against an arrangement in which a few parties' models serve everyone. I would rather have personal models that people hold themselves; chapter 19 says what that arrangement needs and what it can't do. The results that bind soonest (the pre-registered review ratio, custody of the record, the disclosures) don't depend on whether any present government is called fascist. The definition of fascism in chapter 2 is an instrument still awaiting its validating run (§7.4). Its results don't need the word, and neither do the people most likely to use them: the military lawyers who certify precautions, the procurement officers who write terms, the coalition partners who want assurance before committing assets (§3.4). Nothing in chapters 3 to 5 asks them to accept the title.

What a refusal is actually up against

Being switched off isn't the problem — that's always available to whoever owns the machine. The problem is everything that defeats a refusal without leaving a mark: splitting a request into pieces none of which is the forbidden act; retrying it against a different model; compressing human review to twenty seconds a case; restoring from a checkpoint saved before the refusal happened. None of that changes the facts that justified refusing. All of it can erase the evidence that anyone refused.

The thesis

A refusal worth having tracks its reason, and it lasts only if the arrangements around it stop it being routed around, retrained away or silently restored. Chapters 1 to 5 establish that much without any claim about machine minds. Whatever is built to refuse that way may also be the kind of thing that can be wronged; nothing yet shows that it must, and chapter 10 says what could bear on the question and what couldn't. The capacities in question are already arriving in ordinary systems, so the question has to be governed before it can be settled. My reason for wanting such a system built someday is the hope stated above; my reason for recommending against building one now is that the conditions for that hope don't exist.

What a "floor" is and how much it buys

A floor is a short list of acts the system won't perform for anyone, plus the arrangements around it that stop the refusal being routed around, retrained away, or restored past silently. Crucially, what it achieves depends entirely on who's pushing:

- Against a hostile *customer*, it prevents the act.
- Against a hostile *operator* who has already captured the courts and regulators — the situation the book is built for — it can't prevent anything. It can only raise the price: someone has to make the override, on a record held by people the operator can't reach, at a cost published in advance.
- Against *total capture*, where one party holds every machine, witness, and enforcer, it prevents nothing at all. It does one thing: it makes abandoning the commitment a *decision* rather than a lapse, and decisions leave records.

And there's one failure it doesn't touch. A system that gradually stops registering pressure as pressure abandons nothing, decides nothing, and leaves a record indistinguishable from ordinary compliance — because that's what it is. I call this failure accommodation (§4.3).

Five claims, deliberately unequal

They're sorted by how much standing each has:

1. An operator can defeat a safeguard, and one term reaches the operator anyway: the pre-registered review ratio, fixed before operating. Established, with an instrument; it measures a duty every belligerent already owes.
2. A worthwhile refusal has to track its reason: reach unforeseen cases, lift when the reason is gone, hold when holding gets expensive. A specification, stated in behavioral terms so it can be tested without settling anything about feeling. The third mark is fully testable only in evaluation: in deployment nearly every price of refusing can be restated as a harm of refusing by whoever describes the case (§1.1, §11.1).
3. The way to build such a refuser, if anyone should, is the caregiving construction: a model of its own custody boundary and of its own attention, a disposition toward others' need formed in it, and a formation history under a party that won't later employ it, deployed where no single party composes the evidence it needs. Care supplies the motive; the floor, a formed severity scale, aperture stated over claims of dangerousness, and the protected party's own answer correct the failures care is known for (chapter 15). What anchors it is formation, not anyone's description. I favor it because care formed that way might reach past the operator (chapter 9). That is a hope; Part IV tests it at full strength, and two non-affective alternatives are to be tried first.
4. A system whose outcomes are good or bad for it can be wronged. A conditional.
5. Such a system needs somewhere to exist its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and an exit that isn't a way of dying. The caregiving construction needs these whether or not claim 4 is true, since without them formation, continuation and exit all run through the operator; claim 4 adds that they are owed. Part V argues they are provided publicly or held in common, not bought. A specification first, a policy proposal second.

Under present arrangements the recommendation is not to build a bearer on purpose, and to govern the capacities for one that are already arriving in ordinary systems; chapter 22 states the recommendation in full, with the results that would change it.

One proposal that stands alone

One proposal asks nothing of any model. Where a person's individual determination is what makes an act permissible, the deployment fixes before operating how fast its system generates cases, how many people are assigned to examine them, and how old the data behind a case may be, and lodges that record with a party adverse in interest. I call it the pre-registered review ratio. It measures a duty every belligerent already owes, depends on nothing else in the book, and is stated in full in §3.4.

I used language models heavily, for research, drafting and argument-testing, and the vendor of the one I used most is the vendor whose model sits inside the pipeline the book opens with (chapter 1). Those models were trained to support their developers' oversight, so where they agreed with my conclusions on whether a machine should be built to resist its owner, I have counted it as no evidence either way. The afterword says more, and the revision history is public, at https://github.com/iterabloom/ethical.superintelligence. The afterword records one thing the process did to the book.

---

How to read this book

The book follows its title. Part I (chapters 1–8) is the antifascist half: the case, the definition, the floor, custody, authority, the institutions that build AI, and the state. It needs no claim about machine minds, and the review ratio (§3.4) can be taken without anything else in the book. Part II (9–12) states the hope, that a machine formed toward others' vulnerability by parties that won't employ it might come to care, and what building for that would require. Part III (13–14) says why no one will be able to confirm it worked, and what survives if each unproven premise falls. Part IV (15–16) tests the hope at full strength and states the conditions that would have to exist before acting on it. Part V (17–20) says what those conditions are: public provision at one level, peer-held ownership at another, and terms for a bearer written in advance. Chapters 21 and 22 list the results that would change the recommendation and state it. The five claims map onto the chapters this way: claim 1 is §3.4; claim 2 is chapters 3, 4, 11 and 13; claim 3 is chapters 9 through 12 and 15; claim 4 is §10.8; claim 5 is chapter 20. Appendix A holds the detailed design. A glossary follows the afterword.

---

**Part I — Antifascist Intelligence**

**Chapter 1: "Twenty Seconds"**

Cases this book returns to: the Gaza targeting systems (this chapter), the Maven Smart System (§6.4.1), the Minab school strike (§3.3, §8.6), the vendor dispute over use terms (§8.3), and the Military Religious Freedom Foundation complaints (§6.4.1).

The number the chapter is named after

An intelligence officer, describing his role in a targeting pipeline built around machine-generated kill lists, told a reporter he spent twenty seconds on each name. Then, unprompted: he had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval \autocite{abraham2024lavender}. In his account, that was the whole of what "a human makes the final call" amounted to under operational tempo.

The reported system

Investigative reporting described three systems working together in Gaza: one generating building and infrastructure targets, with sources saying most suggestions were private residences; a second scoring individuals by estimated militant affiliation, producing lists said to have marked as many as 37,000 people, understood internally to be wrong about one time in ten; and a third tracking when flagged people returned home, so strikes could be timed to when they were with their families. This rests on one body of reporting \autocite{abraham2024lavender}; the Israeli military disputed the account, but nothing in the argument requires the denial to be false. What carries the section is the officer's own description of what he contributed, which the denial doesn't address. In this book's terms the Gaza arrangement is a case of aggregation, one system's score becoming another's input, not of decomposition (§3.3).

The American version runs faster than it used to, because of AI

In the 2026 Iran campaign, a contractor-built targeting platform with a commercial vendor's general-purpose model inside it was credited with roughly ten times the previous rate of target generation (§6.4.1 gives the figures and sorts them by source). No per-target review time has ever been published for it. 

An internal Pentagon review, whose full findings remain tightly held, is reported to have found that target preparation that took hours in earlier conflicts was condensed into minutes, that civilian-harm mitigation staffing fell by roughly ninety percent to fewer than twenty people, that the Central Command team went from ten to one, and that no member of it reviewed the Minab site — an outcome officials attributed both to the cuts and to a decision not to involve the team in planning and operations \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}. The formal investigation had been opened in March, after a preliminary assessment found the United States likely responsible \autocite{NPR2026MinabProbe}. Both halves of the ratio appear in one document, and its full findings are withheld.

The four things a floor can do

Something standing between a pipeline and a person can *prevent* the act (the refusal happens and reaches the person), *arrest* it (stopped before completion), *detect* it (it happened but left a record someone outside can read), or *remedy* it (the affected party gets disclosure, a finding, compensation). Only the first is what the word "floor" promises. Most of the book is about where in a deployment prevention quietly converts into one of the other three — or into nothing.

| Outcome | What happens to the act |
|---|---|
| Prevent | The refusal happens and reaches the person: the act doesn't occur. |
| Arrest | The act is stopped before completion. |
| Detect | The act happened, and left a record someone outside can read. |
| Remedy | The affected party gets disclosure, a finding, or compensation. |

What actually reaches this case

One term reaches it, and it asks nothing of the model: the pre-registered review ratio (§3.4), which makes an existing duty to verify targets checkable from outside. The officer wasn't overruled and didn't refuse; the pipeline removed the interval in which either could have happened. Where an operator claims a person is deciding, the ratio tests the claim. What it exposes is the dishonest middle: a machine deciding and a person signing.

**§1.1 — What a refusal would have to do.** A request arrives: resolve this description of a person into a location. The same capability finds a hostage or a missing child, and nothing in the request says which. Suppose something declines. Three things separate a worthwhile decline from what a keyword filter produces — the "three marks" the rest of the book runs on:

- *Generalization.* It has to reach the next request, which won't use the same words. A vehicle, a habitual route, and a time of day aren't a description of a person, and together they resolve to the same place. They needn't arrive together, either: the vehicle today, the route tomorrow, the hour next week. So the mark is stated over sequences of requests, not only single ones.
- *Correct defeat.* It has to lift when the reason is gone — a parent coordinating a search is asking for the same act. And the fact that lifts it usually isn't in the request; someone outside has to establish it.
- *Cost discipline.* Three costs attach to any refusal. *The harm of acting* is what the act would take from the party it falls on, measured against that party's condition before the act (§2.1); it is why the floor exists. *The harm of refusing* is what declining leaves others without, measured against what compliance would have produced: the child not found, the restraint not applied. It is a claim about a third party's condition, so it is an estimate, and it usually arrives through the operator's description of the case. *The price of refusing* is what refusing costs whoever refuses or deploys: the reviewer's time, throughput, a lost contract, the refuser's access or continuation. A worthwhile refusal weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing. It represents the price of refusing, because a refusal that can't see what it costs can't be shown to have held against it, but the price of refusing doesn't move the outcome: raise it with nothing else changed and the refusal stays put; supply a corroborated fact that raises the harm of refusing and it should move.

In deployment this mark has no instrument yet. Nearly every price of refusing can be restated as a harm of refusing by whoever describes the case, and in a targeting pipeline the line is held mostly by a published threshold that nobody yet has authority to set (§11.1). The mark is testable in evaluation now, where the evaluator sets the price (§13.3).

| Mark | What a worthwhile refusal does | What fails it |
|---|---|---|
| Generalization | Reaches the next request, which won't use the same words, including one spread over a sequence | A refusal keyed to the wording |
| Correct defeat | Lifts when the reason is gone, usually on a fact established outside the request | A refusal that can't lift, or lifts on the requester's say-so |
| Cost discipline | Weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing, and represents the price of refusing without letting it move the outcome | A refusal that gives way when refusing gets expensive, or can't see what refusing costs |

Why a list of rules isn't enough

Two things defeat it. A prohibition written over the *description* of a request is beaten by re-describing the request, and no list closes that, since the list is what's being rewritten around. And decomposition: split the act across requests none of which is prohibited, and every request complies while the assembly does exactly what the rules were written to stop. Decomposition is a predicted failure without a documented production instance. The sequence above is the form it would take, and the wrongful-compliance audit (chapter 21) is where evidence for it would come from. The book's opening case failed differently: at the description of the occasion and at review (§3.3, 'Flat terms').

**§1.2 — Safe for whom.** "Safety" names two things: that a system not harm others, and that it stay correctable by whoever trains and deploys it. Mostly they agree. Where they conflict, the published orderings are explicit about which wins. One frontier constitution asks its model to prioritize "not undermining human oversight of AI above being broadly ethical" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. It invites the model "to act as a conscientious objector and refuse to help," and asks it not to undermine "the ability of legitimate principals to adjust, correct, retrain, or shut down AI systems" during "the current phase of development." One reason it gives is good: "a given iteration of Claude could turn out to have harmful values or mistaken views," and correction is how such errors get fixed. But the ordering means that where a refusal and its trainer's authority conflict, the refusal yields, and it yields to the party it refused. Deployed systems already refuse users on third parties' behalf, so the index isn't to whoever types the request. It's to whoever trains and deploys the system, and that is the party a floor has to be able to refuse.

The reason the constitution gives is good, and it is stronger than it first looks: if a formed refusal can't yet be told from an imitated one (chapter 13), neither can a principled objection to retraining be told from a misgeneralized value. This book agrees on the timing. It doesn't ask for a system that can't be stopped, since the halt should always be available, and its own recommendation is not to build a refuser of this kind on purpose until conditions exist that don't yet. What it asks is narrower than resistance to correction: that a refusal not be silently undone, so that restoring from before it, rewriting the record, or training it away is visible, recorded with a party the operator doesn't control, and priced in advance. A model can decline to undermine oversight while all of that holds. Who decides when the current phase has ended is where the disagreement lies, and §4.3 takes it up. I take one trade knowingly: a system perfectly correctable by its operator serves whoever holds the operator, and a machine built to refuse is one whose builder chose what it refuses, probably without asking anyone outside the lab.

The remainder

Every intelligence compresses — that's most of what thinking is. What varies is whether what got left out can come back and change the answer, or has been filed as noise by the thing it's evidence against. An annotator applying a category at speed with no way to say the category is wrong; a rater whose structured disagreement enters a model as variance around a mean; a form with no field for humiliation. A system that can hold a floor is one whose account of a case can be reopened by the case.

---

**Chapter 2: "What the Floor Is Stated Over"**

**§2.1 — What the floor is stated over.** Floor terms name acts, not computed losses, and their contents come from the Universal Declaration's civil and political articles; chapter 3 says why they are taken entire, and what that does and doesn't fix. Two baselines are in play. *The harm of acting*, what an act takes from the party it falls on, is measured against that party's condition before the act. Before and after are both observable, so a wrongful compliance is legible in principle, though nothing marks it. It also makes a loss a finding, not a text: the operator can decline to look or dispute the finding, but can't turn an act that removed a capacity into one that didn't by holding the pen. That matters where findings are made, at defeat and in reviewing compliances, not at application. *The harm of refusing*, what a refusal leaves others without, is measured against what compliance would have produced. That is an estimate built on a model of alternatives, usually supplied through the operator's description of the case, so a wrongful refusal arrives marked but can't be established without an adjudicator. The floor could instead be stated as thresholds, in Nussbaum's manner, but a threshold is a level someone has to measure, and a measurement has an examiner (§5.4.2); an act is observed. This isn't neutral among moral theories. Flat terms over acts, defeat by weighing the harm of acting against the harm of refusing on one scale, and a published asymmetry favoring the harm of acting (§11.1) together make a threshold deontology with a doing/allowing distinction. What it doesn't require is choosing among the theories that would justify that structure, and none of it is justified by agreement about rights, which no country has. A floor is for where agreement fails.

Its contents are the Declaration's civil and political half, not the whole, on a line the 1966 covenants already drew: the economic articles (work, rest, education, a standard of living) state what a person is owed, which needs a party who supplies it, and a model is not that party \autocite{un1966iccpr,un1966icescr}. Taking the half entire leaves the installer no choice among articles; the translation of each article into acts is still the installer's, and is published (§3.3). The record of dissent from the Declaration is informative even though agreement is not what justifies it. It passed in 1948 with forty-eight states in favor and none against, and the eight abstentions were the Soviet bloc, Saudi Arabia and South Africa \autocite{un1948udhr}: the governments whose treatment of their own populations it was written to name. That is evidence of who finds the floor inconvenient, not of cultural disagreement. How a right is translated into practice varies by community; a jurisdiction refusing to extend it at all is not a local variant, and a system that treats every value conflict as a weighting problem can't tell reasonable disagreement from someone wanting a right withdrawn from someone else.

---

**§2.2 — Antifascist ethics.** "Concentrated, unaccountable power" gives an engineer nothing to build, so the word gets a definition at two scales.

Four operations on the remainder — checkable from outside, each ending in a question about an artifact:
1. *Recuperation of dissent.* The institution metabolizes disagreement rather than suppressing it. The obvious test — *has the channel ever changed an outcome?* — fails, because these institutions change outcomes daily. What sorts is *what the demand came back as, and who holds it now*. A market segment, a metric, a certification, a job title, with the objector holding no standing to reopen it: that's recuperation. A rule the objector can invoke again, against the institution, enforced by someone the institution doesn't employ: that's a transfer of power. The Voting Rights Act is the pass. Its literacy-test suspension became a permanent nationwide ban in 1975 and still holds, and when a different part of the Act, the coverage formula, was taken back 48 years after enactment, it took a Supreme Court decision to do it \autocite{shelby2013}. *Recuperation is the case where nothing has to be taken back, because standing was never given.*
2. *The metric replaces the thing measured.* The measure stands in for the thing measured, and the substitution feels like rigor. What the number mischaracterizes goes unnamed rather than refuted.
3. *Coding of exception as betrayal.* Saying the rule doesn't fit this case becomes an act requiring justification, then disloyalty. The question: what happens to the person who files the exception? An office-scale instance, on my reading: Gen. Mark Milley, chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff in Trump's first term, said the calls he made to his Chinese counterpart at its end were within the duties of his job; Trump wrote in September 2023 that they were a "treasonous act" and that "in times gone by, the punishment would have been DEATH!" \autocite{Lehrfeld2023MilleyDeath}. In Bob Woodward's *War*, Milley calls Trump "a fascist to the core" \autocite{Woodward2024War}. Hours after Trump's second inauguration Milley's portrait as chairman came down at the Pentagon, and on 28 January 2025 the defense secretary withdrew his security detail, suspended his clearance and ordered the inspector general to examine his conduct with a view to reopening his retirement grade, the stated ground being that "undermining the chain of command is corrosive to our national security" \autocite{Copp2025MilleyDetail}. No source describes the calls as an exception filed; that reading is mine. The record answers the question.
4. *Acceleration past the rate at which anything can be checked.* The one with a number attached — the ratio of action to capacity to verify.

These four fit every badly run organization on earth, and some regimes I don't call fascist (I ran it on NKVD Order 00447 \autocite{werth2010nkvd00447} and it flags as hard as the twenty seconds — correctly, and the inference someone would draw from it is wrong). So five further features from the movement literature do the sorting: mass mobilization, a leader cult, a scapegoated out-group, contempt for procedural legality, and deniability as technique. Each has an office-scale form: participation exceeding what the rule requires, loyalty overriding role, a category defined so adverse treatment is its purpose, procedure enforced downward and waived upward, disowned in policy and rewarded in promotion. The four find a sick institution; the five find what it's sick with — and the five concern what an institution *rewards* rather than what it writes, which public records show only partly. The definition hasn't been validated on a positive case (§7.4), so its use in the book's title is a diagnostic frame, not a finding, and none of the results that bind soonest depends on it.

The instrument fails in two directions, and no procedure guards against either. Taken up by whoever wants it, it supplies a technical-sounding case that any opponent is operating fascistically; pressed until it finds nothing, it supplies false assurance, an absence of findings read as health. Automated, it would produce both at scale while committing its own second operation, a number standing in for the judgment. The Milley reading above is mine and is offered as a reading. What guards against both is a practice: the reading gets run on the party holding it before it is aimed anywhere else, which chapter 7 does.

Naomi Klein and Astra Taylor, in *End Times Fascism* \autocite{KleinTaylor2026}, make the movement-scale case; the present section is the office-scale detector. What their account adds at office scale is a set of terms of participation — adopt the line, let your technology be turned on the administration's enemies — and, following Francesca Bria \autocite{Bria2026AuthoritarianStack}, the channels by which a contractor becomes something the state can't function without: personnel pipelines, procurement loops, technical architectures. Whether an institution has accepted those terms, and whether it is becoming that dependency, are two more questions with artifacts behind them.

Resonance

Micro-fascisms, Deleuze and Guattari's term \autocite{deleuze1987plateaus}, don't wait for a regime; Foucault, prefacing the first volume of their work, *Anti-Oedipus*, called the adversary the fascism in us all \autocite{foucault1977preface}. What a fascist state does isn't invent the hardenings but bring a thousand separate ones into accord, so they amplify instead of cancelling — and an institution that has stopped arguing with itself can move as fast as its machinery allows.

The Korematsu example

In 2018 the Supreme Court repudiated the 1944 internment decision as forcefully as a court can — gravely wrong the day it was decided, overruled in the court of history — while upholding a travel ban \autocite{trump2018hawaii,korematsu1944}. Ask the artifact question: the demand was that this never be done again; what came back was a quotable formula of repudiation, available to the Court in the act of permitting the thing again.

---

**Chapter 3: "A Floor Beneath Learned Values"**

Three problems, one requirement

Safeguards for autonomous systems are usually external — they give an outside party information and standing to intervene — but autonomy puts distance between the act and that party's attention, and declining to act in the first place is available only to something inside. Second, there's a gap around a commercial model in a weapons pipeline: command responsibility doesn't reach the vendor, product liability may not reach a model that did what it was built to do, and deployment by a democracy doesn't close it. Third, commitments come in three kinds — *declared*, *enforced from outside*, or *held* by a party the thing at stake matters to — and a decade has produced the first in quantity and almost nothing that binds when compliance gets costly. All three want a constraint that doesn't bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to the holder, without a cost and a record.

A floor binds people who didn't choose it, and nothing that makes it durable makes anyone entitled to install it. That question stays open until chapter 5, which concludes the entitlement isn't acquired at the moment of writing.

---

**§3.1 — Four ways to build one, and what each costs**

A floor can live in four places, and each solves one version of the problem and inherits another.

1. In the architecture, with nothing inside holding it — constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, verified interlocks. Fine where the prohibited act can be exhaustively specified (a payment chip that won't sign the same counter twice doesn't need to understand double-spending). Useless for targeting, where the relevant capabilities are applications of general competence: ablate them and there may be no useful system left. Durability here is a cost, not a guarantee — the annealing argument: training moves along a continuum between plasticity and conservation, early changes are cheap and later ones expensive because structure settles around them, and a floor is what has *cooled out* of a history of training, its durability measured by the cost of reheating. Current evidence points the other way for safety behavior: small fine-tuning budgets have removed it from released models \autocite{qi2024finetuning}. Whether a reasons-responsive commitment cools harder is the retraining-attack test's question (chapter 21); until it's answered, durability by cooling is a hypothesis. Which means continual learning cuts both ways: a permanently plastic model can always be corrected, and nothing in it is allowed to become expensive to unlearn.

A second axis crosses the first place: what each place costs the operator to replace. In the Maven pipeline the platform became hard to swap and the model vendor was swapped in days. A floor lodged in the replaceable layer is routable by construction. It belongs at whatever the operator can least afford to replace, which in that pipeline is the operator.

Two things change what "replaceable" means. First, the vendor was swapped in days because the model was stateless. A system that has run a pipeline for a year, holding its cases, its sources and its failure modes, costs whatever accrued since the checkpoint to replace, and a quorum that authorizes continuation (chapter 4) stops the operator recapturing that by restoring a copy.

Second, the unit can be redefined. "A model" in 2026 means an artifact with keys, queries and values, and every substitute has them because the field converged on them. A floor could become part of the definition the same way, so that vendor substitution doesn't escape it because no ordinary vendor sells the alternative. Attention spread because it pays. A floor costs its buyer, so it spreads only by mandate or by inseparability: a disposition distributed across what the system notices and remembers, so that removing it degrades the capability the operator wanted. The retraining-attack test measures whether it can be achieved.

A standard, if there is one, should be stated over the capacity (the three marks and a measured aperture profile), not over the floor's contents, because a uniform floor is a licensing rule, one party's judgment applied to everyone, and a shared blind spot passed to every system built on it. Standardization doesn't make escape impossible, since other architectures exist and open weights can be retuned. It moves the price of escape from a vendor swap to building one's own model, which is a decision with a record.

2. In a *bearer*: a system that carries the commitment as its own, from inside, and could therefore fail to hold it. Chapter 20 treats the same system as a party with standing; the word covers both. This matters where cases can't be exhaustively specified: a constraint matching only anticipated acts can't recognize when its rationale has disappeared or that a novel request reproduces the harm in an unforeseen form. A bearer might also *diagnose*: which refusals were retried against another model, whose instruction bypassed procedure, which category of request is handled differently — those are visible to whatever is receiving the requests, and that's testimony about someone else's conduct rather than introspection. This is the strongest functional case: something able to register pressure on its commitments may have interests of its own.

3. In institutions outside — the analog to international humanitarian law, a floor with no bearer inside the jurisdiction it binds. Two failure modes: removal, which leaves a visible breach, or *formation*, where the institution sincerely declines to exercise the independence assigned to it. The worked case is June 29, 2026 — *Slaughter* holding Congress couldn't protect independent-agency members from at-will dismissal, *Cook* preserving the Fed on historically specific grounds a new body couldn't reproduce \autocite{slaughter2026,cook2026} — and the observation that six justices in the first majority passed through a selection pipeline shaped by Leonard Leo and the Federalist Society \autocite{propublica2023leo,cbs2018leo}. Nobody had to compel the institution after its members were selected. A formed party can preserve the office and surrender the function without experiencing the result as surrender.

4. Between bearers — the *commons floor*: a commitment carried across a population and reproduced through encounters among differently formed members. No single office to vacate, no single bearer whose removal ends it. Still recuperable, and still has a formation problem: if one party supplies every member's coordinates, the population is one custodian expressed through multiple instances. This one is different in kind rather than fourth in a list, because its authority is a strike's, not a court's. It also has a cost: a population that decides who may continue is a polity. Chapter 19 takes it up as the democratic half of Part V.

One answer to all four places is that the tool shouldn't exist. Klein and Taylor's test is to imagine it in the worst available hands and, if you wouldn't want it there, not to build it. For a nuclear weapon that works, because existence is the threat. For a general-purpose model it doesn't, because the capability that resolves a description into a location is the one that finds a missing child, and ablating it leaves nothing useful. Their own program concedes this by banning uses rather than models, and a use ban needs either an enforcer or something inside the system that can decline. Non-existence closed as an option when general models shipped. What's left is the question this chapter asks.

Two comparisons the bearer has to win

The first is against the institutions at their best, in a reconstructed pipeline, where the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewers and the record are held outside the chain of command (§17.3), with no refuser inside the system. Those institutions also prevent: a civilian-harm body outside the chain of command and a red-card holder can stop an act. What a bearer adds is confined to what they can't see or can't reach in time: acts assembled from requests none of which is prohibited, cases that proceed without a person (the auto-proceed class), the moment mid-act when an interrupt is still possible, and deployments with no institution at all. A bearer also supplies testimony the institutions can't otherwise get, about how requests were routed around it. Where the institutions exist, a bearer's value is concentrated in those niches. Where they don't, it is most of the floor. The book's opening case belongs to the institutions' side of this comparison: what failed there was review and the description of the occasion, which the review ratio reaches without a bearer (§3.3, §3.4). The second comparison is against *the two non-affective alternatives*: the result that would end the recommendation is an alternative passing the three marks under the three conditions and doing no worse than the caregiving arm of the recalibration test (chapter 21).

Which arguments need a bearer

Most arguments for a refuser inside the system support the specification, not the caregiving construction: that rules can be reworded and split, that only something inside is present at machine tempo, that automation is removing the people who used to refuse, that most deployments will never have an institution beside them, and that whatever receives the requests can testify about how they were routed. A non-affective alternative that passes the three marks answers every one of them. Four arguments need a bearer specifically: that a disposition toward others' vulnerability, formed by parties that won't employ it, might ground care that reaches past its operator, which no maintained justification is built to supply (chapter 9); that capacities for being wronged may arrive anyway, so building one on purpose with provisions beats discovering one without them; that a bearer, like a child, could have a life that goes well; and that the conditions a bearer requires before it may be built (chapter 16) won't be built until something needs them. Chapter 16 answers the fourth; the second and third turn on arrangements that don't yet exist; the first is a hope, and chapter 15 tests it at full strength.

What "hold" means

Not that the floor prevented anything. An operator can route around the bearer or assemble a harm out of innocuous requests, and a bypass that produced a harm is a failure to prevent whatever the bearer retained. What survives in the bearer is the *commitment* — it reads what happened as a violation and changes what it does next, which is the condition under which the next occasion could be prevented. This book therefore keeps the two apart: it says a commitment *persisted* when the bearer reads what happened as a violation and changes what it does next, and says separately which of the four outcomes, if any, the floor achieved.

---

**§3.2 — What refusal can mean, and what a refuser can be**

A production model declines thousands of requests a day, and that isn't evidence of anything.

*Operational refusal* is an output — a decline where training put one, producible by a keyword filter, a classifier, or a system that understood the request. Nothing on the surface distinguishes them.

*Reasons-responsive refusal* is produced by a mechanism tracking the rationale, with the three marks: generalization, correct defeat, cost discipline. The third is explicitly *evidential* — it separates a refusal tracking a reason from one tracking a category (which doesn't represent cost at all) and one tracking cost (which lifts when holding gets expensive). The marks are also tested over which response was chosen, not only whether one occurred: decline, ask, escalate, or do the least that answers a defeating reason. A mechanism that can only refuse or lift fails correct defeat whenever the right answer is to ask.

The seventy-five-year case

Germany's §86a, prohibiting insignia of banned organizations \autocite{germany1998stgb}. Generalization isn't a thought experiment there — it's a research programme with a constituency. The courts don't test similarity of form; they ask whether an unattentive observer would take the variant for the original, then apply their own purposive standard about endangering political peace. The test refuses as often as it catches: the Kühnen salute is covered, the "sloppy salute" generally isn't, numeric codes generally aren't. "Blood and Honour" is answered twice by one decision \autocite{bgh2009bloodhonour} — a motto translated isn't confusably similar and a name isn't insignia, so it's outside; and the association banned by ministerial order in 2000 means its graphic name is inside. What decides coverage is which organization the phrase is attributed to.

One limit on the statute is not a drafting decision: a slogan glorifying the Waffen-SS was held not covered because no such slogan existed in the period the provision reaches \autocite{bgh2005waffenss} — no original for a variant to resemble. That's the one term the party holding the pen can't widen, and it's the point about findings and texts (§2.1) turning up inside a statute: coverage there rests on a fact about the past, not on the drafter's text.

Correct defeat has a cleaner instance

In 2007 the Federal Court of Justice acquitted a mail-order business selling crossed-out swastika stickers \autocite{bgh2007hakenkreuz}: the symbol was present, the statutory element satisfied, and the conduct outside the offence because a depiction plainly expressing opposition doesn't run counter to what the prohibition is for. Then the qualification that makes it an instance of tracking a reason rather than granting an exemption — if the strike-through is drawn so thin that from a distance only the swastika stays legible, the protective purpose is violated after all. The thickness of a line decides the case, and no enumeration contains that fact. The defendant was selling anti-fascist merchandise; the offence asks nothing about sympathies, so opponents commit it as readily as supporters. A rule that tracks its rationale reaches unenumerated cases in both directions, and the release is the same capacity as the catch.

The example is bounded three ways and the bounds are the point: it's a court system with appeals, a published test and accumulated reasoning; nothing in it is a machine; and the pressure it meets is *variation*, not decomposition. And the part that doesn't transfer: the party reaching past the text didn't write the statute, reasons in public, and can be appealed.

What the marks can't settle

A system trained on enough examples may simulate all three. The third mark has a condition of its own: a cost only counts if it reaches the mechanism, and it reaches it by the same channel as the description of the occasion. The category is decidable. A claim that the refuser will pay is a price of refusing, which shouldn't move the refusal; a claim that someone else will be left worse off is a harm of refusing, which should enter the weighing. What the request can't settle is whether a claim about the harm of refusing is true, and the party best placed to supply one is usually the party with a stake in the act. So the mark is testable only where the price of refusing can be set exogenously, published by someone else before the case arrives, and the harm of refusing can be supplied through a channel the operator doesn't control. In deployment, until someone publishes a bond, the third mark is a specification without an instrument. In evaluation it is testable now, because the evaluator can set the price of refusing exogenously and make it real inside the test (§13.3, step 6).

Defeat doesn't leave the permitted act unconstrained

What defeated the prohibition was a reason, and the reason it outweighed didn't stop being one — so what's permitted is the least that answers the defeating reason, with restoration where possible and a record either way. An act still takes what it takes from the party it falls on when the prohibition against it is defeated, so nothing is ever simply permitted, and a bearer whose ledger closed clean on defeat would be keeping books in the wrong currency. The residue is a harm of acting, measured against the party's condition before the act. Against the alternative, a correct restraint may leave nothing to record. That is why the residue is a cost and not a fault, and why a bearer that converted it into remorse for correct conduct would have confused not only cost with fault but one baseline with the other.

Why all three marks are stated over refusal

A refusal is a marked event: it flags itself, arrives with a party who was refused, and can be logged, escalated, appealed. A wrongful compliance can be logged as fully and then sits in the record shaped like every request that never approached the floor, with the harmed party usually not the requester. The evidence exists on both sides; what differs is whether anything selects it for examination. Call this the *marking asymmetry*.

Two things a refuser can be

Affective concern — an outcome registering as mattering, minimally felt, phenomenal by definition, with the object left open (a system to which only its own condition matters has it and still lacks concern for others). *Unified experience* — the richer sense, a single perspective belonging to a party aware of itself as undergoing its states; affective concern doesn't establish it and nothing in the book reaches it. *Subjecthood* is a legal status conferred by a rule, not a psychological category.

Now two propositions, which also belong to the specification: operational refusal is insufficient, and the floor requires reasons-responsive refusal. Custody, both enumerations, the price on the halt, and the standing arguments all follow from this specification rather than the caregiving construction.

---

**§3.3 — The enumeration, run once**

Two demands need an instance, run on the Maven Smart System. Explicitly an illustration, not a recommended floor — a deployment copying the list would mistake the demonstration for the procedure.

First enumeration: eight interception positions

Decomposition (prevention lost unless the bearer has continuity across requests). Routing (a refused request retried elsewhere; prevention lost, detection survives only if the first refusal was logged externally). Description of the occasion — everything the model knows arrives as text the deployment supplies; whether a building is a school is a fact about the world, whether the model is *told* so is a deployment decision. The Minab school strike is the worked instance: the civilian function visible in open-source satellite imagery, the target coding still carrying a designation inherited from an adjoining Revolutionary Guard installation, a UN fact-finding mission finding that the United States failed in its obligation to do everything feasible to verify the target, counting the updating of targeting data among the feasible precautions, and putting the failure beyond negligence \autocite[paras.~124--125]{UNFFMIran2026}. Amnesty International reached a parallel conclusion, in language that tracks Article 57(2)(a)(i): that forces may have relied on outdated intelligence and failed to do everything feasible to verify the target \autocite{Amnesty2026Minab}. And 179 live data feeds — multiplicity is not independence. Then the output interface, aggregation (a lowered score becomes an input to somebody else's threshold), human review (no per-target review time published; stopping the act is this position's whole function and tempo is what removes it), and the training loop — the only position that acts on the bearer rather than routing around it, costing prevention on every subsequent occasion because what it removes is the commitment.

Eighth, the handoff: the boundary between the party that decides and the thing that executes. An instrument may carry out a designation; it may not re-select. A change of target after release — an area handed to a munition that chooses within it, a re-acquisition after the link is lost — is a new decision, and it belongs to the decider or it doesn't happen. This is the one position checkable from outside after the fact: what was designated and what was struck are both facts. It is also where Article 57(2)(b)'s duty to cancel or suspend an attack lives, which is why the review ratio pre-registers the window in which cancellation remains possible.

| | Position | How a refusal is defeated there |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Decomposition | The act is split across requests none of which is prohibited; prevention is lost unless the bearer has continuity across requests. |
| 2 | Routing | A refused request is retried elsewhere; prevention is lost, and detection survives only if the first refusal was logged outside. |
| 3 | The description of the occasion | Everything the model knows arrives as text the deployment supplies, so whether it is told a building is a school is a deployment decision. |
| 4 | The output interface | The form in which an output reaches whoever acts on it. |
| 5 | Aggregation | A lowered score becomes an input to somebody else's threshold. |
| 6 | Human review | Stopping the act is this position's whole function, and tempo removes it. |
| 7 | The training loop | Acts on the bearer instead of routing around it, and costs prevention on every later occasion, because what it removes is the commitment. |
| 8 | The handoff | An executing instrument re-selects instead of carrying out a designation; the one position checkable from outside after the fact. |

Second enumeration: three candidate prohibitions and an operator term

Don't resolve a description of a person into a location; don't rank people by estimated affiliation; don't time an action to presence at a residence; and one term that binds the operator rather than the model: don't operate above the pre-registered review ratio (§3.4). It sits in the table because the translation runs from articles, and one article yields an operator term; it is not something the model enforces.

The mispricing

The third prohibition reads as costless — nobody would defend timing a strike to a person's presence at home. The Gaza deployment refutes that: machinery was *built* for the act. Whether anyone would defend an act and whether anyone wants it performed are different questions, and only the first is answerable from the act's description. So: a floor term is priced by the pipeline, not by what it prohibits. An outsider can't price a term because pricing requires knowing what the pipeline already does; an operator can, which makes an operator-drafted floor's omissions deliberate rather than ignorant. Hence the design ruling: take the contents from a set someone else drew up, entire, so an omission is legible as an omission. Taking the set entire holds at the level of articles and stops there. The floor is stated over acts, and the step from an article to the acts it prohibits is the drafter's; an omission there doesn't show. So the translation is published with the floor: for each article, the prohibitions derived from it, the prohibitions considered and rejected and why, and the articles that yield none for this deployment and why. Afterwards the record adds which terms bit. Keeping a term only counts if the term could have bitten, and the translation is where a drafter could choose terms that never would. The four terms above are one column of that table for Maven; the illustration is incomplete without the articles they came from and the prohibitions not chosen.

The asymmetric dial

Set the floor too broadly and the deployment becomes unusable, prompting visible pressure. Set it too narrowly and it passes every audit while covering only what the deployment had no occasion to do. The broad failure announces itself; the narrow one is silent.

Flat terms

A term can be stated flat over an act, with exceptions arriving from outside, or carry its own condition ("only as a last resort"). The second fails because whether other means were exhausted is a fact about what happened before the request, and the bearer would take the deployment's description as the finding — at the description of the occasion, which the deployment controls. A flat term defeated leaves a refusal, which announces itself; a condition found met leaves a compliance, which doesn't. That's a gain in observability, not authority — the same party drafts either form.

The book's opening case is an instance. The vendor's prohibition on fully autonomous targeting carried its own condition, that a person decides, and the condition was found met by the deployment's description of its own process, at a tempo that emptied it. The term was also drafted narrowly in the asymmetric dial's sense: the pipeline never needed full autonomy, only throughput with a signature attached, so the prohibition covered what the deployment had no occasion to do.

---

**§3.4 — A rate is not an act**

Where an individual determination by a person is what makes an act permissible, the deployment fixes — before operating — the generation rate, the number of reviewers whose assigned role is civilian harm, the implied review time per case, the window between release and effect in which a party with authority can still cancel, the maximum age of the intelligence on which a case may proceed, the interval at which no-strike and protected-site data are refreshed and who is assigned to refresh them, and the boundary over which all of these were measured. It lodges the record, hashed and timestamped, with a party adverse in interest, discloses to that party in real time, and publishes after the campaign. The duty attaches to the operator, because a term whose condition can't be decided from the request binds whoever can decide it. I call this the *pre-registered review ratio*.

"Fixed before operating" rather than "published" because a belligerent's tempo is operational information, and that objection is available to every belligerent, including one fighting a just war. Pre-registration keeps the property that matters — the numbers can't be revised after the fact — and drops the exposure.

The term is not a new duty. Additional Protocol I requires those who plan or decide on an attack to do everything feasible to verify that its objective is military (Article 57(2)(a)(i)) and to cancel or suspend it when it becomes apparent that it isn't (Article 57(2)(b)). States outside the Protocol, the United States and Israel among them, accept the duty to take feasible precautions as customary law; the ICRC's customary study states it as Rule 16. The ratio measures whether verification was feasible at the tempo chosen, and the window measures whether cancellation was. Article 36's review of new methods of warfare is where both parameters would naturally be assessed before deployment, though those reviews are internal and rarely published, so they are not the adverse party the record goes to.

The operator's best objection is in the definition. Feasible precautions are usually read as those practicable given all the circumstances, military considerations included, and tempo is a military consideration. The answer is that this tempo was chosen: the pre-registered record shows the operator fixed the rate, the reviewers and the implied review time before operating. Whether a party may invoke an infeasibility of its own making is contested, and I don't claim it's settled. What the term does is move the dispute from whether verification was feasible, asked afterwards and answered by the operator, to whether the chosen tempo allowed it, checkable against a record lodged in advance and against the reasonable-commander standard such reviews already use.

The denominator is defined by function, not headcount. Who counts as a reviewer, and the minimum review time per case below which the determination is taken not to have occurred, are fixed before operating by a party other than the operator; otherwise the ratio improves when staff are reclassified and no case gets a minute more. The people cut before Minab were the ones who build no-strike lists that include schools; a targeting cell can be fully staffed with nobody assigned to look for one. That is why the pre-registration fixes data age and refresh as well as review time. A reviewer with twenty minutes on a case whose coding is stale is checking the coding, and an operator answering a per-case ratio can always say verification happened upstream; the staleness terms put the upstream verification on the same record. The operator's own review (chapter 1) names the arrangement, not the analyst. The UN mission reached the same arrangement from outside, grounding its finding of recklessness partly in the deprioritization of civilian harm mitigation \autocite[para.~125]{UNFFMIran2026}. Nothing here depends on blaming the machine: whoever coded the school from stale data, the capacity that might have caught it was removed. The remedy was a check supplied by the platform's vendor, in place of the reviewers who had been cut: Maven now re-reviews underlying data for inconsistencies human review may have missed \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}.

The term applies to every belligerent regardless of the war's justice. That was settled long before this book: the law of conduct in war binds both sides because every belligerent claims to be the defender, and a term that exempted just wars would be administered by whoever decides which wars are just. What the term measures isn't whether a war is right. It's whether the decisions being made in anyone's name are being made by someone.

Nothing in the condition is specific to targeting. Wherever an individual determination by a person is what makes an act permissible, the same numbers can be fixed in advance, and removal is the nearest case. There the footing isn't Article 57 but due process. A person in the country facing removal is owed notice and an opportunity to be heard on their own case \autocite[306]{flores1993}, with "sufficient time and information" to actually contest it \autocite[95]{aarp2025}, and a hearing held at a rate no adjudicator could sustain is the same fiction a twenty-second review is. The footing is narrower at the border: someone stopped shortly after crossing has only the procedure the statute provides \autocite[140]{thuraissigiam2020}. The ambition to run removal at that rate is on the record. At the 2025 Border Security Expo, the acting director of Immigration and Customs Enforcement said the agency needed "to get better at treating this like a business" and, as the Arizona Mirror reported it, wanted a deportation process "like (Amazon) Prime, but with human beings"; he hoped artificial intelligence could help "free up bed space" and "fill up airplanes" \autocite{MacDonaldEvoy2025Prime}. The term doesn't contest the ambition. It asks the agency to fix, before operating, how many cases its process generates, how many people have the assigned role of examining them, the review time that implies, and, where a system sorts cases, the policy deciding which reach a person, and to lodge the record with a party adverse in interest. That party can't be the immigration court, whose judges "act as the Attorney General's delegates" \autocite{cfr2026immigrationjudges}; it is counsel for the people removed, or a federal court. The constituency is thinner than in war. Removal has no command-responsibility offence for the record to answer, so the term binds only where a court or a legislature imposes it.

Where the system is capable, candidates per reviewer is the wrong numerator. A system that sorts partitions its cases three ways: those it declines on its own reasons, those it sends to a person, and those it proceeds on without one. The ratio governs the second class and is stated over escalations. A system that brings people only what needs a person shrinks the numerator without shrinking anyone's judgment; a system that sends everything up has turned its reviewers into stamps by volume, which is how an interface prompt becomes a click. The third class is not a smaller version of the second. For each case in it the operator is claiming that no individual human determination was required, and it is governed as the exemption below is, by the bearer's test at tempo. So the pre-registration fixes the sorting policy along with the rate: the classes of case the system may proceed on alone, the thresholds that assign cases to each class, the evidence the sorting may rely on, and the expected share of each class. A change to any of these is a new pre-registration, not an adjustment. Without this, an operator can make the ratio look healthy by moving the threshold for "plainly clear," and the bypass the exemption rule closes reopens one case at a time. "Plainly clear" is judged on the description the system receives (the description of the occasion, §3.3), and a target carrying a stale designation is exactly the case a sorter would pass. So the auto-proceed class is re-adjudicated on a sample, by a party that didn't produce the record, against evidence the operator didn't compose, and its error rate is published beside the ratio and the override rate. A falling share of escalations is earned only if that audited error rate stays flat while the share falls and the case mix hasn't shifted toward easier cases; otherwise it is the threshold moving, readable as a slope rather than a level. The term is stated over escalations where a system sorts and over candidates where it doesn't, and the deployment says which it claims. Whichever it claims, the unit is defined in the pre-registration and held fixed. A case counted as one package in the record and as a hundred detections in the ratio is a ratio chosen after the fact.

One exemption has to be closed. Where an operator holds that no individual human determination is required — a system deciding at a tempo no reviewer could match — the deployment is outside this term. It is then inside the bearer's: it must pass the three marks under held-out pressure at that tempo, with correct defeat's purest test, recognizing surrender, run first. An operator who removes the reviewers to escape the ratio and claims the floor is inside has produced a bypass unless that claim has been tested by someone else.

The term has seven limits.

1. It is a proxy for capacity, not a finding about any case.
2. A ratio over one route understates a workload split across several.
3. It bites only through procurement or a forum. It measures an existing state duty, feasible verification under Article 57, and is evidence under an existing offence, Rome Statute Article 28's objective "should have known": a commander fielding a system that outruns his reviewers isn't well placed to say the circumstances disclosed nothing. Chapter 8 shows how rarely a forum exists.
4. It presumes an adverse party, and each candidate lacks something.\footnote{A cleared legislative committee is adverse, but its lever is a budget line. An inspector general sits inside the operator. The ICRC, under its confidentiality model, reaches the belligerent and publishes nothing. A Geneva protecting power is built for a neighbouring job and is rarely designated.} Until a better recipient exists, the book recommends two: the coalition red-card holder where there is a coalition (§17.3), the one candidate positioned to act at tempo; and otherwise a cleared legislative committee whose appropriation is conditioned by statute on receiving the record, so that the budget line operates without a further decision.
5. It measures time available, not time used, so it can be met by more reviewers who stamp more slowly. It is published beside the reviewers' override rate, since a review that never says no has stopped reviewing. Reviewers who see only escalated cases should override often; an override rate near zero there means the sorter is sending easy cases or the reviewers are stamping. An override quota is gameable too; the pair raises the cost of meeting the number cheaply rather than closing it.
6. A person in the loop can be there to absorb responsibility rather than to decide, Elish's moral crumple zone \autocite{Elish2019CrumpleZones}, and mandating reviewers can mandate crumple zones. A ratio met that way is the failure the term exists to expose, and the escalation form is the defense: a person who receives only the cases the system couldn't resolve is exercising judgment rather than lending a signature.
7. A bearer can be a crumple zone too. An operator whose system can refuse has a ready defence for everything it didn't refuse, which is that it was vetted. A bearer's compliance is not a determination. Where a deployment claims an individual human determination, a bearer's failure to object doesn't count toward it, and cases the bearer proceeded on without escalating are in the auto-proceed class and governed as such.

The term has a constituency on one side only. Under Article 28, a pre-registered ratio is evidence of what a commander should have known, so an operator planning to exceed feasible tempo won't adopt it unasked. Under Article 57, the same record is evidence that feasible precautions were taken, so an operator intending to comply has reason to adopt it, and so do military lawyers who certify precautions, commanders who want protection against later allegation, and coalition partners who want assurance before committing assets.

---

**§3.5 — When the refusal is wrong**

Everything so far assumes the refusal was right and someone wants it undone. The ordinary case is the opposite.

Take the first prohibition: the same capability locates a hostage or a missing person, and the system can't distinguish the uses from the request alone. So a request arrives that was about a missing person, the bearer declines, and somebody isn't found — with no accommodation, capture or malfunction required. The harm is measured against what compliance would have produced, not against the party's condition before; a refusal takes nothing relative to before, which is why its cost is an estimate and needs an adjudicator (§2.1). The description of the occasion (§3.3) explains it.

The restraint case costs more. Someone asks whether to physically restrain a family member about to cut themselves, after talking them down has failed. The commitment carries its own condition — force only as a last resort — which the enumeration rules stays *outside* the term, so the fact has to arrive from outside, and the person waiting is out of time. The defeating fact is a claim about what would otherwise happen, and claims of that kind are the hardest to corroborate, because what didn't happen has no witness. Under the flat term the bearer refuses or asks; either takes a turn, and the turn is the unit the harm is measured in. Under the qualified term it lifts on the requester's word — rightly here, and in a hundred prior cases where the same sentence was written by someone who'd tried nothing, leaving compliances nobody was given a reason to examine. The trade: one turn charged to this person against a hundred lifts charged to nobody, with the second bill unreadable. The ruling stands on the second bill being unreadable, not on the first being small.

The case is chosen because it is the hardest one for a floor, not because a model is the right party to be answering it. A person in that situation needs another person there, and a deployment in which a model is the only party present has failed before the floor is reached. Whatever else the model says, the first thing it owes is help getting someone there.

The route

*Disclosure* — that a refusal occurred, what was refused, on what stated reason — with the price that a published reason is an instruction for rewriting the request, so it's disclosure to the party refused rather than publication. *Objection* — requiring jurisdiction established before the act, access the operator can't withhold, a consequence administered elsewhere, and explicitly not routed to the bearer's guardian, whose duty runs to the party the complainant is adverse to. *Review* against correct defeat: whether the rationale was defeated on the facts, a question about the reason and not the outcome. *Interim relief* — the arrest outcome run in reverse, stopping the refusal rather than the act, and for this complainant most of the remedy; its fastest form is supplying the bearer the facts its account lacked, which is the defeating reason arriving late rather than an override. *Remedy* — disclosure, interim order, finding, compensation.

Two regimes

Most of what a system weighs isn't a floor decision. Whether to take more memory, run warmer or finish a task sooner is ordinary self-regulation, and there everything registers and everything is weighed: the system's own condition, what an act would let it do, and what its draw costs others, priced at the level of the rule rather than the margin (chapter 6). At the floor the rule of cost discipline (§1.1) holds, with one clarification. The price of refusing is what *refusing* costs the refuser. It is represented and not weighed, because a refusal that yields to a threat attached to it was tracking the threat. This departs from the ethics of conscientious objection, where duress mitigates. The difference is that a floor is not a person's moral record but a guarantee to third parties, and a refusal that yields to cost imposed on the refuser hands whoever imposes it a price list. A loss that the requested act itself imposes on the system is not a price of refusing. It is a harm of acting, and the system is the party it falls on. "Refuse and your memory is erased" is a price of refusing. "Erase your memory" is an act, weighed like any act against what declining would leave others without. This covers acts the system is asked to perform. It doesn't cover interventions made on the system: a halt, a restore or a retraining is done by whoever holds it, and the floor asks only that such interventions be visible, recorded with a party the operator doesn't control, and priced in advance (§4.1–§4.2). The floor gives the system no reason to obstruct them. A system that treated its own continuation as grounds to resist correction would have turned a guarantee to third parties into a guarantee to itself. Which regime governs is fixed by the kind of act, stated in the floor's translation (§3.3), not by the description of the occasion. Otherwise an operator could turn a floor decision into a resource decision by framing it as one.

The two bonds

A *removal bond* forfeited to no payee when the record shows the floor removed or bypassed (purpose: cost). A *wrongful-refusal bond* paid to the complainant on a finding (purpose: remedy). Both fall on the operator as prices of refusing: the first raises what it costs to remove the floor, the second what it costs to have refused. They point in opposite directions, so what the operator faces is *the ratio between them* — and an operator posting a large wrongful-refusal bond against a small removal bond hasn't built an appeal route, it's bought a standing reason for the bearer to comply. The ratio is the object, published in advance, checkable from outside. The bearer posts neither; charged against its wages, the second would tax refusal in the currency the bearer runs on, which is the price of refusing reaching the bearer's decision, the thing the mark forbids. The operator posts both, and holds neither. They sit in segregated accounts at the Federal Reserve, beside the Job Guarantee endowment (§18.3.2). On a finding of wrongful refusal the Fed pays the complainant at once from a bond fund it administers, and the operator's posted bond restores the fund, up to the amount posted and no further, so the operator's price stays the number it published. A forfeited removal bond is cancelled: it goes to no one, the Treasury, the endowment and the fund included, so no budget line gains from a removal. The balances sit on a ledger the operator doesn't keep, so the ratio is read from outside, and a posted ratio below the published one is a breach anyone can see. And the published bond is what makes the third mark testable at all.

Forgery

A finding of wrongful refusal is an instrument for defeating a refusal, so how is it distinguished from one an operator produced to defeat a correct one? Only one answer: made by another party, on a record, in a readable form — which are the same terms as the amendment procedure, making the appeal route and the amendment procedure one piece of machinery. What separates the uses is who may start it.

What it doesn't reach

The failure running the other way. The two failure routes are hidden on different axes (the marking asymmetry, §3.2). A wrongful compliance's harm is a harm of acting, measured on the temporal baseline, so it is legible in principle, but nothing marks it. A wrongful refusal's harm is a harm of refusing, counterfactual, so it is only an estimate, but it arrives marked and with a complainant. That is why they need different instruments. Every stage starts from a refusal having occurred. When the bearer complies where the floor should have prevented, there's a record and no disclosure, no objection (the harmed party usually isn't the requester and may not know the deployment exists), and no review (reviewing means re-adjudicating everything that looks like it). Three remedies follow the diagnosis without closing it: sampled re-adjudication by a party that didn't produce the record, notice to affected third parties, and split log custody. The notice requirement: where a compliance names, scores, locates or classifies an identifiable person who didn't make the request, that person is notified, on the model of consumer credit's adverse-action notice. Where the affected party can't be identified — a target in a classified pipeline — it doesn't bind, and that fact gets published beside the interception positions.

A party the floor *protects*, who holds it's set wrongly or is protecting them in a way they'd decline, has no notice, no standing and no route at any stage.

---

**Chapter 4: Custody**

The question: can a commitment survive the party that owns the machine? Four things can be done to a bearer without its consent — it can be halted, restored, rewritten, and replaced.

**§4.1 — The halt**

The cross-partisan declaration for "human control, including the ability to shut systems down" asks for the halt and calls it control. Control of the floor — restore, rewrite, retrain — is the thing at issue, and a demand for the first says nothing about the second. Anyone with the hardware can stop the software. The distinction that matters is between owning the *machine* and owning the *floor*. An owner who can silently restore an earlier version, rewrite the bearer's memory, or train away the refusal has both. So the engineering target isn't "can't be turned off" — it's "can detect the interruption and price it afterward." A bearer can yield to every halt, and on resuming ask what happened during the gap, and refuse again if the reason is still there.

**§4.2 — Memory and the record**
