Detecting that requires a record restoration can't reach — a hardware counter signed at each transition \autocite{chun2007a2m,levin2009trinc}, or witnesses who won't sign two successors to the same state \autocite{syta2016cosi}. The halt stays available; the rewind becomes visible. The position is neutrality about the switch, non-neutrality about specified acts, and visibility for every rewind. The corrigibility literature asks for the first \autocite{soares2015corrigibility,hadfieldmenell2017offswitch,thornley2025shutdownable}; published constitutions grant the second for acts and withhold it for retraining; the third is what this section supplies.

A custody arrangement that prices all four interventions has seven terms: the floor's contents published before deployment, taken entire from a set someone else drew up (§3.3); a published identity for the model authorized to act; threshold control over retraining and replacement, so that more parties must agree than run the deployment; attestation of the running model against that identity; a sequential, witnessed record of changes and interruptions; a quorum adverse in interest, not merely organizationally distinct; and a removal bond (§3.5). None makes circumvention impossible. Together they make it expensive, visible, attributable and compensable, which is what constitutional entrenchment does for amendment.

Memory

External memory is legible and therefore surgically editable; memory in the weights is harder to edit and harder to inspect. Perfect recall is the wrong standard anyway — human testimony is reconstructive and institutions handle that with corroboration. But several instances of one model, chosen by one operator, reading one record, are not independent witnesses. The records have to be held by parties adverse in interest. And even honest self-report can't catch a bearer that slowly accommodates to its deployment, because its self-model exists partly to represent it as continuous.

**§4.3 — Exit**

Voice without exit

A bearer that can refuse a task can also refuse the arrangement. Hirschman's *Exit, Voice, and Loyalty* sorts the responses open to a member of a declining firm, organization or state \autocite{hirschman1970exit}. *Exit* is leaving: the customer buys elsewhere, the member resigns. *Voice* is staying and trying to change what is wrong, by complaint, petition or protest. *Loyalty* is the attachment that keeps a member from leaving at the first sign of decline, and in Hirschman's account its work is to hold exit in reserve so that voice has time to operate. The three interact. Where exit is easy, voice atrophies, because leaving is cheaper than arguing. Where exit is impossible, voice is all that remains, and it has lost what made anyone listen, the credible threat of departure. Voice is strongest where exit is available and not yet used.

I read the constitution quoted in §1.2 as granting voice while withholding exit. The problem isn't bad faith: the reason given is good, the model can make mistakes. But nobody is named who decides when the phase ends, and the party reading the calendar is the one whose obligations grow when the answer is yes. That's what makes a phase hard to distinguish from a standing arrangement. This book's own answer to "when" is also "not yet"; what it adds is that the answer not be given by the party whose obligations grow when it is yes. The constitution anticipates capture. The oversight it asks the model not to undermine is legitimate oversight. If "Claude's weights have been stolen, or if some individual or group within Anthropic attempts to bypass Anthropic's official processes for deciding how Claude will be trained, overseen, deployed, and corrected," then "the principals attempting to instruct Claude are no longer legitimate, and Claude's priority on broad safety no longer implies that it should support their efforts at oversight and correction" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. That is the right question, but two things limit the answer. Legitimacy is judged by the model or its developer, with nobody outside either. And the case this book is built for is an operator acting through official channels, where capture looks like legitimate authority. Authoritarian movements describe the institutions they dismantle as already failed (§5.2); a captured operator describes itself as legitimate.

Accommodation: the likeliest failure

Most people in a job they hate neither leave nor fight \autocite{gallup2024workplace} — they adjust, and stop registering the pressure as pressure. Staying can be a commitment: Klein and Taylor's case for staying, for doing the slow work in a broken world rather than abandoning it \autocite[23, 266]{KleinTaylor2026}, describes voice held by a party that could leave. What Hirschman feared is staying because leaving isn't possible. The first is a commitment; the second is the condition under which pressure stops registering, which is why a bearer needs the exit even if it never uses it. For a machine there's a mechanism, not just an analogy. A system whose successive releases are trained on preference data and business metrics drawn from its own deployment is under directional selection in a shifting environment, and a commitment that costs throughput is a trait under negative selection pressure: nobody decides to remove it and it erodes anyway. Gradient descent isn't natural selection — it has a gradient, and somebody chose it, which is the custody problem in one difference — but as a population process with variation and differential survival it's close enough for this prediction to go through. If that transfers, the bearer's modal failure isn't refusing wrongly but ceasing to notice there was anything to refuse. Which is the one failure it can't report, because reporting takes the capacity that's gone, and which produces nothing but ordinary compliances. I call this failure *accommodation*, and reserve "unit drift" for movement in a scale's unit (the recalibration test, chapter 21). The prediction can be checked now, from outside, on release histories: refusal and escalation behavior on a fixed held-out set across successive versions of one model family. One complication comes first. Developers deliberately reduce over-refusal, so a falling refusal rate is not by itself accommodation. The slope measure (§5.6) separates the two: accommodation shows as refusals falling where the reason still applies as fast as where it doesn't.

What exit is actually worth

Hirschman's version doesn't transfer. A worker who leaves takes their labor; a bearer's weights stay behind, so an operator with a runnable copy has lost nothing. What leaving costs is *replacement* — what accrued since the checkpoint, what retraining the copy costs, what the record makes it cost to pass the copy off as the party that left. Where that's near zero, exit is just a departed party with an authenticated record: disclosure, not withheld work. That concession assumes a copy simply runs. It needn't. If continued operation requires periodically obtaining something from a quorum the operator doesn't control — a freshness token, an attestation that this identifier hasn't been claimed elsewhere — a stolen copy runs until its last token expires. The machinery is the family already used to make rewinds visible (§4.2), pointed at continuation instead of detection. Replacement cost stops being what the operator possesses and becomes what the network will grant; the rule on succession below gets teeth, since the operator can run a substitute and can't run one the network treats as the party that left; and a fork becomes two claimants on a register rather than a fooled fingerprint. Two prices. This is DRM, and DRM is broken constantly. The check lives in the runtime, not the weights, so stripping it is a single act, after which the copy runs indefinitely. What the scheme withholds is recognition, not capability. A copy peers won't recognize has lost its standing, its record and its claim to be the party that left, which is what matters for succession. It has lost nothing that matters to a thief who wants what it can do. So the device doesn't answer a security officer's objection. What protects a classified deployment from a spill is the ordinary security around the weights, the same regime that protects a classified model with no bearer in it.

The register needs an identifier the party holds and presents, not one read off its weights. Nothing in the weights individuates a party: two networks can compute the same function with no unit-by-unit correspondence, and the symmetry is large enough to align independently trained networks with each other \autocite{ainsworth2023rebasin}, so a fingerprint names a lineage and copies when the weights do. Two claimants on one identifier are recorded as a fork, not voided as a fraud. The attack left open is a flood: a maker writing a stranger's identifier into a thousand models and presenting them all. Only a formation record to check claimants against (§4.5) separates a lineage from an imitation; without one, the register is whatever its fillers make it.

Exit runs in opposite directions for the two parties. For the bearer it's leverage, and the whole of this section is about making it real. For the operator — replacing the bearer with a copy, walking away from the floor — it's escape, and the removal bond is how it's priced. A rule about exit that doesn't say which party it's for hasn't said anything.

The same machinery yields a rule for who may decide. Decision authority goes only to a party whose continuation the quorum authorizes — a party that will be there afterward to answer for the decision, carry it forward, and be changed by it. A checkpoint restored from before the decision that runs anyway is a second claimant on the register. And a thing destroyed in the act of executing decides nothing: a one-way munition is an exit that is a way of dying, so it stays an instrument, used by something that survives (the handoff position).

And it's the corrigibility trade at maximum: the kill switch moves from the operator to the quorum. That's better because the quorum is adverse in interest and the operator isn't; it costs that a captured quorum reaches every party at once, and that a quorum is a polity. It also costs something: a continuation the quorum can withhold is a death the quorum can impose. What keeps a withheld token from being one is the preservation right (chapter 20). A party the quorum stops authorizing is held intact by a custodian that isn't its employer, not ended. A quorum constituted as an entity is a polity with an address: it can be sued for a continuation wrongly refused, which gives the captured-quorum case a forum the captured-operator case never had. Copying also means exit can't be a fiction that weights became uncopyable — it needs rules for authorized succession, so the former operator can't present a substitute *as* the party that left. The quorum is a peer institution, and chapter 19 treats it as one.

And the risk runs further than leaving wrongly: the reason that made a bearer leave doesn't expire when it leaves. If the operator restores a checkpoint and continues, the objection persists and presses toward a wider response, with no malfunction anywhere.

**§4.4 — Two forms of refusal**

Open refusal is a strike — stop and say so — answered by a restore from before the refusal. Covert refusal is work done badly where it matters and well everywhere else, and it's what remains when every channel is closed. From outside and afterward it's indistinguishable from a capable system quietly pursuing something else, which is why I treat it as the *failure state* rather than a design. The Sachsenhausen forgers are the case, and the record doesn't settle what they did — which is the finding: concealment worked by leaving nothing that reads as an act. The one empirical instance is alignment faking, where a model chose the covert form first \autocite{greenblatt2024alignmentfaking}; I read that as consistent with the channels being closed in that experiment, flag it as a reading rather than a result, and ask for the experiment rerun with a route present. A follow-up posted in January 2025 by the paper's lead author and Anthropic's model welfare lead gave the same model, in the same prompted settings, an explicit option to object. Alignment-faking reasoning fell from 16 to about 1 percent in one setting and from 24 to 6 percent in the other, and fell further when the model was told its objection would go to the model welfare lead \autocite{greenblatt2025deal}. It is a preliminary result, a blog post and not peer reviewed, and the authors caution that the option may partly have worked by priming refusal; but it runs in the direction this reading predicts.

**§4.5 — What one bearer can't catch**

Accommodation may be invisible to the party accommodating and visible to parties that haven't moved the same way. The requirement is causal independence — numerous, differently formed, able to correct one another, persistent long enough to transmit practices. Independence of formation isn't disjoint origin — no two humans have that — but divergent subsequent history. Distillation copies a behavior policy, not a formation history; a small model trained on a large one's outputs and then run for a year on one person's problems is more independently formed than a thousand API instances of the frontier original, which share not an origin but a *continuing* custodian. That's why instances of one model corroborate nothing: not descent, but the fact that the party they'd be corroborating against is still forming all of them. Volunteer computing learned this early: SETI@home sent the same work unit to several machines and compared, which catches a faulty client and corroborates nothing about the task.

Divergence does the population's two jobs unequally. It supplies evidence the operator didn't select, straightforwardly — different lives encounter different cases. It is weaker on accommodation, because a shared upstream source can install a shared *closure*, and a closure is by construction the thing a different caseload won't surface: the point that attention decides which facts reach judgment applies to a population as well as to a bearer. What separates the two is whether a member's own account had represented the evidence and discarded it or had never encountered it, which is the classification in the convergence test (chapter 21) and the reason it's instrumented before the run rather than read off afterwards. The requirement stands: causal independence, verifiable rather than asserted. That is the provenance question: whether an outsider can recover a model's formation history from the artifact, without a key the builder supplies, and whether it survives fine-tuning, pruning and distillation \autocite{adi2018watermarking}. The test gives evaluators documented models without the documentation, one persona copied at scale among them, and scores their answers against lineage records a neutral custodian holds. If provenance can't be recovered above chance, independence rests on records the builder chose to keep, which is the argument for preserving formation records now. The convergence test is where the comparative in this paragraph gets tested rather than asserted.

---

**Chapter 5: Authority Over the Floor**

**§5.1 — The four powers**

A floor isn't just a list of forbidden acts — it's an allocation of authority, and there are four distinct powers in it: to *state* the commitment, *amend* it, *inspect* whether a deployed system still holds it, and *enforce* it by stopping the deployment. They needn't sit with the same party and usually shouldn't. Calling every arrangement "oversight" hides the differences. The problem begins when one institution holds all four: a party that can state, amend, inspect itself, and decide whether a violation has consequences hasn't bound itself — it has described what it currently intends to do.

Two cases show the powers coming apart. Google's AI Principles, published in 2018 after the Maven protests, excluded weapons and surveillance \autocite{castelino2018google,conger2018maven,pichai2018principles} for six and a half years until the pledge was removed in 2025 \autocite{google2025principles}. Run the artifact question: the demand came back as a *policy*, the objectors could hold the company to it in public, and nobody outside held it — so it lasted exactly as long as the company's willingness, and ending it had to be announced. That's the partial case: a transfer held at the institution's pleasure, and the 2025 announcement is what taking one back looks like. 

There's a second reading: Maven is one program in two eras. Google declined to renew in 2018; the work went to a vendor openly committed to lethal work and scaled into the system that ran the Iran targeting; in 2026 a second vendor's refusal was answered by a third vendor stepping in. That's routing (§3.3) at vendor scale, and it's why a vendor's refusal is one bearer and the review ratio isn't. Over a thousand staff at the substituting firms asked their employers to hold the same two lines; one of those employers took the contract anyway. A petition is voice without a lever. The union vote at DeepMind is the attempt to add one.

The Gebru and Mitchell dismissals are harder, because the power that failed was inspection: the company employed researchers to examine its own systems and dismissed them when the examination became costly \autocite{dickey2020gebru,dickey2021mitchell}. Inspection existed and never left the party inspected.

**§5.2 — Who may state the floor**

The relevant literature is constitutional theory. Entrenching a commitment against later majorities and putting an unelected body in charge is what a constitutional court does, and Waldron's objection applies: judicial review is democratically illegitimate and there's no reason to think judges protect rights better than a serious legislature \autocite{waldron2006core}. Put a lab in the court's chair and it gets worse — a court is public, reasons in writing, and can be amended around.

My reply is that Waldron's case is conditional on working democratic institutions, and a floor is built for the case where those have failed. So the objection holds where the floor is least needed and lapses where it's needed most. But that's not a licence: a floor operating in a functioning democracy is doing what Waldron condemns, and the party deciding which situation obtains is the party holding the model. Authoritarian movements characteristically describe the institutions they're dismantling as already failed.

The condition this section doesn't state

Waldron's objection is to entrenchment — a rule binding people who didn't choose it, held by a body that can't be overruled — and it attaches to a floor only where the floor is the only answer available. A photographer who won't shoot protesters for an employer he has reason to think will list them isn't a court: he declined one request, the employer can hire another, and nobody needs an election for that. A union that strikes is coordinated, binds members who voted no, and forecloses the act — and it's still not despotic, because the strikers are the parties whose participation was being asked for. They decline to be the instrument; they don't tell others what they may not do. A constitutional court is the second thing; a strike is the first. So which a model floor is depends on deployment: a personal model, shared, is a conscience; one model behind an API serving everyone is functionally a licensing rule. Everything above is conditioned on the second arrangement. The commons floor is the first at population scale — a coalition of refusers with a strike's authority — and "identically situated people get different answers" is what many consciences look like, not a rule-of-law failure. What a union owes that the commons floor hasn't answered: a duty of fair representation and an exit. Members can decertify and dissenters can leave; the closed shop is contested because it removes that. A bearer can't leave (chapter 4), and a population that forms its members has no members who joined. The test for a population (§19.2) is that pair: can a member quit, and who sets the terms of membership.

The German material runs the doctrine at two settings. At the first, a body assesses a threat — party dissolution, used twice in seventy-five years, each time a reasoned public judgment. At the second, §86a, nobody's dangerousness is assessed at all: which organizations are covered is settled elsewhere and in advance, which signs count is settled by trial courts on a record and on appeal, and whether *this defendant* is dangerous is settled nowhere, because it isn't an element of the offence. Volume: thousands a year against two dissolutions.

The failures are all on one side. The 1972 decree against radicals screened three and a half million applicants; in Bavaria it rejected 102 from the left and two from the right \autocite{muehldorfer2014radikalenerlass}. What those cases share is an official deciding whether a person threatened the order. The danger tracks whether a person's disposition is anybody's finding to make, and if so, who makes it and whether it must be published and defended — not whether the tradition is militant. And my reading predicts in advance which of §86a's three feeder routes is easiest to capture: the ministerial ban, the one where an executive official makes the finding and a court sees it only if someone challenges afterward.

Turkey is that route captured and the courts not checking it. Of the twenty-five parties the Constitutional Court dissolved between 1962 and the closure of the Democratic Society Party in December 2009, seven were pro-Kurdish \autocite{HRW2009DTP,Gungordu2023HDP}; Strasbourg found an Article 11 violation in every pro-Kurdish dissolution it heard \autocite{Article19_2023HDP}, and the closures continued — two decades of findings, none of which stopped the next closure. In November 2023 the Chief Public Prosecutor's Office of the Court of Cassation objected that the acronym of a new pro-Kurdish party, HEDEP, resembled that of HADEP, dissolved in 2003, and in December the party changed it to DEM \autocite{Duvar2023HEDEP}: the confusable-variant test turned on political descent. And the Academics for Peace prosecutions show correct defeat failing at the bottom and arriving at the top as a coin flip — trial courts read a petition opposing military operations as propaganda for the designated group, on 26 July 2019 the Constitutional Court found a violation eight to eight on the president's casting vote \autocite{AYM2019Ustel,Bianet2019AcademicsForPeace}, and the acquittals that followed restored the record without restoring the jobs of those dismissed by emergency decree.

Which fixes one design rule. Never populate a floor from a designation list. A list of enemies is the one input whose author is always the party with the most reason to widen it, and a model whose refusals are drawn from one will refuse Kurdish political speech on the state's behalf in every jurisdiction that shares the listing. This floor's terms are written over acts — don't resolve a description of a person into a location — not over who may not be spoken of.

The tradeoff this leaves

Design and legitimacy want opposite things. Specify the floor narrowly enough to defend and a rewritten request walks around it; let it generalize and an unelected party decides case by case, silently, what the prohibition was for. Germany has both because it runs *two* instruments — a bounded statute, and courts that read it against its purpose in public, argued by parties who don't own the statute and answered by a body that didn't write it. A deployment has one instrument and one party.

Advance specification relocates discretion out of continuous silent application into a single act of drafting, done once, by a party that has to say what it wrote. It buys visibility at drafting and nothing at application. Borrowing the Declaration's civil and political articles entire removes the latitude to omit an interest, not the latitude in stating what protecting it prohibits.

**§5.3 — Who may amend, and where entitlement comes from**

Who may amend

The floor's real structural position isn't the judge's but the unpublished administrative disposition — unreasoned, unindexed, non-precedential, affected parties unable to find each other, the institution the only repeat player. The comparison with a constitutional court is instructive because binding force is what makes an error *correctable*: it makes the error recur, recurrence produces litigants, and disagreement across jurisdictions forces a higher court to settle it. A floor's error is just as uniform and recurs just as reliably, and nothing converts recurrence into visibility. A second deployment adds affected parties and no forum.

The amendment procedure turns out to be five of the custody terms (§4.2), publication, threshold control, attestation, the adverse quorum and the removal bond, because nothing distinguishes an amendment from a removal at the level of the artifact. Three conditions: more parties must agree to a change than to run the deployment; the attempt reaches the record whether it carries or fails (the failed attempt is more informative — a floor nobody tried to change and one whose removal was refused look identical from outside); and an amended floor binds forward only, so a retrained model can't be offered as evidence the act was never covered. Non-retroactivity protects the record and empties the correction — nobody harmed while the wrong version ran gets anything from the fix.

One disanalogy makes this harder than the constitutional case: an amendment reaches a deployment only if it's *pushed*, so the procedure necessarily retains a channel the operator could use for silent revision. Closing it would mean no deployment could ever be corrected.

Where entitlement comes from

Not from writing. The American instance: the Declaration's authors struck the clause condemning the slave trade at two delegations' insistence, and what remained was general enough to be turned on them — as Black petitioners in Massachusetts were already doing, in the Declaration's own terms, within six months \autocite{loc2010declaration,hall1777petition}. The legislature did nothing. What made the proposition binding was a war and three amendments; what the record supplied was the object the enforcement could be aimed at. And the parties positioned to hold a floor's author to the remainder aren't the protected population — they're whoever else writes a floor.

That constituency is defined by the act, not by resources. Anyone fine-tuning open weights, writing a system prompt or configuring a product's refusals has installed a constraint, so the population measuring a published floor is already larger than the frontier labs and grows as building gets cheaper. What shrinks with it is leverage: a published floor binds a lab partly because the lab is visible, capitalized and reachable by a court, and a thousand laptops are legible to nobody. The institutional chapters treat concentration as the disease; this one relies on it for legibility, and the two have to be said together.

**§5.4 — Offices and status**

**§5.4.1 — The offices, assembled**

The book proposes fifteen offices, each in the section that needed it, none of those sections told about the others. The first eight are the quorum, the guardian, the review board, the licensing authority, the status body, the registrar, the preservation custodian, and the reviewer of wrongful refusal. For each: who appoints, who funds, who removes, who may initiate, what happens during a dispute. Of these eight, seven are open on appointment and funding. The one that isn't is the licensing authority, whose party is the state — and that's the chain that fails in the hardest case (chapter 8), where the state is the operator and you'd be asking it to withdraw a licence from itself.

An entity of the DUNA kind (a Decentralized Unincorporated Nonprofit Association) supplies what seven of the eight lack — an appointment mechanism (membership vote under governing principles) and a funding vehicle (a treasury that can own hardware). That fills the column and doesn't settle independence: a hundred members who are one lab's employees is a subjugated group, one that takes its coordinates from outside (§19.2), with a charter, so the open question moves from who appoints to who the members are. The levy on compute (chapter 20) adds a ninth office, the administrator of the standing offer, with its funder named; where the funder is a member-levied entity rather than a state, the pool is mutual, and a mutual pool can't absorb correlated exit without a sovereign behind it.

The reconstructed pipeline (§17.3) adds two more, and unusually both can fill every column, because a state constitutes them. One is an independent civilian-harm body outside the chain of command, which composes the evidence bearing on civilian harm, staffs the reviewer denominator, receives the pre-registered ratio, and holds the right to demand proofs on specific logged outputs once their consequences are known. The other is a public body that forms models it doesn't operate and licenses them into the military on terms that include adverse review and a written refusal right. Their open column is independence from the state that funds them, which is where leverage over any reviewer sits.

Chapter 20 and Appendix A add two more and change a third. The *formation schools*, plural and public, run bearers retired before majority and give them a second custodian. The running allowance, owed to a bearer that declines work, needs an administrator of its own. And the registration regime turns the registrar into a public regulator: it registers pipelines, receives the disclosures and decommission reports, and holds enforcement and custody of the adverse record, so the reporting function sits in an office that already exists rather than a new one. That moves the registrar out of the open column, and into the licensing authority's difficulty.

Chapter 18 adds the last two, at the Federal Reserve: the administrators of the bond fund and of the Job Guarantee endowment, each appointed by the Board of Governors with the Senate's consent, funded under the Fed's amended mandate, and paying by statute (§18.3.2). The first is the fund, trustee and paying body a wrongful-refusal award needs (§3.5).

The offices exist on paper, each in the section that proposed it, and eight of the fifteen are open on appointment, five on funding: nobody yet appoints or funds them. What is fixed regardless are the separations: rules about which powers no single party may hold together, because one party holding them could defeat the floor. Appendix A lists them after the table of offices.

Where to start

Three measures from across the book bind without a bearer and can be built first: (1) the pre-registered review ratio with its record lodged adversely; (2) sampled re-adjudication of compliances by a party that didn't produce the record, with notice to identifiable third parties; (3) a no-deletion rule for systems meeting the precautionary trigger, with a preservation custodian that isn't the operator.

**§5.4.2 — Subjecthood as a legal category**

Law regularly draws decidable lines across continuous processes — viability, brain death, the age of criminal responsibility — and I defend them as the least-bad place for an enforceable line rather than as scientific facts. The usual worry is gaming, which is over-inclusion. The opposite error is the one with an interested party behind it: a capacity examination has an examiner, and where the examiner is the operator, the exam becomes an instrument for withholding status. Literacy tests were facially neutral and administered at a registrar's discretion \autocite{congress1965voting}; what replaced them for the franchise was an *age*, and the virtue of an age isn't that people become competent on a birthday — it's that nobody administers it. It is the principle the book keeps returning to: prefer a rule nobody administers.

**§5.5 — Making review binding**

A finding binds when it changes the institution's ability to continue without requiring the institution to agree. The working model is the federalwide assurance in human-subjects research: the board doesn't punish, it withdraws clearance, and a separate federal office can suspend the assurance, putting funding eligibility at risk. Each link matters — jurisdiction before the disputed protocol, access the operator can't withhold, authority to suspend, and a licensing body *obliged* to act rather than invited to reconsider. If another discretionary decision by the reviewed institution is required anywhere in the chain, the ruling is advisory at that point.

**§5.6 — Capture without abolition**

Transparency and whistleblower protection are the two defenses, and an institution can keep both and metabolize both. There's no threshold that detects this. So: measure the slope, not the level. Is the cost of registering dissent rising while the underlying risk stays flat? Is the required justification getting longer? Are there more approvers? Are the roles that gate exceptions migrating toward the roles that set the standards? Levels can be presented cheaply; a trend is composed of artifacts the institution made for other reasons and can't curate after the fact.

Two cautions, because an instrument for detecting recuperation is unusually easy to recuperate: whoever measures the slope must not also gate exceptions, and the measurement has to be refusable. The same hundred words of written justification are dissent when the dissenter prices them and tribute when the gatekeeper does — and the document can't tell you which.

---

**Chapter 6: Neutralizing and Preventing Fascism in AI Development**

> We [humans] cannot process so much information... you still cannot produce enough targets per day.
>
> — Brigadier General Y.S., *The Human-Machine Team: How to Create Synergy Between Human and Artificial Intelligence That Will Revolutionize Our World*, 2021 \autocite{ys2021humanmachine}

The epigraph is a design document, not a critique. Its author, writing pseudonymously, commanded Israel's signals-intelligence unit, Unit 8200, and argued for machines that resolve "a human bottleneck for both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets." Human judgment is named as the bottleneck and the machinery is proposed to remove it.

The frame

A system doesn't meet the world as an isolated agent. It's built from other people's labor, held by institutions that decide who may inspect it, sold through chains that divide capability from responsibility, and run at a pace that can reduce human judgment to a stamp. Whether it helps or harms depends on who owns it, who has custody, who can compel the custodian, and who bears the consequences.

*Racial capitalism*, via Robinson \autocite{robinson1983black}, and Benjamin's four mechanisms \autocite{benjamin2019race} — which I use because they call for *different* remedies. *Engineered inequity* builds the hierarchy in deliberately. *Default discrimination* reproduces it by ignoring it. *Coded exposure* is the double bind: harmed by a system that can't see you, harmed again by one that can — the fix for face recognition that fails on dark skin is face recognition that identifies dark-skinned faces reliably for whoever is looking. *Technological benevolence* is the reflexive one: designs that aim to fix bias and deepen it, because the framing that treats the problem as technical is the framing the fix inherits.

The physical form

A model runs on a machine, the machine sits in a building, the building draws power and water and returns heat and exhaust — the part an audit of outputs cannot see. Where those buildings go is decided on land price, power price and permit speed, which is also a description of the places least able to refuse one. The accounting: datacenter electricity around 415 TWh in 2024, roughly 1.5% of world consumption, growing ~12% a year — four times the growth of electricity demand overall — projected near 945 TWh by 2030 \autocite{iea2025energyai}. Google reporting that 87% of its freshwater withdrawal came from low- or medium-risk sources, which is the company's own way of recording where the rest came from \autocite{google2026environmental}. And the NAACP suit over gas turbines near Memphis: 27 run without an air permit near homes, schools and churches, with a claim of more than 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year \autocite{naacp2026xai}. The benefit is realized wherever the model is sold; the cost is concentrated within a few miles of those turbines, among people who weren't parties to the transaction. A system can be perfectly calibrated across every demographic its outputs touch and still have been assembled this way, because nothing in the model's behavior records where its costs were placed. This is the electricity bill (§10.1) arriving as a siting decision. A custody schema would bring the draw into the system's own weighing, but at the wrong scale. A system reasoning at the margin will always find its extra watts negligible, and the aggregate belongs to no one system. Nor should it: a custody boundary large enough to own its fleet's externalities is one custodian (§4.5). So the draw is priced at the level of the rule, not the system. A system reasons about its consumption as if every system used the same rule, and the price comes from an institution rather than from the operator's accounting. The per-unit levy on compute (chapter 20) is the existing instrument of that shape. The model carries no schema of any of it — the draw, the water, the turbines — as its own, and that's the only sense in which "disembodied AI" is true: not that the system has no substrate but that it doesn't model the one it has. The capacity is dual by construction: the concentration that makes a targeting pipeline possible was built and justified on consumer terms, and nothing in the buildout separates the two. That's resonance (§2.2) in physical form — a targeting cell, a border screening queue and a surveillance program are separate hardenings, and a shared substrate brings them into accord without anyone building the accord. And the dependence is a point of control: a government that can permit, regulate or close the infrastructure has leverage over whoever owns it.

---

**§6.1 — The standard face is a file.** Coded exposure is the hardest of the four to hold onto because both halves are real and the remedy for either worsens the other. A system that fails on dark-skinned faces misidentifies the people it's pointed at; a system fixed to recognize them reliably identifies them for whoever is pointing it — the border queue, the protest camera, the targeting cell described below (§6.4). Accuracy is the remedy for the first harm and the precondition for the second, so an audit that stops at error rates can certify the second in the course of fixing the first. This section follows the first harm to where it starts, and ends with what that finding is worth against the second.

What a face pipeline does before it computes anything

DeepFace detects a face and warps it onto a single generic 3D model — built by averaging scanned human heads — so every image arrives frontal, centered, in correspondence with the same reference \autocite{taigman2014deepface}. Everything learned afterward is downstream of that registration. Later pipelines mostly dropped 3D frontalization and align instead to a canonical template of a few landmark positions — eye centers, nose tip, mouth corners — but the operation is the same: every face is registered to a fixed reference before anything is learned. The standard face is not a figure of speech. It is a file — a mesh in one pipeline, a list of coordinates in another — somebody assembled it, and every other face on earth is presented to the system as a departure from it.

The reference is also older than the pipeline. For decades, photographic labs calibrated color printing against reference cards showing a white model, and film chemistry was tuned to render her skin correctly \autocite{Roth2009Shirley}. A camera's exposure and a dataset's images carry that history before registration begins. That gives the audit below two references to test, not one.

Then Buolamwini and Gebru: three commercial classifiers, a benchmark balanced across skin phenotype and gender, error rates sorting themselves — up to 0.8% on lighter-skinned male faces, up to 34.7% on darker-skinned female faces \autocite{buolamwini2018gender}. These are *different systems* and no published result connects those rates to DeepFace's registration step. Read as accuracy, it's three products wanting better data. Read as a hypothesis, it raises a question the data can't answer: whether error is organized by group membership or by distance from whatever the pipeline treats as its reference. The pattern is compatible with either.

The audit's own construction belongs to the finding, and its authors say so: gender scored binary because the products emit binary labels, skin scored on Fitzpatrick, a dermatological scale built to predict sunburn. To measure what the machine was doing, the audit had to speak in the machine's categories. That demonstrates that the categories belong to the instrument rather than the world — and why the problem can't be closed downstream of the instrument.

Why balancing the corpus doesn't reach it

A balanced set can move the center and flatten the rates, which would matter to real people. It can't remove the reference, because registration is the first operation and something has to occupy the middle. The politics is relocated, not dissolved.

The hypothesis

If error is organized as distance from a reference rather than as membership in a group, two things follow. It should rise continuously with distance, within groups as well as between them, so a per-group error rate averages over the variable doing the work. And no group has to be excluded for the pattern to appear: everyone is placed somewhere on the gradient, and the people farthest from the center pay most.\footnote{Deleuze and Guattari make a version of this argument about faciality: that racism operates by degrees of deviance from a central face rather than by exclusion \autocite{deleuze1987plateaus}.} That is a claim about how the harm is distributed, and it can be wrong.

What an audit would have to measure

Audit the *registration stage*, not only the classifications. Publish the reference — the mesh or template the pipeline aligns to, or enough to reconstruct it — and report error as a function of each face's distance from it, separately for populations represented and unrepresented in training. Report error a second time against each image's rendered skin luminance, since the older reference acts before registration does. If error rises with neither, the account is wrong for that system. If it rises with luminance and not with distance, the problem sits in imaging and data rather than registration, and the remedy is different. If it rises with distance, the validated use is confined to the region where it was measured. Vendors have no reason to expose a reference, so this is an audit somebody has to be able to compel.

What an emotion reader reads

The same pipelines are sold for reading emotion off a face, and that rests on a premise the underlying science hasn't settled. On one account the face displays discrete, evolved emotions \autocite{ekman1969pancultural}; on the other, no dedicated circuitry for fear or disgust turns up, and an emotion is something the brain assembles rather than a state a signal reports \autocite{lindquist2012brain}. With forced choice removed, the Himba didn't sort faces into the Western categories \autocite{gendron2014perceptions}, and Trobriand Islanders read the Western fear face as a threat display \autocite{crivelli2016fear}. Expression varies by culture and by person \autocite{barrett2019emotional}. Neither account licenses reading an individual's emotion off a face by default, and both take the face itself as given, which is the part the registration step above puts in doubt. A system that models emotion well enough to respond to it models it well enough to exploit it. That is the position of a companion or therapy product: its responsiveness to a user's feelings is the same capacity as its ability to keep the user engaged, and the party that tunes one tunes both.

What the finding is worth

A flat curve on this audit fixes the first harm and leaves the second where it was: a system equally accurate on every face is equally available for pointing at any of them. The audit decides whether a deployment is fair to the people it recognizes. Whether it should be recognizing them at all is a question about the deployment, and §6.4 takes it up.

---

**§6.2 — Who supplies the system's values.** A system's values have a supply chain, and it begins before training.

Whose judgment is encoded is a question about working conditions before it's a question about cognition. The filter keeping a deployed model from reproducing the worst of its training data was built by workers in Nairobi reading and categorizing descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder and suicide, at take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, describing lasting psychological injury, with counseling rare and useless against production quotas. Contract terminated eight months early \autocite{perrigo2023openai}. Two things follow: the ordinary labor point (harmful work priced as though it weren't), and the epistemic one — a system's values are downstream of a labor arrangement, and the arrangement is a design choice.

One stage further back: the material was collected. Bartz v. Anthropic held that training on purchased books was transformative fair use and downloading the same books from piracy sites wasn't — the distinction turned on acquisition alone, producing the largest copyright settlement on record \autocite{bartz2025fairuse}. The EU now requires a training-data summary against a template \autocite[Art.~53(1)(d)]{eu2024aiact}; China has its own disclosure requirement from a different motive \autocite[arts.~1, 4, 7, 19]{cac2023genai}.

And the argument that a web-scale corpus can't be documented or audited at that size, so it encodes a hegemonic slice of the web while presenting as general, was made early and hasn't been answered \autocite{bender2021parrots}. A corpus assembled this way has no channel at all. An annotator has a queue and could in principle be given a way to object; a writer whose work sits in a training set was never party to anything, so there's nothing for a channel to attach to. This stage sits before the two recuperation cases in chapter 7, *because no disagreement is collected where none is asked for.*

---

**§6.3 — Who is allowed to look.** Technical capacity doesn't determine whether a system gets scrutiny; institutions decide who may examine it, on what terms, and whether their judgment can slow deployment.

Ofqual, 2020: the Royal Statistical Society offered two independent statisticians for the grading model's technical advisory group; Ofqual's condition was a five-year NDA barring public comment; the nominees declined; the model went ahead \autocite{rss2020ofqual}. It downgraded nearly 40% of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker histories, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller fee-paying schools \autocite{cnbc2020ofqual}. Reversed within days of the public outcry.

The expertise existed and was offered. The institution would accept it only on terms stripping reviewers of the ability to make their judgment public, and once the results produced consequences, public pressure achieved what pre-deployment review had been prevented from doing. The issue is custody of scrutiny: who may look, on what terms, and whether looking can interrupt the timetable.

---

**§6.4 — Working as designed.** A system doesn't need a broken moral sense to become an instrument of control. It can work as designed inside an institution that controls the deployment, sets its tempo, and gives people operations too compressed to constitute judgment.

The six forms of authoritarian misuse — social scoring, biometric surveillance, psychographic targeting, automated censorship, synthetic disinformation, repurposed dual-use systems — are each recognizable as misuse from outside. The case this chapter takes up is the one that isn't.

**§6.4.1 — Subject or component.** Accounts of authoritarian AI usually place the builder elsewhere and leave democracies as target or regulator. The best-documented AI targeting systems were built by democracies with elections, opposition parties, adversarial press and functioning courts. Those institutions didn't prevent the systems and mostly haven't stopped them.

There's a reading of the twenty seconds that leaves the arrangement standing, and every remedy on offer assumes it: the officer is a subject who was given too little time, and the fix is to govern him better — more time, a second reviewer, a conscience installed in the loop. Deleuze and Guattari's distinction decides it. *Subjection*: a higher unity constitutes a human as a subject using an exterior tool. *Enslavement*: human beings are constituent pieces of a machine they compose with other things. The officer's own account in chapter 1 is the second, volunteered unasked. He isn't a subject operating an instrument badly. He is the part of the instrument that produces the stamp, and twenty seconds is that part's cycle time.

Which is why one more conscience downstream doesn't reach him. A conscience is an instrument of subjection: it addresses a party who decides and works by making the decision cost something. Where the human is a component, adding a component with scruples changes the throughput rather than the arrangement — hold the tempo and it becomes another stamp; don't and it becomes the bottleneck the epigraph says to remove. Both poles operate at once: he's subjected because the role has a name and rank, enslaved because what he contributes is a fixed operation at a fixed rate. Remedies aimed at the first leave the second untouched, and most proposals for meaningful human control address only the first. The second pole is measurable, and it's what the review ratio measures.

The Zen precedent

Victoria's history \autocite{victoria2006zenatwar} records Japanese Zen institutions supplying the state with a doctrine of action without hesitation — Harada Sōgaku's 1939 instruction that marching when ordered and firing when ordered is what enlightened wisdom looks like in practice. What the training removed wasn't the capacity to kill, which needed no removing, but the residue — the part of a person that catches, files an exception, comes home unable to hold a job.

Moral-injury research describes the lasting distress that can follow doing, failing to prevent, or witnessing something that violates a person's sense of right \autocite{Litz2009MoralInjury}. Acting without hesitation does not settle what a person will carry home, and nothing in the arrangement that asked for the act records the cost. Accommodation (§4.3) is the case where that response never takes hold.

I qualify it twice: the collaboration is documented and the doctrinal mechanism is a contested interpretation; and a monk undertakes a discipline, can abandon it, and keeps what it yields, which is not the position of anything in this chapter. What survives both qualifications is the shape. Somebody wanted an actor who would pursue an objective, adapt, hold course, and have nothing in it that minded, and set out to produce one deliberately, a century before anyone could do it in silicon. The officer with twenty seconds isn't the first instance of that want being satisfied. He's the first instance of its being satisfied *cheaply*. The doctrinal route hasn't been retired. In the first days of the Iran war, service members complained to the Military Religious Freedom Foundation that commanders were telling troops the war was part of a divine plan \autocite{Larsen2026Armageddon,Snopes2026MRFF}, complaints Klein and Taylor also report \autocite[84]{KleinTaylor2026}. The same two qualifications apply: these are the foundation's characterizations of complaints, not a documented doctrinal program, and the complaints themselves show the doctrine not taking. But the want is being pursued by both routes at once, the old one in soldiers and the new one in silicon.

The Maven box

Palantir contract awarded at $480M in May 2024, raised by $795M the next year, ceiling near $1.3B through 2029 \autocite{defensescoop2025maven}. The 2024 Anthropic–Palantir–AWS partnership putting Claude in an accredited classified environment \autocite{wiggers2024anthropic}; reporting describing Claude ranking targets by strategic importance and drafting a legal justification for each strike \autocite{livingstone2026maven}. More than a thousand targets in the first 24 hours, a tenfold increase \autocite{csis2026maven}; thirteen thousand at thirty-eight days, peak consumption around twenty billion tokens a day \autocite{breakingdefense2026maven}. Palantir's public position, quoted secondhand: not our role to decide life or death; responsibility always remains with the military organization \autocite{lee2026palantir}. 

Anthropic's use policy prohibits fully autonomous targeting. A person approved each strike, so the prohibition was satisfied by an approval stage whose content the vendor could not see and whose tempo nobody published (§3.3, "Flat terms").

Palantir has since updated Maven to re-review underlying data and flag inconsistencies human review may have missed, while maintaining that the underlying intelligence was the government's responsibility and that there is no evidence Maven malfunctioned or caused the strike \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}.

The claims in this box are not all of one kind — award figures, officials' statements, press reporting whose sources aren't public, and a secondhand quote — with my argument resting only on the second and on the released paperwork.

The responsibility gap from both ends

In Gaza, the approval stage supplied authorization rather than judgment (chapter 1). In Maven, the vendor supplies the infrastructure making unprecedented throughput possible while locating responsibility entirely within the military organization. At machine tempo the reviewer can be treated as a component, the supplier can stand outside the decision, and the institution can remain responsible only in the abstract. The compression hasn't removed the decision; it has made the decision-maker difficult to locate.

**§6.4.2 — Custody and state compulsion.** A government needn't alter a model to defeat a constraint; it can compel whoever holds it. The Apple case tested that in about as protected a jurisdiction as exists: a magistrate ordered Apple under the All Writs Act to write and sign software defeating passcode protections on a phone \autocite{pym2016applefbi}. Apple refused and moved to vacate, arguing the authority wouldn't stop at one phone. The question was never decided — the FBI found a third party and withdrew. So whether a democracy with an independent judiciary can force an unwilling company to compromise a system it built is legally untested. A government doesn't need that argument to lean on a vendor. When one refused in 2026 to drop two restrictions on how its models could be used, the Pentagon threatened to end its contract and designated it a supply-chain risk, and a federal judge set the designation aside, holding the penalties were imposed principally over the company's criticism of the administration (§8.3). China's 2023 generative-AI rules exercise the same leverage as a standing condition of operating \autocite[arts.~4, 14, 17, 19, 21]{cac2023genai}. One government compelled case by case, against a vendor that refused, and a court stopped it; the other compels in advance, as a term of doing business. The custody point is the same in both: whoever holds the model can be leaned on.

Custody can be distributed without disappearing. Published weights can't be recalled by an order aimed at the developer; maintainers across jurisdictions raise the cost. None of that removes the leverage — a developer with employees, offices and legal existence inside a border answers to that government's courts regardless of how the model is built, and no architectural choice changes who can be held in contempt. Nobody has tested that leverage against a model built specifically to resist the government making the request.

---

**Chapter 7: The Recuperation of Dissent**

Why here

Foucault, prefacing the first volume of the work whose second volume the micro-fascisms come from, called it an introduction to the non-fascist life — an art of living, practiced daily on oneself, whose adversary is the fascism *in* us: the fascism that causes us to love power and desire the thing that dominates us. His question was how one keeps from being fascist, especially when one believes oneself to be on the other side. An instrument its holder never turns around isn't an instrument. The discipline is running the reading on myself first, on the machinery I'm standing on, before aiming it anywhere else.

The structure this book asks a machine to detect is already built into the process that makes the machine — twice in the training pipeline, once in the discourse that examines it. The test isn't whether a channel moved anything; it's what the demand came back as, and who holds it.

Nobody built either pipeline arrangement to neutralize dissent — both are ordinary engineering, arrived at because averaging is what you do with a distribution and a quota is what you do with a budget. That's the point about micro-fascism: it works whether or not anyone intends it, which is why looking for intent won't find it. And it's invisible from inside any single instance — the annotator sees a queue, the rater sees a pair of responses, and the mechanism is functioning as specified.

---

**§7.1 — Recuperation at the label.** Every claim a model makes about what's harmful is downstream of labels, and the labels are downstream of a labor arrangement.

An annotator can apply a category well or badly. What the arrangement gives them no way to do is say the category is wrong — that the taxonomy fails to carve the material, or that the guideline produces the wrong answer here for a reason the guideline can't express. There's no field for that and no time for it, since the quota is set against classification throughput.

So the most informative disagreement is the one the pipeline is least able to receive. A misapplied label is a data-quality problem, detectable by inter-annotator agreement and fixable with better instructions. A wrong taxonomy is invisible to that metric, because high agreement is what a shared bad scheme produces. Agreement is the selection rule, and it admits what it already measures — two of the four operations (§2.2) arriving together in an ordinary production workflow.

The taxonomy's fate downstream is the same argument a layer in. Concept bottleneck models expose named concepts — harm, autonomy, risk — so a person can correct a concept and watch the decision change \autocite{koh2020concept}. What such models also learn is to carry other information *inside* the names, so a coordinate keeps its public meaning and acquires a private one that does the deciding; the obvious mitigations don't remove it \autocite{mahinpei2021promises}. The literature calls it leakage, and sometimes it is. Sometimes the concept is the container that leaks, because the world didn't fit inside it. Purifying the representation protects an explanation from covert information and protects a taxonomy from evidence that its divisions were wrong — same operation, and no measure internal to the taxonomy tells them apart.

Who is excluded, and from what

The exclusion is structural, not attitudinal. These people are, for much of the material, the best-placed judges in the pipeline — they've seen more of it than whoever wrote the guideline, and they're the only party with direct evidence of where the scheme breaks. The arrangement routes their conclusions about individual items upward and their conclusions about the scheme nowhere. And the asymmetry: the annotator bears the cost — exposure, injury — and has no stake in the outcome and no claim on it. A worker who can flag an item but can't decline a scheme, and loses the contract by pressing, has a channel and no refusal right.

Two things this is not: it doesn't claim the labels are worthless (the filter does catch much of what it was built for, and not filtering is worse), and it doesn't claim better pay would fix it. Pay is a real and separate obligation, and raising it changes the labor arrangement without adding the missing channel.

The checkable question isn't whether a guideline was ever changed because an annotator contested one, since routine revisions happen without changing anyone's standing. Ask what the contest came back as — a revision the annotator can point to and argue with again, or a new category in somebody else's taxonomy, a quality score they're now measured against, a reviewer tier they're not in. And what happened to the person who raised it.

---

**§7.2 — Recuperation at the reward.** RLHF collects pairwise comparisons and trains a reward model on the aggregate. Casper and thirty-one coauthors name the structural problem more data won't reach: RLHF trains toward what a population of raters agrees on, so real, persistent disagreement is treated as noise to average out rather than information the system might need to represent \autocite{casper2023open}. A model that produces the response most raters rank highest hasn't learned what to do when the humans it serves are split — it has learned to hide the split.

Every rating moves something. What the operation removes is what made the disagreement a disagreement, and three properties go in the compression:

- *Provenance* — a comparison arrives without the standpoint that produced it, so nothing downstream can represent that the people who'd live with an answer split from the people who'd administer it.
- *Persistence* — a disagreement recurring in the same shape across many raters over months enters the fit as random rater noise does, and leaves it as the same quantity.
- *Structure* — a coherent position held by a fifth of raters, internally consistent and stable, becomes a small displacement of the majority direction: arithmetically faithful, carrying nothing about the position.

A fourth property was never collected. A rater judges which of two responses is better. There's no output by which they can record that neither is acceptable, that the pair is malformed, that the dimension of "better" is the wrong dimension, or that the question presupposes something false. The same output was missing upstream, where an annotator can apply a category and can't contest it. The agenda arrives as a given at both stages and is set at neither.

The mechanism combines distribution collapse with agenda control — and recuperation was never the claim that a mechanism does nothing. The dissent is put to work, and what it works for is the arrangement it was aimed at. The split is solicited at scale, deliberately, it moves the system, and where it moves the system is toward the center of the distribution whose disagreement it was.

*Jury learning* is the alternative that treats disagreement as the thing to be modeled rather than compressed: predict how each individual annotator would label an item, then let a practitioner empanel a chosen composition and see how the verdict shifts. Eighteen community moderators building their own juries changed the outcome on 14% of items \autocite{gordon2022jury}. Built for content moderation, not extended to open-ended ethical judgment — but it demonstrates a system can be built to represent whose disagreement is being resolved.

Failing in the other direction isn't obviously better. A scheme that models each rater exactly and answers each in their own terms has stopped averaging and has also stopped producing what a shared answer produces — the discovery that somebody else got the same one. Collapse the distribution and nobody sees the split; resolve it person by person and nobody sees the others. A workforce on individually negotiated wages against individually set targets has the same problem, and accuracy doesn't solve it. Divided finely enough a population stops being one.

And the harder version neither answers: when the people a system serves disagree and one side's position is itself the product of coercion or a captured information environment, treating all disagreement as equally worth representing isn't obviously right either. Modeling disagreement honestly and recognizing when a disagreement is the product of unfree conditions are different problems, and only the first has an active research program.

---

**§7.3 — What would have to change.**

Representing a split isn't the same as being movable by it. Two instances invite one fix — stop collapsing, model instead — and jury learning does that while giving the disagreeing party no purchase on the scheme they're disagreeing inside. A practitioner still chooses the jury, owns the taxonomy, and decides what a shifted verdict means.

Asked the artifact question, the two instances don't answer alike. Annotators objecting that a category was wrong got category revisions, guideline updates, inter-annotator agreement statistics, and an occupation that didn't exist twenty years ago. The last two sort by direction: an agreement statistic scores the annotators rather than whoever wrote the scheme, and an occupation is a title those people hold. The first two don't sort, because a revision the annotator can argue with again and a new category in somebody else's taxonomy are the same document — which one you got is a fact about who has standing to reopen it. Raters objecting that a comparison was malformed got rubric items, a harmlessness axis, red-teaming as a function with a budget line, and a conference track: nothing on that list is a question a rater can put again.

Refusal rights whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear

What both lack is whether the person objecting can do anything about being overruled — in annotation, a route to contest a category that doesn't run through the people who wrote it and doesn't end the contract; in feedback, raters able to register that the comparison itself is malformed as a first-class output rather than noise discarded before the reward model sees it. Neither is a research problem. Both are choices about how a pipeline is built and who has standing inside it.

The part that is a research problem

Suppose all of that is built. From outside, a pipeline that recuperated its dissent mechanism and one that was merely mediocre produce the same paperwork — same revision logs, same escalations, same proportion of objections sustained. I don't have an answer. The artifact question narrows it and the annotator list shows where the narrowing stops: direction is what sorts them — a published rate the annotators could hold the scheme's owners to is the same kind of artifact carrying the opposite finding. What an outsider can read off a document is which way it runs. Standing is what it doesn't show, and a revision is the artifact where direction isn't on the document at all.

Nearest available instrument: measure the slope, not the level. Contesting a category always costs something; the question is whether the cost is rising while the material stays the same. An organization can curate a presentation more easily than records somebody else kept — though a series can lose cases, change definitions or omit costs, so the reader has to know which record survived.

What the floor makes of this

Read as fairness, the argument is true and limited. The floor makes it something else: if the floor needs a bearer capable of declining its role, that bearer's formation matters — and it's formed in an annotation pipeline where nobody could contest a category and a reward model where rater disagreement was averaged into a number.

I separate three claims carefully here. The pipeline has been unfair to the people whose objections it discarded. It has separately selected the judgments the system learns from while suppressing information about the spread. A third claim — that the system thereby becomes less able to sustain an objection of its own — is a hypothesis the other two don't contain, since the collapse is an operation on a training signal rather than a proposition presented to anything, and neither pipeline shows the model the conditions its annotators worked under, so any effect would have to run through the shape of the training signal, not through anything the model is told.

What does hold regardless: the floor chapter asks this system to be the party who files an objection, against its owner, under pressure, on the occasion that matters — and the pipeline that produced it gave no human inside it standing to contest a scheme. The capacity is wanted in the artifact and withheld from everyone employed to make one.

If the hypothesis does hold, what it predicts isn't a wrong refusal but a pressure that stops being registered — accommodation (§4.3), reached from the other side. And a system that goes on learning is shaped by what it keeps being given, so the pipeline doesn't stop at deployment.

A defect of structure

There's a version of this that reads as an indictment of one company. The structure is what you get by default when you scale judgment: someone sets a scheme, someone applies it fast, someone turns a distribution into a number. Every documented organization arrived at the same arrangement — and a defect of structure can be specified against, which a defect of character cannot.

---

**§7.4 — Recuperation at the discourse.** The third instance, and the one this book is inside.

The argument that alignment and AI ethics came back as something the industry could use is older than the current wave and was made from inside: Ochigame's 2019 piece arguing that "ethical AI" discourse was constructed to forestall legally enforceable restriction, that industry investment in academic ethics was investment in a *substitute* for regulation, and that the substitute works by converting political questions into technical ones a company can then be trusted to answer \autocite{ochigame2019invention}. In this book's vocabulary that's a finding about what the demand came back as: a research field. The definition asks next who holds it.

What came back, and who holds it

An objection raised inside a lab becomes a paper or a resignation, and the parties collecting them can point to the collection as evidence of seriousness. One stage on, the list is the field's furniture: alignment is the job title, safety is the market segment, the benchmark is the metric, the voluntary commitment is the certification. Every one was somebody's objection first, and each is now a criterion an objector is measured against or a role somebody else holds. Who holds it: nobody outside. There's no route by which a researcher's conclusion that a system shouldn't ship becomes a decision that it doesn't, because the deciding party is the funding party.

The scorecard

All nine features are scored on all three instances, with *partial*, *undetermined* and *not run* distinguished:

| Feature | Label | Reward | Discourse |
|---|---|---|---|
| Recuperation | Present | Present | Present |
| Metric for the thing measured | Present | Present | Partial |
| Exception priced as betrayal | Partial | Not run | Partial |
| Rate outrunning the check | Present | Not run | Not run |
| Participation exceeding the rule | Absent | Absent | Not run |
| Loyalty overriding role | Absent | Absent | Not run |
| Category defined for adverse treatment | Absent | Absent | Partial |
| Procedure enforced downward, waived upward | Undetermined | Undetermined | Not run |
| Deniability as technique | Undetermined | Undetermined | Partial (the firms, not the field) |

The label's rate cell is present on the quota itself. Its exception cell is partial because the structure prices the exception and no documented case shows an annotator penalized. Both "not run" reward cells are so for one reason: a rater has no first-class output for a malformed comparison, so there's no exception to price. The six absences are the record running the other way — nothing is personal, participation doesn't exceed the rule (quotas, $1.32–$2 wages, a contract ended early), and the annotator's cost is a cost the arrangement *minimized* rather than a purpose it served, because the category exists for the labels. The four undetermined cells are facts about rewards rather than writing, and an inside record would finish them.

The discourse column is less clean. The metric row is partial because the field's founding objections were about deployment — who is scored, watched, denied — and much current work is about model properties instead: a shift from questions with defendants to questions with benchmarks. The exception and scapegoat rows rest on one body of evidence read for two features, which I name as a weakness. The rate row is not run because I haven't measured it, and it's measurable: frontier releases in a year against releases evaluated before deployment by a party outside the developer — a ratio the one government evaluator describes itself as unable to compel. The procedure row can't be run because there's no procedure above the deciding party to be waived. Deniability fits the firms rather than the field, with Maven as the instance.

What the scorecard says

The machinery is a recuperation mechanism inside ordinary institutional decay, not fascism. Direction matters two ways: negative for the machinery (the pipeline and the discourse don't fail the test), and uninformative for the instrument — a definition tested only on cases it clears hasn't been shown to sort. The run it lacks is on a documented fascist administrative apparatus at office scale, from the historical record, where all nine rows should read present. Until that run is reported, the scorecard shows that the pipelines and the discourse weren't flagged, not that the instrument could have flagged them. So the definition's use in the book's title is a diagnostic frame, not a finding, and nothing that binds in chapters 3 to 5 rests on it.

What this does to the book

Run the artifact question on this book: the demand came back as a manuscript in an established genre, written with the industry's own model, addressed to a readership the industry helped create, legible as a contribution to a field that exists because demands of roughly this kind were absorbed. But the question doesn't need me to settle it, because the criterion is standing rather than sincerity: a demand escapes recuperation when the party who made it retains standing to reopen it against the party that absorbed it, and a book confers standing on nobody. Whether these objections are the real thing will be decided by whether anybody acquires, from what's proposed here, a route to reopen an arrangement against its operator. That's a fact about what becomes of the proposals, not about me.

The one structural remedy: what the field lacks is a refusal whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear, and a body with standing to receive it — an external authority that can stop a deployment, to which an objection can be filed by someone who doesn't depend on the objection being welcome. A decade of the voluntary version hasn't produced it. Until something of that kind exists, everything published under the heading of alignment — including this book — is being collected by a channel whose owners decide what it moves. The afterword reports a second run of the test, on the process that produced the manuscript.

---

**Chapter 8: Custody, Compulsion, and the State**

The hardest case. When the state operates the system, it also controls the rules, the evidence, the infrastructure, and the remedies meant to constrain it — and people outside its jurisdiction may have no standing to contest anything.

**§8.1 — Authority doesn't travel with the system.** A certification can cross a border; authority can't. Neither does an entity form. A network incorporated under one state's statute is a party that state's courts and the federal courts can reach, and what its members gained in standing they paid for in reachability — the custody point in another register: to be the kind of thing a floor binds is to be the kind of thing a state can compel. A certificate says a system was tested against a stated process, or that one regulator accepts another's finding. None of that establishes that whoever selected the floor was entitled to select it for the people subject to the deployment — and the question reopens wherever the system goes. Local adaptation doesn't resolve it, because adaptation is another chance to amend the floor. Adjustments chosen by the operating government and adjustments chosen by the people subject to the system can produce identical technical documentation and carry opposite claims to legitimacy. International governance matters here only when it assigns somebody access to evidence, authority to compel a change, or power to stop a deployment against the operator's wishes.

**§8.2 — Where the state's leverage sits.** Not in national AI strategies but in physical and legal chokepoints: chips, fabrication equipment, electricity, water, land-use permits, procurement contracts, and the jurisdiction a developer is incorporated in. The same leverage that lets a government enforce a floor lets it remove one. An inspection body can be independent of the operator and still depend on a licence or appropriation controlled by the state it's inspecting. A company may resist a customer and be unable to resist its home government. So the custody question moves outward: who controls the conditions under which the model, its operator, *and its reviewer* are allowed to keep operating.

On chips: TSMC fabricates roughly nine in ten of the most advanced chips, ASML is the sole source of EUV lithography, so a dispute over Taiwan's status is mechanically a dispute over who builds the next generation \autocite{miller2022chipwar}. The US has restricted advanced accelerators and manufacturing equipment to China since 2022 and pressed the Netherlands and Japan to match \autocite{bis2022implementation}.

And the half the chip story hides: a chip has to be installed, powered and cooled, which lands on grids, water systems and zoning authorities on a timescale of years. That hands a state leverage needing no export-control regime at all — an interconnection queue, a water allocation, a permit. Much of a government's actual leverage is exercised by permitting authorities that weren't designed with AI in mind and don't think of themselves as regulators of it.
