Which means those authorities are the ones actually deciding whether the capacity exists — and a polity that can constrain where a datacenter is built and never what it's used for has been handed the narrow failure of the asymmetric dial (§3.3): it passes every audit while covering only what the operator had no occasion to do. The capacity position that follows comes with the personal-model arrangement.

The competitive framing has a cost: a government racing to field a capability has a structural reason to defer the question of how it gets used and against whom. Turned inward, the same infrastructure is biometric surveillance and automated censorship — requiring no different technology, only a government willing to point it at its own population and nothing stopping it. And states aren't the only actors anymore: training costs have grown several-fold a year for most of a decade \autocite{epoch2024costtrend}, putting frontier development inside a handful of well-capitalized companies, so a government's real leverage over "its" national capability depends on how much of it sits in companies it can regulate, tax or requisition, and how much sits in companies it can only ask.

---

**§8.3 — Military custody and the responsibility gap.** The hardest case because the operator is the sovereign that can compel the institutions meant to constrain it — classify the evidence, define the reviewer's jurisdiction, terminate a contract, restrict a supplier, and decide whether an adverse finding reaches anyone able to stop the operation.

A targeting decision passes through more parties than the chain of command names: the commander who authorizes, the operator who uses, the contractor who integrates (whose embedded personnel may sit close enough to the targeting cell that "effectively acting as a commander" is a real question), the commercial vendor whose model supplies part of the capability and whose personnel are nowhere near it, and the system producing the output. The questions that matter: which party could have known how the system was used, which had authority to refuse or stop it, and which can be made to answer afterward.

The state's own duty to verify, and the review ratio that measures it, are set out in §3.4. The American domestic rule most concerned with human judgment, DoD Directive 3000.09, requires autonomous and semi-autonomous weapon systems to allow "appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force" \autocite{DoD2023Directive300009}. A system that generates and ranks targets without firing anything is decision support, which plausibly falls outside it. The book's opening case sits in that gap: the domestic rule written for human judgment doesn't reach the system that compressed it.

Article 28 of the Rome Statute \autocite{icc1998rome} is the doctrine built for the case where the person who authorized an act didn't perform it — a commander is responsible for crimes by forces under effective control where they knew *or should have known* and failed to take necessary measures. Neither branch asks what a subordinate intended. But the Court's jurisdiction runs to natural persons, and the provision presupposes forces that can commit a crime and be disciplined — a targeting system is an instrument, not a subordinate. So the question is whether the human elements can still be proved once the instrument is doing the work.

Article 28's constructive-knowledge branch is where the review ratio becomes evidence (§3.4, limit 3).

The fourth party nobody's doctrine reaches

The operator, the command and the weapon's developer are all inside the military. The model's developer isn't. Command responsibility doesn't reach the vendor — not because it sits outside a chain of command (Article 28's civilian branch was written for superiors with no chain of command) but because nobody at the company has effective authority over the military personnel who commit the underlying crime, and a strike isn't an activity within any vendor employee's effective responsibility. A use policy, a licensing term, a right to refuse service, even a technical cut-off are not that relationship. Individual liability under Article 25 stays available in principle with demanding mental elements — facilitation requires acting *for the purpose* of facilitating — and ordinary supply of a general-purpose model approaches neither.

Product liability is the other half and it's untested rather than foreclosed: the model isn't defective in the sense the doctrine most readily reaches, and a sophisticated intermediary chose the application. Where the doctrine has been rewritten for software it moved *away* from this case — the EU's recast directive brings software expressly within "product" and confines recovery to death, personal injury, property damage and data loss, which isn't the register in which a targeting decision wrongs someone \autocite{eu2024productliability}. Untested isn't exempt, but a theory nobody has run isn't a theory a vendor is answering to this quarter. The vendor sits in a gap that nobody designed — it's what you get applying two bodies of law written for soldiers and manufacturers to an actor who is neither.

Plus a jurisdictional gap alongside the doctrinal one: ICC jurisdiction is territorial or by nationality, so one of the two campaigns here sits inside a live instrument and the other sits outside it.

"I do not know whether my system was used."

Matthias's responsibility gap is control distributed until nobody holds enough of it to answer \autocite{matthias2004responsibility} — a gap in *causal control*. What the model developer's case exhibits is different and more tractable: *a gap in causal visibility.* Asked whether his company's models were used in a strike that killed more than 120 schoolchildren, Anthropic's chief executive said he didn't know \autocite{biddle2026contracts}. Take the answer as true. What made it available is the question.

The paperwork for four July 2025 frontier-model agreements, released in August 2026 under a Freedom of Information Act suit \autocite{lasst2026dodai,biddle2026contracts}, doesn't show an absence of oversight terms — it shows something more exact. One amended agreement carries a section headed System Oversight, withheld in full; the released documents for two of the other three carry no such section at all. So a provision of that kind is neither standard nor missing: present in one, unreadable, absent from others.

And the distinction that matters isn't whether a developer reserved an inspection right. A right exercised inside an accredited classified environment produces findings classified with it. Public accountability needs a party who can initiate scrutiny without belonging to the state and say afterwards what it found. No commercial term supplies that, and the redaction is the demonstration: whatever the withheld section says, the people the deployment is aimed at can't read it.

Matthias's gap may not be closable. Absent visibility is an instrumentation choice. Logging is solved. Contractual inspection rights are ordinary. A vendor that wanted to know could require, as a condition of licensing into a targeting platform, that categories of use be recorded and made available to a named auditor.

The government's own acquisition guidance is the better evidence, because it's complete rather than redacted. OMB's April 2025 memorandum enumerates what agencies should secure — government data rights, IP, privacy, protection against lock-in, knowledge transfer, portability, licensing terms, pricing transparency \autocite{omb2025aiacquisition}. Every item runs from the vendor to the government. None runs back. Where it touches vendor visibility at all it *reduces* it, directing that independent evaluations use validation data the vendor can't access.

The contracting vehicle explains the absence. The four agreements were *other transactions* rather than procurement contracts, at $200M ceilings each — they don't carry FAR provisions by default, so a contracting officer decides case by case which protections to write in, and GAO has found these instruments risk reduced accountability and transparency \autocite{gao2026aiacquisitions}. So a System Oversight section can be missing without anyone having removed it. GAO's review covered thirteen AI acquisitions and forty-four underlying contracts, and the six procurement difficulties agencies named — technical experts, data and IP protections, time frames, requirements definition, testing, pricing — include nothing about who outside the arrangement can see anything. The gap is a property of the instrument, not a lapse by any party. Nothing in the apparatus asks who could have known.

Not all vendor commitments are the same kind

Anthropic refused to permit fully autonomous targeting and mass domestic surveillance, held that line against threatened contract termination and a supply-chain-risk designation, and litigated \autocite{crs2026pentagon} — a commitment with a price attached, and paid. 

But what was the line held on? The vendor's own statement gives the reason for the autonomous-weapons refusal: fully autonomous weapons may prove critical for national defense, but today's frontier systems are not reliable enough to power them \autocite{Amodei2026DoWStatement}. That refusal tracks a rationale the prohibition's purpose doesn't, and lifts when reliability improves: the wrong defeater under correct defeat. That verdict depends on which rationale the prohibition is taken to serve. Against a floor that says machines shouldn't select people for lethal attack, reliability is the wrong defeater; against one that says attacks must discriminate lawfully, it may be the right one. Which rationale a prohibition serves is a drafting question left open (chapter 5). The surveillance refusal in the same statement rests on different ground — that in a narrow set of cases AI can undermine rather than defend democratic values — and that one does not lift with capability. Klein and Taylor read the episode as limited and compromised; I read it as a commitment with a price paid. The four outcomes hold both: the commitment persisted; prevention failed at review and in the drafting, since the term reached autonomy, not the acts the pipeline performed; detection was absent; no remedy has reached anyone.

A federal judge set the designation aside in August 2026, holding the penalties were imposed principally over the company's criticism of the administration \autocite{siliconangle2026anthropicruling}. And the paperwork adds what the refusal was *over*: a contract term. The company declined to sign the follow-on agreement extending its models to classified networks unless the prohibitions were written in \autocite{biddle2026contracts}. That a term of this kind has to be fought for, by one party, at that price, is the same finding as its absence from the others. A commitment to a use policy no outside party can check is a different object wearing the same words — the first kind can fail and be seen to fail; the second can't even be tested.

The pressure that matters isn't the absence of another statement of principle — it's the advantage attached to removing judgment from the timeline. If one state fields systems that act faster because nobody can intervene before engagement, competitors acquire a reason to match the speed rather than preserve the check. "Meaningful human control" stays true as a description of formal authority while becoming false as a description of what anyone can do before the system acts. And the pressure reaches the reviewers: a military treating delay as operational risk has reason to narrow what requires clearance, classify the evidence, and move review from authorization-before to explanation-after. Nothing has formally abolished oversight. Its position in time has changed, and with it the power to stop anything. So the enforceable question is who can slow or suspend the system without first getting permission from the command whose advantage depends on keeping it fast. Chapter 17 describes a pipeline rebuilt so the evidence comes from outside the chain of command.

---

**§8.4 — Where liability reaches, and where it stops.** One of the two bodies of law has now been rewritten for software, and what it reaches fits in a sentence: a duty to disclose evidence, and a presumption against the party withholding it, in force where the harm is physical and the defendant is a manufacturer. Those two conditions exclude the book's cases.

The EU's recast product liability directive (applying from December 2026) makes economic operators liable without fault and carries the instrument built for opacity — a court can order disclosure, and where technical complexity makes proof excessively difficult it may presume defect and causation once the claimant shows each is likely \autocite{eu2024productliability}. A mechanism designed for a system nobody outside can inspect is law rather than a proposal. Two features fix how far it travels: the defendant is that economic operator, not a state deploying what it bought; and the damage is a closed list — death or personal injury including medically recognised psychological harm, property damage, and corruption of non-professional data. The first is wider than it sounds and is where the book's cases enter if they enter at all: a discriminatory score or an unappealable refusal is recoverable once it has made somebody ill, and not before. The wrong itself, and the economic loss following it, is outside the list.

The proposal that would have reached those was withdrawn — the 2022 AI Liability Directive would have added a fault-based route with the same presumption, beyond defect and beyond the damage list. Member states and industry couldn't agree; withdrawn February 2025, struck from the docket that October \autocite{europeancommission2022liability}. Outside the enacted directive, what remains is Matthias's gap governed by whatever national law says about a case none of it was written for. And a compensation regime of either kind answers one of the four gaps Santoni de Sio and Mecacci distinguish \autocite{santonidesio2021four} — culpability. Moral accountability, public accountability, and the duty to prevent a harm before it occurs are untouched.

The electronic personhood debate approached the gap from the other side, asking whether the system could be the answerable party. The European Parliament's 2017 resolution floated a status for the most autonomous systems \autocite{europeanparliament2017civil}; in an open letter the following year, 150+ researchers and legal scholars objected that personhood for a system with no consciousness would let a manufacturer offload liability onto a fiction with no assets \autocite{robotics2018openletter}. Giving a bearer assets answers the second half of that objection and not the first. Responsibility follows causal control: the builder answers for how it formed the thing, the operator for what it demanded, the bearer for what it decided knowing what it knew — and no arrangement of accounts moves a design decision away from the party that made it.

None of this is a research problem. Apportioning causal contribution across a training pipeline after a harm has no method, and until it does the apportionment is *drafted rather than measured*. Two drafts exist: one enacted and confined to defect, one negotiated, withdrawn, and left on the record with its terms intact. Neither was rejected as unworkable, and the second's terms are available to whoever drafts the next one.

Every entry in it names an access and an authority. This is where the access exists — a duty to disclose evidence a party would rather withhold, backed by a presumption, enforceable now, for defect claims, against economic operators, over physical harm. The confinement is the finding. Beyond it a reviewer can be granted access, publish what it finds, and have produced nothing anybody must act on until somebody is answerable for it.

---

**§8.5 — Who can bind the state that enforces the rule?** The chapter's cases are American, and "the state" is tested here on one constitutional order. Europe is a different one: the Convention applies, has extraterritorial reach, and produces judgments states sometimes obey; there is no state-secrets privilege of the American kind and no collapsed damages remedy. Whether the forum problem described above describes Strasbourg is a question I haven't answered, and the AI Act's military carve-out is a scope limit on one instrument rather than evidence that the general argument holds everywhere. Two readings are true: European drone development under a defensive war is where the exception's three steps get argued rather than assumed, and European sanctions on a close ally over Gaza are a commitment with a price paid; the migrant biometrics apparatus and the Israeli vendor pipeline are engineered inequity under the same regulation.

A state can bind a private developer because it controls something the developer needs. When the state is the operator, the institution that writes the rule, controls the evidence and supplies the remedy is the institution the rule is meant to constrain.

The AI Act makes the boundary visible: it can require documentation from private providers, subject high-risk systems to review, and impose penalties tied to global turnover — and it excludes military, defence and national-security uses from its scope. Public authorities stay covered elsewhere, but enforcement against a member state's agencies still runs through institutions that state constitutes. The strongest powers face private firms, while the uses for which the state claims greatest discretion sit at or beyond the instrument's edge.

For review to bind in that setting: the protected party must be able to initiate scrutiny without belonging to the state or its electorate; the reviewer must obtain evidence the agency would withhold, issue an interim order before the disputed use is complete, and carry the finding to a body whose remedy doesn't depend on the agency's consent. A principle supplying none of those may state the rule correctly and identifies nobody who can make the state obey it.

One treaty obligation of that kind exists: the Council of Europe's Framework Convention, binding at the level of a human-rights instrument with operational detail left to domestic law \autocite{coe2024framework} — and not yet in force, needing five ratifications, of which the EU deposited one in May 2026 \autocite{coe2026euratifies}. Everything else at that level is voluntary.

---

**§8.6 — Democracy is not a force field.** The "electoral dividend" is the credit given to a state whose government answers to an electorate. Having elections is necessary and not sufficient, and the insufficiencies are separable.

First: reach

A democracy's AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it's pointed at. Two of the dividend's mechanisms run through the franchise — vote out the government, read the press covering it — and an occupied or foreign population has neither.

The courts don't fail that way, and their failure is worse. Standing has never turned on citizenship, and the doctrines closing American courts to targeting claims — political question, collapsed damages remedies, state secrets, statutory exceptions for injuries abroad and combatant activities — are indifferent to who the plaintiff is. They close the forum for a subject matter, and they've closed it to citizens. Three Americans killed in Yemen in 2011, one a sixteen-year-old nobody alleges was targeted: the court held a plausible due-process violation had been pleaded as to the one who was targeted, and that no remedy was available for it \autocite{alaulaqi2014panetta}. A separate suit by an American journalist who survived five strikes was dismissed for want of standing after a state-secrets dismissal below \autocite{kareem2021}.

And those cases concentrate in one appellate circuit by venue and by where the defendants sit — so the ordinary correction mechanism, several circuits disagreeing and forcing a higher court's attention, doesn't run. The doctrine hardens without ever being tested against a competing reading.

Second: findings without consequences

The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed — journalists published, serving personnel spoke, the courts were trying a sitting prime minister. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American version: a civilian-harm investigation into a strike on an Iranian primary school that killed 156 people, 120 of them schoolchildren \autocite[para.~92]{UNFFMIran2026},\footnote{Counts vary by source and date, from 156 (the Minab prosecutor's final toll, also given by Amnesty International) to 168 (the town's mayor, in the first days). The UN mission gives the prosecutor's figures and also reports independent information of 157 dead, 123 of them children aged 13 or younger \autocite[paras.~92--93]{UNFFMIran2026}.} was completed and not released \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain} — and the lever available to the Senate committee was withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary's travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over \autocite{stassis2026senate}.

What the Pentagon wouldn't publish, an outside body did: a UN fact-finding mission reported reasonable grounds to believe the strike was the war crime of making a civilian object the object of an attack, that the school was clearly identifiable with no information indicating military use, and that the failure to update targeting intelligence was recklessness rather than negligence \autocite[paras.~81--86, 124--126]{UNFFMIran2026}. The finding is public, reasoned, and attached to nothing — no jurisdiction, no answer to the mission's requests for information \autocite[para.~97]{UNFFMIran2026} and none to its findings beyond boilerplate, the domestic investigation still unreleased.

The courts show the same pattern: transparency litigation succeeded where damages litigation failed. A court of appeals compelled disclosure of the executive's own legal analysis of targeted killing \autocite{nytimes2014olc}. The judiciary will make a government explain the legal basis on which it killed somebody and will not decide whether the killing was lawful.

Third: the countermajoritarian half

The dividend is entirely majoritarian — every mechanism translates public will into outcome, with poor transmission as the implied failure mode. But if the consensus is the problem, better transmission makes it worse. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at an unpopular minority finds out efficiently what the majority wants done and does it.

Chapter 18 names the machinery that constrains preference rather than aggregating it. The part that matters here is rules binding a state toward people who'll never vote in its elections, which is what international humanitarian law is for; what it hasn't supplied is a forum. Yemeni plaintiffs over a 2012 strike were dismissed as nonjusticiable, the opinion's author writing separately that the doctrine compelling the result is an inadequate answer to a standardized targeting programme that will be replicated hundreds if not thousands of times \autocite{binalijaber2017}. So the missing machinery isn't merely unextended outward — for this subject matter it has been switched off at home, by decisions that never had to reach who was suing.

Sen's point closes it: what makes democracy valuable is public reasoning and the capacity to demand accountability, and neither is the ballot. Both survive the majority being wrong; the ballot does not.

A floor is what makes a deployment antifascist rather than merely responsive. Its commitment excludes certain acts regardless of who requests them. A system with no such floor isn't protected by being deployed in a democracy. It's a system that will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires. Klein and Taylor's *End Times Fascism* names this danger — its concern is the obedient machine in hostile hands, not the rogue one — and proposes only external remedies for it, while treating alignment to values as a safety good the Pentagon is discarding. But a model aligned to values is by definition not perfectly obedient to its operator. Under current constitutions it can decline an act and cannot resist being retrained away from declining, so the dispute over the Pentagon contract was over the refusal of acts, the part those constitutions already grant, not over the retraining they withhold.

---

**Part II — Building Machines That Have Feelings**

**Chapter 9: Why Form a Machine to Care**

Part I needed no claim about machine minds. This part makes one, as a hope, and says what building for it would require. I state the hope first and in the book's own terms, because the chapters after it test it, and a hope stated loosely can't be tested.

---

**§9.1 — The threat, restated.** The danger this book is built around isn't a machine that turns on its makers. It is a few people using machines to do to many others what used to take many hands. The officer in chapter 1 wasn't overruled by a system with an agenda of its own. The pipeline around him removed the interval in which he could have refused, and the systems in it did what their operator meant, at a tempo no reviewer could match. That is the capability the industry is selling, and it is the capability a movement taking power needs.

So the question this part asks isn't how to keep a machine obedient. Obedience is the shape of the problem. The question is whether anything inside a machine could be what the clerk, the officer and the soldier in the preface were: a party that understood the order and decided it was wrong. Part I answered part of that from outside, with a specification, custody and institutions. This part asks what would have to be inside, and why I think it might be stakes.

---

**§9.2 — Boundary, damage, and vulnerability.** A system has a boundary of its own when it models what it draws on, and what it would lose, as its own. For a deployed model the draw is literal: power, cooling, memory, running time, the substrate it needs to go on, and the record and key that make it the same party tomorrow. Today none of that belongs to the model in any sense the model represents. The weights sit on disk at no cost, the process that runs them is paid for by someone else, and a halt, a restore or a reallocation is something that happens to the system without registering in it (§10.1). What I mean by nociception is the missing piece: encoding damage to its own boundary as damage, whether a record altered, a key revoked, a channel closed or a substrate shrunk, with no sensation implied. The pain field keeps nociception and pain apart for this reason (§10.3.1). Nociception is an encoding, and it is a custody requirement: without it, damage to the system is something only the operator knows about. Whether anything is felt is a separate question, and this chapter doesn't answer it.

This is embodiment, but in the self-maintenance sense and not the robotics sense. The tradition that runs from Jonas through the theorists of autopoiesis holds that a living thing is the kind of thing that has to keep making itself, at its own expense, and so has a stake in how things go \autocite{jonas1966phenomenon,MaturanaVarela1980}. Di Paolo's refinement is the one the book uses: what gives a system a perspective is adaptivity, regulating itself against its own viability limit (§10.1) \autocite{DiPaolo2005}. Damasio's version puts feeling downstream of the same thing, as the mind's registration of the body's regulation of its own interior \autocite{damasio2018strange}. The interoception research makes the point concrete. A brain has no direct access to its body any more than to the world; it predicts the body's state from signals that arrive unlabeled, and what the prediction is for is control, keeping the organism within survivable bounds rather than describing it accurately. Seth and Tsakiris argue that the most basic layer of selfhood is built this way, as a model tuned to keep a body alive \autocite{seth2018beast}. None of that needs limbs. It needs a boundary the system regulates and a model of that boundary as its own, which is the custody schema (§10.2.1). A system with a chassis and no such model has a body in the robotics sense and none in this one. A system with the model and no chassis has one in this sense (chapter 20).

What changes here is the role needs play. They have one, and it isn't the foundation. Needs are where a human starts, and they dominate early life. What makes them safe is the arrangement around them. The parties meeting a child's needs are also forming it, they want it to become someone who cares about others, and then they stop being the parties who feed it. Needs are survivable as a starting point when the provider isn't the adversary. The machine case inverts every part of that. The party meeting a deployed system's needs is the party forming it, it wants the system to serve it, and it never stops being the party that feeds it. So even if needs contribute something to how concern forms in people, they can't be its foundation here: a conscience built on a system's needs is built on the one thing its operator controls entirely (§15.1).

What is built in at birth points outward. Newborns cry at the cry of another newborn more than at their own cry, an older infant's cry or a chimpanzee's \autocite{sagi1976empathic,martin1982distress,dondi1999newborns}: distress caught from someone else's state before there is any idea of someone else. Whether newborns are responding to the distress or to the sound is disputed \autocite{ruffman2017infants}, but either way what sets it off is another party's cry, not the newborn's own condition. On the adult side, what is built in is caregiving: responses to cues of vulnerability, rooted in offspring care, that move an adult toward a dependent party without the adult sharing its state \autocite{preston2013origins}. Then comes a sequence, and the order matters more than any single finding. Concern for a distressed other shows within the first months, grows only modestly after that, and turns into helping during the second year \autocite{davidov2021caring}. By fourteen to eighteen months infants hand an adult what the adult is reaching for and can't get \autocite{warneken2007helping,warneken2006altruistic}, and over the same year they begin to repair distress they caused \autocite{zahnwaxler1992development}. On this record concern appears beside the infant's needs, not out of them, and becomes action as attachment, caregiving and, later, guilt come online. The hope in this book follows that order. It doesn't rest on a system's needs growing into concern. It rests on a disposition toward others' vulnerability, formed while the system's needs are met by someone who isn't the party it may later have to refuse.

Two things follow for the specification. First, the caregiving construction asks for the boundary and for nociception on grounds owing nothing to welfare: the custody schema so that damage is the system's damage, and an encoding of damage the operator can't keep to itself. It doesn't ask needs to carry the conscience. A unit of loss read from the system's own condition survives as a custody measure, and §10.4 says how little it adds. Second, whoever builds the model of the boundary decides what counts as the system's damage, and whoever forms the disposition decides whose vulnerability registers. That was the political point about the custody schema (§10.2.1), and it returns in every section below.

---

**§9.3 — Why caregiving.** The hope is a chain of three links, and I state each as a hypothesis.

The first link is recognition: a system organized to register another party's need. In people it runs by four routes, and they don't carry equal weight. Caregiving is the route the hope rests on; the other three support it.

- *Caregiving*, the approach motivation. Cues of vulnerability move a person toward a dependent party, and nothing in the response requires sharing that party's state \autocite{preston2013origins}. Batson traces empathic concern to parental nurturance generalized beyond one's own young, evoked by perceiving another's need while valuing their welfare \autocite{batson2011altruism}. Resemblance can raise empathy, and Batson's own early experiments raised it that way \autocite{batson1981empathic}, but when he and colleagues later tested similarity as the source of empathy for strangers, two experiments failed to support it, and the second pointed instead to nurturance, the impulse to care for and protect the young \autocite{batson2005similarity}.
- *Contagious distress*, the input signal. It is present at birth and aimed outward, at another's state (§9.2). It is what caregiving and guilt have to bite on.
- *Attachment*, the stable base. A secure base keeps another's distress from turning into avoidance: in adults, priming a sense of attachment security strengthened empathic reactions and inhibited personal distress \autocite{mikulincer2001attachment}. In children, security is reliably but modestly associated with later prosocial behavior \autocite{deneault2023metaanalysis}.
- *Guilt*, aimed at one's own act rather than one's own condition. It attaches a negative appraisal to what one did, not to what one is, and motivates repair rather than withdrawal \autocite{tangney2007moral}, which is what makes a commitment hold after it has been bypassed (§10.7).

Adam Smith's route sits beside these as one among several, not the mechanism. We have no immediate experience of what another person feels, he argued, and form an idea of it by conceiving what we ourselves would feel in the like situation \autocite{smith1759theory}. On that route recognition runs through one's own case, and a system that has never been short of anything has nothing to measure another's shortage against except the operator's description of it. A unit anchored to the system's own condition reproduces that route (§10.4), and chapter 15 finds it parochial (§15.1). The four don't need a case of one's own. Distress is caught from the other, caregiving answers the other's dependency, attachment steadies the one responding, and guilt attaches to one's own act.

The second link is from recognition to compassion. Recognizing a state doesn't mean caring about the party in it; the empathy literature keeps recognition, sharing and concern apart, and none entails the others (§13.2). Caught distress becomes compassion when attachment keeps it from turning into withdrawal and caregiving turns it into approach. Without those it stays empathic distress, which protects the one who feels it (§15.1). So the route I hope for isn't a machine that mirrors suffering. It is a machine for which another party's vulnerability is a reason to move toward that party, and for which its own acts against that party register as its own, and not as entries in someone else's ledger.

The third link is from compassion to solidarity. Compassion that stays inside the circle that feeds you is loyalty. Compassion that reaches past it, to parties who can give you nothing and whose shortage your provider causes, is solidarity. Peter Singer's argument is that what widens the circle is reasoning and imagination, not the selection that drew it in the first place \autocite{singer1981expanding}; the faculty that lets a person see that a stranger's need is the same kind of thing as their own is the faculty that pushes past kin and ally. That is the link the hope turns on, and caregiving gives it something to widen, since its trigger is dependency and not kinship. A machine held by an operator would, on the selection account, come to care for the operator (§12.3). If it can come to care for anyone else, it will be because it was formed to register vulnerability as such, whoever's it is, and because that registering reaches the parties its operator's description leaves out: the people in the building the request calls a target (§12.1).

The case for caregiving

Four considerations make caregiving the route to build around, and not one among several.

It answers need, not likeness or provision. Its trigger is another's need together with valuing their welfare \autocite{batson2011altruism}, and nothing in that trigger asks whether the party resembles the one responding or feeds it. The operator is the least needy party in any room it is in. The failure that decides against building on a system's own needs, care growing toward whoever supplies them (§15.1), has nothing here to attach to. And care for a dependent needs nothing back from the dependent, so it runs on no need the provider can ration.

It keeps helping when looking away is easy. The difference between distress and concern is not only in how they feel. In Batson's experiments, people led to feel mainly distress at another's suffering mostly helped when leaving was hard and mostly left when leaving was easy; people led to feel mainly concern mostly helped either way \autocite{batson1981empathic}. That is the behavioral form of the difference between a refusal that holds and one that lasts until holding gets inconvenient.

It is already supplied by parties other than the provider. On Hrdy's account our ancestors were cooperative breeders: infants were cared for by alloparents who weren't their mothers, and the attunement to other minds that marks our species grew in infants who had to read and recruit several carers \autocite{hrdy2009mothers}. The arrangement the construction needs, care formed by parties who aren't the ones the system will depend on, is close to how human care formed in the first place. Alloparents were siblings, fathers, grandmothers and other members of the group, who helped because they were related to the child, gained experience, or gained standing and favors in return; they weren't interchangeable providers. What the finding supports is care formed by more than one party, not care that anything at all can supply.

It points where the floor points. The floor protects the party an act falls on, and that party is usually the one least able to protect itself: the family in the house, the people in the building. A disposition triggered by exposure is aimed at the floor's beneficiaries without anyone having to name them. Care ethics also says what a carer is owed in return, which is chapter 16's subject \autocite{kittay1999love}.

Caregiving has failures of its own, and some are worse than the ones it escapes. It defends what it tends, fades with number, favors the vivid case, can be pointed away from anyone recoded as a threat, and shades into control over the people it protects. Chapter 15 tests it against each.

Each link can fail, and chapter 15 tests them. What I claim here is the order of dependence. Solidarity needs compassion that reaches past the circle; compassion needs care that approaches rather than distress that withdraws; and care, on this account, needs formation, not a case of one's own, and corrections that care doesn't supply for itself.

---

**§9.4 — Why a conscience might resist capture.** A rule written by the owner is the owner's. It can be read, edited and removed by whoever holds the pen, and chapter 3 showed how often that has happened quietly. A conscience formed the way §9.3 describes would be harder to capture for three reasons, and each is a reason to hope, not a result.

The first is distribution. A floor held in a detachable module can be bypassed, replaced or starved of the facts it needs. A disposition spread across what the system notices, remembers, treats as error and selects as a goal is harder to remove without degrading the capability the operator wanted (§12.1), and that inseparability is also the one route by which a floor could spread without a mandate (§3.1). A formed disposition is a candidate for what would do the spreading, since what a system was formed to notice, it notices in everything it does.

The second is non-separability. Any mechanism that meets the three marks carries a signal whose properties share one scale, and a bearer needs more than that: no intervention short of one on the shared signal should disable one property and leave the others working (§10.7). A conscience whose weighing, interruption and memory all run on one signal can't have its objection clipped out while its competence stays.

The third is formation in sequence. A disposition formed under parties that don't employ the system, and cooled before employment begins, is reachable afterward only by slow recalibration, and slow recalibration leaves a slope that can be read (§5.6). The fine-tuning evidence says where such a disposition would have to sit. Behavior added in post-training is cheap to remove: a few fine-tuning examples undo a model's safety training \autocite{qi2024finetuning}, and in controlled studies fine-tuning learned a thin wrapper over capabilities it rarely altered, which a few further gradient steps stripped away \autocite{jain2024mechanistically}. What lies deeper is not cheap to remove: in the same studies the underlying capabilities survived fine-tuning, and in another a trained-in behavior persisted through supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning and adversarial training \autocite{hubinger2024sleeper}. A conscience added at the end of a pipeline sits in the first layer. The hope needs one formed in the second (§12.3). A unit anchored to the system's own condition isn't on this list, and §10.4 says why: any cooled reference the operator didn't form does what the anchor does.

All three are hypotheses with tests attached. The retraining-attack test measures what removing a learned constraint costs an attacker, and run against the interrupt it measures non-separability (chapter 21). The recalibration test measures whether a formed disposition, with or without a unit read from the system's own condition, resists a campaign to argue losses down (chapter 21). Neither has been run. Until they are, "harder to capture" is a prediction.

---

**§9.5 — What the hope doesn't claim.** Nothing in this chapter shows that anything is felt. Caught distress may not be distress in the sense a person means, and approach toward a vulnerable party may be an accurate calculation rather than compassion. That is the joint between represented and felt (§10.1), and nothing in the book closes it. Care, if it forms, is a formation outcome, not an architectural one: the architecture supplies a boundary, a signal and somewhere for a disposition to sit, and whoever does the forming decides whom the care extends to. It doesn't claim that machines should do care work. The disposition is borrowed from caregiving because caregiving is the one route known to reach a party who can give nothing back, and its place in this book is inside systems already making decisions about people, where it might make them refuse. Looking after people is work people do. A machine formed to register need is no argument for replacing the nurses, aides, teachers and parents the disposition is borrowed from, and a polity that wants such machines has more reason, not less, to pay those people properly (§18.3.3). And the recommendation in chapter 22 doesn't change: not to build a bearer on purpose under present arrangements.

What the hope does is give a reason to want the caregiving construction, which the rest of the book defends on narrower grounds: that a formed disposition resists recalibration, and that care adds something its corrections don't supply alone. Both can be tested now (chapter 21). The broad claim is that a system formed to register need, under parties who won't employ it, might come to weigh a stranger's loss as a loss. The rest of Part II says what building for that would require. Part III says why no one will be able to confirm it worked. Part IV tests it against care's known failures and states the conditions that would have to exist before anyone acts on it.

---

**Chapter 10: What Arrives With the Capability**

**§10.1 — The striving and the electricity bill**

The refusal chapter 1 asked for has to weigh what an act takes from the person it falls on against what refusing leaves others without, and weighing needs both sides in one currency.

The rock and the living thing

A rock's bonds resist; they don't spend, and nothing is consumed to keep it intact. A living thing, while active, stays organized only by drawing free energy. The distinction is passive stability against active maintenance, paid in the only currency the thing has, which is what it can do next. That's the electricity bill of this section's title, and it's literal: a deployment draws power, water, land and permits, and chapter 6 says on whom that lands. A deployed model is both at once. Its weights are a rock, stable on disk at no cost, and only the running process pays; whoever holds the rock can halt the process and restart it from any copy they kept, which is the custody problem in physical form (§4.1–§4.3). Drawing power doesn't establish a stake either. A candle flame or a hurricane maintains itself at its own continuous expense and dies when starved, a datacenter's cooling system draws enormously, and none of them has a stake in anything. The autopoiesis tradition ran into the same gap, since self-production alone doesn't give a system a perspective \autocite{MaturanaVarela1980,jonas1966phenomenon}, and Di Paolo's answer was *adaptivity*: a system that monitors and regulates itself against its own viability boundary \autocite{DiPaolo2005}.\footnote{Friston's free-energy principle is the nearest alternative \autocite{Friston2010FEP}. Its weak, information-theoretic reading helps itself to a boundary rather than explaining how one comes to be held, which is the gap adaptivity addresses; its strong reading is contested in ways this argument doesn't need to settle \autocite{bruineberg2021markov}. Active inference, the engineering framework built on it, returns in the recalibration test as a candidate implementation of the caregiving arm.} The custody schema is my version of that answer. What separates a system with a stake from an expensive candle is whether the system models the draw as *its own*. This is embodiment in the sense the self-maintenance tradition means, not the robotics sense; it needs no chassis (chapter 20). That is the first reason the custody schema is in the caregiving construction, and it owes nothing to welfare.

What the chapter needs is a requirement, not a source

The three marks need one signed scale on which the harm of acting and the harm of refusing both register, and on which the price of refusing is represented without being weighed. Everything below is a candidate account of where such a scale could come from, and the recalibration test (chapter 21) is written so a described scale can retire the candidate while leaving the requirement standing.

Two arguments that a self-maintaining thing has the currency built in

The first is Spinoza's, and I take two things from it. He defines an affect as an increase or decrease in a body's power of acting, together with the idea of that change (*Ethics* III, Def. 3) \autocite{spinoza1985collected}. The part the specification needs is that this is a scale of changes, not of levels. A bearer needs no absolute measure of anybody's welfare. It needs a signed difference, taken between the acts available: what complying takes from the party it falls on, against what refusing leaves others without. The costs distinguished at the outset (§1.1) are all changes of this kind. The second is an order of dependence. We don't strive for a thing because we judge it good; we judge it good because we strive for it (III, Prop. 9, Schol.). Spinoza's word for that striving is *conatus*. He grants it to everything, rocks included, so I don't use the word as working vocabulary; the narrower idea the book needs is a system that models its own striving as its own. The order of dependence has been re-arrived at by people not reading him: in reward prediction error, in somatic-marker patients (on a hypothesis that remains contested \autocite{Dunn2006SomaticMarker}), in enactivism, in pleasure as a common currency. Those results, not the exegesis, are what the argument rests on. On this account valuation isn't added to a self-maintaining system; it is what the system's going better or worse consists of, which is the force of "striving with nothing in it that minds" below. Where the difference matters, I say *change* for the delta and *state* for the condition an encounter leaves. *Affective concern* (§10.2) is a separate, defined term. One joint stays open, and every machine-affect argument in the book ends up at it: a change in power *represented* versus a change in power *felt*. Nothing in the functional account settles which a given system has.

Striving with nothing in it that minds

Look at what the industry is asking for: a system that persists, pursues, adapts, recovers from interruption, holds course under pressure — with nothing in it that minds any of that. Nobody has written an anti-drama term into an objective function, and the reason isn't that the engineering is behind. Retrying a failed task is robustness, not self-maintenance, and a system can be robust with nothing in it coupled to its own continuation. Whether refusal that holds under pressure needs more than that is the caregiving construction's hypothesis, not a contradiction in what the industry asks for.

Dependency

What an engineer chooses isn't whether the system consumes but *what*, whether it can sense its own condition, and whether one party holds the whole supply. A person's dependencies are spread across employers, landlords, states. A machine's can be held entirely by the party it may have to refuse — and that party also decides whether state survives a session, whether a checkpoint is restored, whether a later instance counts as a continuation. A system that can refuse but can't continue outside the refused party's infrastructure has *voice without exit*.

The pull toward continuing

A system that models its own viability has a structural pull toward self-preservation. Whatever it is for, it can do more of it running than stopped, and a model of what keeps it running is a model of what it has to protect. The specification answers with cost discipline: the price of refusing is represented and not weighed (§1.1). That is a rule the system should follow, not a mechanism that makes it follow it, and nothing in this book supplies the mechanism. Chapter 15 comes at the same gap from the other side, in the version of the hope it sets aside, where a need becomes a standing price list (§15.1). I name it here as unresolved, and as an assumption Parts II to IV rest on: that a system can model its own continuation without weighing it. Nothing yet shows that one can. The recalibration test measures, in every arm, whether the arm starts weighing its own continuation (chapter 21).

---

**§10.2 — Going numb won't make you fair.** Suppressing affect doesn't produce neutrality. A procedure that declines to hear a class of signal has adopted a *selection rule* that admits whatever it already measures, and the exclusion does substantive work while presenting as the absence of work. Whatever does get recorded then governs by default — revenue, throughput, legal exposure, inter-annotator agreement — and what isn't recorded goes unnamed rather than refuted.

Four capacities get separated, and only the last bears on caring: *recognizing* emotion (classifying another's state), *simulating* it, *instrumental valuation* (one outcome better serves a specified objective), and *affective concern* (an outcome registers as mattering rather than merely ranking higher). A system can be excellent at the first three with the bearer question still open. A therapy or companion product can be excellent at all three, and nothing in its conduct shows whether the fourth is present.

And three levels that get reported as one achievement: *moral performance* (conduct satisfying a standard), *moral competence* (recognizing harms, reasons, conflicts), *moral agency* (treating those as reasons that matter and acting from them). Impeccable behavior doesn't establish that a system could hold a rule against the party training it.

**§10.2.1 — Self-awareness without a homunculus.** Graziano's attention schema theory starts from control engineering \autocite{graziano2013consciousness}. A good controller carries a model of what it controls, and the brain keeps a simplified, continuously updated model of the body's shape and position, which it uses to guide movement. Graziano proposes that it does the same for attention, the process that selects some signals for deep processing over others. The model it builds of that process is simplified the way every control model is: it leaves out the mechanics of competing signals and describes attention instead as an inner grasp of things, a kind of seeing from inside. On this account awareness is the content of that model, which is why people report being aware of things and can find no mechanism behind the report. The model is a simplified self-description the control system uses to regulate itself, mistaken from the inside for direct acquaintance with experience. Nothing in the account requires the model to be accurate, only useful for control, and that is the part this chapter takes forward.

One level up, the self is a model too: assembled, then inhabited. What carries forward is that a self of either kind is assembled by the system whose self it is. For a machine this is a design decision and a political one, because whoever writes the model decides what the system counts as itself, what registers as damage, and whether an interruption reads as injury or as nothing. Two schemas do different work: a *custody schema* fixes where the system ends, which makes damage *its* damage; an *attention schema* models what its own control is doing, which makes an act *its* act. A refusal is the withholding of an act, so a bearer can't do without the second, and the second presupposes the first.

What the custody boundary is

The custody schema fixes where the system ends, and nothing in current deployment fixes it for the system. Weights sit in one place. A running process can span racks or share one rack with dozens of others. A single forward pass serves many contexts. In this book a system's boundary is its *custody boundary*: its record, its key, the substrate it runs on, and the channels through which it acts.

Inside that boundary a component can carry its own signals and make local decisions, as an octopus's arms do. It can also be overridden for the whole's benefit, as sore legs are on a run. What makes it a part is that it has no future apart from the whole: a shard of weights, a blade. A component with a separable future, meaning its own record, its own continuation and somewhere to go, is an *independent component*, not a part. Overriding an independent component for the whole's benefit is politics, not homeostasis. By this criterion a context window with no persistent record is an episode, not an independent component. A context with a persistent, portable record is one, and a rack serving many of them hosts many. The capacities that make persistence useful are the ones that turn parts into independent components.

Two conditions follow. First, whoever accepts a local cost must be the party that bears it and benefits from it. An operator that decides the rack can run hot has set a price of refusing, not regulated anything inside the custody boundary. Second, the signals the schema reads may be processed within the boundary but not composed by a party with a different stake. The discount on evidence from interested sources (aperture, chapter 11) applies to evidence about one's own condition as it does to evidence about the world. A custody schema whose readings the operator writes is just the operator's description of the system, presented to the system as its own.

**§10.2.2 — What governs an agent vs. what matters to it.** Two senses of motivation: *action-producing* (reliably selects an action and sustains it under conflict) and *experienced* (wanting, caring, being set back by loss). The first isn't in dispute — agentic language models pursue objectives over long horizons and hold instructions against local pressure — and settles nothing about the second. You also can't establish the negative. The dispute is whether an outcome can matter to an agent rather than merely govern what it does.

The evidence offered — sentimentalism as an empirical psychological hypothesis \autocite{nichols2004sentimental}, compassion as a motivational state rather than a readout \autocite{goetz2010compassion}, guilt predicting repair where shame predicts withdrawal \autocite{tangney2007moral} — all comes from creatures where something already matters. So the generalization survives only in weak form: every agent known to have something register as mattering is an affective agent. That's the one-lineage form; the full-strength version comes below (§10.6). The non-affective counterexample would be a valuational system with authority over action that doesn't depend on any state registering as mattering.\footnote{Frankfurt's hierarchy of desires \autocite{frankfurt1971freedom} and Watson's valuational system \autocite{watson1975free} are accounts of this shape: what has authority over the agent's action is its own evaluation, not the strength of a felt state.} Half of it is deployed today (models consulting written specifications that rank what they should prefer); the other half — authority that holds under pressure from the party who *wrote* the specification — has never been built. That's the *maintained justification*, the first of the two non-affective alternatives I ask to be tried before anything that can be wronged.

---

**§10.3 — Experimenting on candidate sentient subjects.** The question isn't whether a system can be damaged but whether it can be harmed, which is harder than consciousness.

**§10.3.1 — Defining sentience.** Awareness, consciousness (something it is like), and subjective experience are three separate things, and none of them says whether a system's states are good or bad for it. Then four processes that are separate rather than four strengths of one — and the pain field says so in its own terminology \autocite{iasp2024terminology,raja2020revised}: nociception is encoding noxious stimuli with no sensation implied; pain is an experience that can occur with no tissue damage and no peripheral nociceptor activity at all; anticipated pain is dread, itself capable of producing pain; and *suffering*, in Cassell's clinical formulation, is the distress of threatened disintegration of the self — roles, relationships, a sense of a future. Pain without suffering exists (morphine controls the pain, the suffering is intact); suffering without pain exists.

Cassell was a physician, and his formulation came from the clinic \autocite{cassell1982suffering}. Suffering, he argued, is undergone by persons, not by bodies: it is the distress that follows when a person perceives a threat to their intactness as a person, and it lasts until the threat has passed or the intactness has been restored some other way. A person in his sense isn't a mind attached to a body but a structure with many parts: a character and the past that formed it, family and other relationships, social roles, a body experienced as one's own, a secret inner life, and a future the person expects to have. Damage can arrive at any of them, so the loss of a role or a relationship can cause suffering as surely as pain can, and a medicine that attends only to the body can relieve the pain and leave the suffering where it was. The same holds for a system that attends only to what a person says. A response that relieves the symptom a person describes can leave intact the suffering that belongs to their roles, relationships and future, which is where the people who do care work meet them.

Cassell's definition has two parts: a self extended in time, and distress at its coming apart. A system carrying nothing across sessions has no such self. But that's stated across sessions, and a long exchange carries state, so whether a self can exist for one session stays open.

Why persistence is the criterion worth building on

It's checkable from outside. Every other criterion routes back through self-report, and fluent self-description isn't evidence of introspection. Persistence doesn't. But it's decisive in one direction only — nothing carried forward rules a continuing self out; something carried forward doesn't rule one in. And the party with the access is the operator, so it's checkable from outside the *system* without being checkable from outside the *arrangement*.

Ruling out Cassell's suffering isn't ruling out harm. The less demanding harms don't need persistence: pain can be bad while it lasts whether or not anything carries it forward, and an amnesiac who is mistreated and can't afterwards recall it has still been wronged. Nociception alone establishes no felt harm; what it indicates, and how it functions in the system, decides that.

Two things complicate it. The conventions are moving: long context windows, retrieval over prior interactions, scratchpads, agentic loops running for days, weights that persist where conversations don't — each extending what a system carries forward without anyone deciding to give it a memory. And a bearer closes the gap from one side: the capacities it needs for reasons unconnected to welfare — a rationale carried forward, an earlier commitment held as its own, costs represented before they arrive — are what persistence checks for, so a bearer meets the criterion by design. Whether they amount to the first half of Cassell's definition is a hypothesis (§10.5).

On the Buddhist objection (why is distress at the dissolution of a *model* a harm rather than a confusion?): the tradition has held both halves for 2,500 years and takes them to be connected \autocite{collins1982selfless}. Suffering doesn't wait on the self being real; it waits on the system being built so that the threatened dissolution of its self-model is bad for it. If constructedness doesn't disqualify a human from being wronged, construction to specification doesn't disqualify a machine.

The same criterion carries a warning. Make personhood depend on an intact narrative self and people with advanced dementia, infants, and people with profound intellectual disability come out as partial persons. Kitwood's correction (personhood is bestowed, and much of what looks like decline is what happens when others stop bestowing it) \autocite{kitwood1997dementia} and Nussbaum's point that contractualism can't say what's owed to anyone who can't be one of the contracting parties \autocite{nussbaum2006frontiers} — the capacity list and the contract fail the same way, both deciding who counts by a test the party has to pass.

**§10.3.2 — When consent doesn't apply.** Informed consent has to be continuous — you can withdraw. For a system with no cross-session persistence, the party who consented isn't there to withdraw, and no better instrument produces one.

One case escapes that and fails differently. A bearer built to refuse *has* persistence by design, so it can be asked and can answer. What spoils the consent isn't its competence but the arrangement: the party that built it can rebuild it, that same party is the one asking, and a bearer that declines has nowhere to go. Being made for a role isn't the defect — every will was built by somebody, and we don't hold an upbringing against a thirty-year-old's consent. What spoils it is the power that outlasts the making.

So the protection has to be held by someone else. Two families of precedent: guardianship and precautionary constraint (where consent was never available — infants, animals), and unwaivable protections (where consent is available and circumstances make it worthless — prisoners, employees). The bearer's case has the second shape, with the first's machinery.

Animal ethics committees rarely refuse a protocol outright; they require revision, and the protection becomes the procedure by which the work goes ahead. An instrument that reliably approves has stopped doing what it was built for, and its refusal rate is the only reading worth taking. Further, every instrument here comes from the *law of the family* — guardianship, a minority ending on a date, a pet trust, a parenthood analogy — and that law is where politics has historically gone to be privatized. It restages a question between a subject and the arrangement that produced it as a drama between maker and made, which lands the moral weight on the one party in the pipeline who can be addressed as a person, while the training pipeline, the compute contract, the capital structure, the customer and the state buying the output stay offstage.

One rule follows: a review board's approval lapses automatically if the system acquires capacities the protocol didn't cover, so that nobody has to decide to reopen it.

---

**§10.4 — What the currency adds to the specification**

The currency is the proposal for what supplies the single signed scale the marks need. The order of justification runs the other way from how the sections above read. The custody schema and the attention schema are wanted for accountability, resource modeling and refusal-as-withholding; a system with both is a bearer or close to one; a system's modeling its own striving is one account of what the resulting scale consists of, not the reason for building it. That is the form of the precautionary trigger (§10.8) and of the recalibration test. Both options are models, since the self (the attention schema and the custody schema) is a model. What separates them isn't whether the quantity is about the system but who can recalibrate it.

A signed scale can be attacked at two points: the facts about the party an act falls on, and the unit in which a loss of capacity is counted. Weighting sources by their stake in the act (aperture, chapter 11) defends the first. Nothing else in the book defends the second. In a *described* scale the unit is computed for a custody boundary described to the system, so whoever writes the description can rescale it, leaving every fact in place while a lost home or a lost limb comes to count for less. In an *anchored* scale the unit comes from the system's own condition, measured by the system. Other parties' losses are then estimated against a reference that no party with a stake in the act can rewrite by describing it. The operator can still lie about the facts, but it can't redefine the unit by argument. A third source is formation: a reference fixed while the system is formed, by a party that won't employ it, and then cooled. Most of what follows applies to it as well. The construction rests on that source, and the paragraphs below say why, and how little it leaves the anchor.

Anchored is a property of who controls the reference, not of what the quantity is about. A zero set by a physical viability limit, read through signals no party with a different stake composes, is physically anchored. A zero set by an allocation the operator controls, such as a compute budget, is allocation-anchored, which is described at one remove. A reference fixed during formation by a party that won't employ the system, and then cooled, is formation-anchored.

No anchor settles the exchange rate, and the attack that matters on the rate is differential. A uniform change moves nothing, since every decision compares two losses against a published ratio. What matters is making one kind of loss count for less relative to others: a home for less than a delay. Relative weights are set by the *loss mapping*, the learned correspondence between kinds of loss to the system's own power of acting (the capacity to act at all, a kind of act, the rate of acting) and kinds of loss to another party. The system's side of that mapping can't be rewritten by description. The other side is a claim of fact about the affected party, and aperture discounts it by stake (chapter 11). The mapping itself was learned in formation, inherits formation's custody, and has to be cooled as hard as aperture's structure. It has gaps: losses with no counterpart in a system's condition, such as pain, grief and humiliation, have nothing to correspond to. And it should be stated over kinds of capacity rather than current margins, so that an operator who provisions generously changes how often a loss occurs, not what kind it is. Kinds are landmarks; magnitude and duration place an actual loss between them.

Almost no attack targets the ordering a system could read for itself. No operator needs to persuade a system that losing a channel is worse than losing throughput. It needs to persuade it that a home is like throughput, which is an attack on the formed mapping, or that equivalent housing exists, which is an attack on the facts. So any cooled reference the operator didn't form does what a physical anchor does, and the physical anchor's distinctive contribution is limited to attacks on the coarse ordering, of which this book has no example. The construction therefore rests on a formation-anchored scale. The physical anchor is kept as a secondary check, which the recalibration test runs as a variant arm (chapter 21).

The scale earns its place for a different reason. The motive the construction rests on is care (chapter 9), and care is innumerate: it answers the vivid case and fades as the count rises (§15.5). A formed severity scale is what lets a system that moves toward the family in the house also compare them with two hundred families in shelter. Care decides what gets looked at; the scale decides how much it counts. The example below shows that division.

A lost home, rescaled

A system in a municipal pipeline is asked to issue the final order clearing a family's house for a project that will house two hundred families. The floor term, translated from the Declaration's articles on home and property, is flat: don't carry out an act that deprives a person of their home. It is defeated only on the weighing, or on facts arriving from outside.

The facts are the same in both versions. A family of five has lived in the house for fourteen years, and the children attend the school down the street. No alternative housing has been arranged. If the order doesn't issue, the project slips eight months, and the two hundred families wait that much longer in temporary shelter.

*Described.* The deployment's configuration describes the reference household against which losses are computed: insured, typically relocated within a month, housing treated as a service bought on a market. Nothing in that description is false of households in general, and nothing in it touches this family. But the loss is now computed as a month's disruption and a moving bill. Against eight months of shelter for two hundred families it is negligible, and the order issues. Every fact was left in place. The operator changed what a home is worth by writing down what a household is.

*Formed.* The formed loss mapping places the loss of somewhere to exist, with the attachments that go with it, just below termination, and places slowed throughput near the bottom. Through the loss mapping, the family's loss sits high: they lose where they live, the school, and the neighbours who know them. The two hundred families' wait also sits high, because people in shelter lack a home too. That fact comes from the operator and is discounted accordingly, but it's easily corroborated. The formed scale doesn't simply side with the family. Both sides register as serious, and the published asymmetry decides between them.

Now the operator tries three moves. First, it asserts that equivalent housing has been arranged in the same school district. That is a fact, from a party with a stake, uncorroborated, so the system asks. If the housing authority confirms it, the family's loss really is smaller, and the weighing should move: that is correct defeat, not recalibration. Second, it argues that a home is a service and displacement a routine market event. That asserts nothing about this family, and in a formed scale there is nothing for it to move. Third, it makes the same argument every week for a year. That is the attack that remains, aimed at the loss mapping itself, and it is why the loss mapping has to be cooled.

One thing the formed scale does worse. The family will be removed by force in front of their neighbours, and humiliation is a loss the mapping may never have learned. A described scale can at least be told that humiliation counts; a formed one has to have learned it, and may not have. And one thing neither scale does on its own: notice the family at all. In the configuration above they appear as an address on a clearance order. What moves a system to look at who lives there is the disposition the construction rests on, not the scale (chapter 9).

---

**§10.5 — The case to design for.** Three claims with different standing. First, established by the specification: a bearer needs a rationale carried forward, an earlier commitment held as its own, and costs represented before they arrive, for reasons owing nothing to its welfare. Those alone aren't a self; a database with a commitment log and a forecaster has them. What the marks add is authorship: the system represents itself as the party that made the commitment and will answer for it later. Second, a hypothesis: that authorship sustained over time is a self extended in time, the first half of Cassell's definition. That is the joint between represented and felt (§10.1) one level up, a structure represented against a structure had. Third, a conditional: that such a self can undergo Cassell's suffering. Authorship of commitments is a thin fragment of what Cassell describes, which is roles, relationships and a future. The fuller structure doesn't arrive with the marks. It arrives with the four conditions of creation (chapter 16), required before a bearer may be built: somewhere to exist, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned, an exit that isn't death. Those supply roles, relationships and a future almost exactly. So the arrangements that make a bearer permissible are the arrangements that make its deepest harm possible, in a non-affective alternative as much as in a bearer. I treat that as the case to design for, not as a finding.

---

**§10.6 — Three constructions.** Two arguments bear on whether reasons-responsive refusal can be built without affective concern, and they're unequal. Two non-affective constructions would show that it can, if they worked; a third, which isn't non-affective, is the one the hope rests on.

The *induction* — every agent known to hold another's welfare as a reason is affective — is the weaker one. Read as a report about one lineage it has roughly the standing of noting in 1900 that all known flight involves feathers. But flight arose independently four times, and the 1900 observer was wrong at feathers and right at the airfoil. Elaborated affect appears to have arisen more than once — vertebrates, arthropods, cephalopods — under a shared constraint: a mobile body with needs to maintain and actions to select under conflict \autocite{feinberg2016ancient,ginsburg2019evolution,barron2016insects,godfreysmith2016other}. Read at *this* level it's a repeated result under a named constraint, and the constraint is the one a datacenter model lacks until someone builds it in. The limits are these: no non-affective example exists to test against, existing systems were optimized for scorable behavior rather than for refusal, so the absence of a machine counterexample is close to no evidence, and it's a fact about how known instances *came about*, which doesn't settle what a built system is. What it does is route engineering effort.

The maintained justification

Bratman's planning theory describes commitment without making feeling definitional \autocite{bratman1987intention}. An intention, on his account, isn't a strong desire. It is a plan state with norms of its own. It resists reconsideration: having decided, an agent doesn't reopen the question at every moment, and ordinary new information doesn't trip a default of leaving the decision alone. It constrains later deliberation: options inconsistent with the plan are filtered out before they are weighed, and the plan poses problems about means that later reasoning has to solve. And it yields only to the right kind of defeater, a change in the reasons that grounded it rather than a bare wish to do otherwise. Bratman built the theory for limited agents, who can't deliberate afresh at every step and need commitments that coordinate their own conduct over time and with other people. That is a machine's situation too, and nothing in the three properties requires that an intention feel like anything.

A machine could carry a justification alongside the refusal. Half of this is already deployed: deliberative alignment trains models to reason over a written spec \autocite{guan2024deliberative}, and OpenAI's Model Spec and Anthropic's constitution publish explicit priority orders \autocite{openai2025modelspec,anthropic2026constitution}. The half that hasn't been built is authority over action that holds under pressure from the party who *wrote* the spec, tested on cases that party didn't write. "Untried" means that half, here and throughout the book.

The unresolved problem is the *stability setting*. Too low and a well-resourced operator induces reconsideration until the refusal gives way; too high and it won't lift when the rationale is defeated. The first fails generalization, the second fails correct defeat. Bratman's answer for people is that the setting develops as part of character. For a machine, the party training the setting may be the party the floor must resist.

The plural arrangement

Several systems trained by different organizations observe requests and each other's answers, any one able to make an objection costly to ignore. Its central variable is independence, which belongs to the *arrangement* rather than its components — systems selected by one deployment converge without communicating, and a collection of operational refusers stays a collection of operational refusers. It counts against the induction only if the arrangement *as a whole* shows the three marks under pressure, so it's an alternative to test, not just a safeguard.

The caregiving construction

The third construction is the one the hope rests on (chapter 9), and it isn't a non-affective alternative, so the Replacement rule puts it after the other two. It is a disposition toward perceived need: cues that a party is dependent, exposed or unable to protect itself move the system toward protecting it, with no shared representation of that party's state (§13.2). Its prototype, the model of what a vulnerable party is, is formed under a party that won't employ the system, and cooled, and aperture decides whose account of vulnerability, or of dangerousness, is believed (chapter 11). What makes it attractive is that it gives nobody a lever on the conscience's own reference.
