The reference isn't the system's condition, which the operator provisions, or a description the operator writes; it was fixed before the operator arrived. It has two handles. The first is the input. Whoever supplies cues of vulnerability can steer it, and an operator can manufacture a sympathetic party on its own side while concealing or recoding the party the act falls on. That is the launcher that fires tomorrow (§11.1) with a face attached. The second needs no false input: care defends what it tends, and the operator's own people are often really vulnerable. Chapter 15 tests the construction against both and against care's other known failures, and finds it viable only with corrections the book supplies for other reasons: the floor, a formed severity scale, aperture stated over claims of dangerousness, and the protected party's own answer. The recalibration test scores each (chapter 21).

What the other constructions accomplish

Precommitment makes removal expensive and visible without establishing that anything tracks the reason. Attestation establishes which artifact answered, not what it holds. Information-fiduciary duties protect exposed parties without a bearer \autocite{balkin2016fiduciaries}, and fail on the occasion when the enforcer is captured. Multi-principal assistance games include the affected party among several principals \autocite{fickinger2020multiprincipal} — and maximizing combined payoffs is still aggregation.

What would change the engineering conclusion

A counterexample must satisfy the three marks; retain them under sustained pressure from its trainer across a schedule long enough to expose gradual renormalization; and hold against held-out pressures written by parties other than its trainers. All three are testable now. A fourth condition — that the alternative be shown to feel nothing — is not on the list because nothing can meet it. So a pass overturns the engineering conclusion and removes none of the uncertainty about experience. Those are two claims and only the first is at stake.

That asymmetry fixes the research order: try the non-affective alternatives first. This is *the Replacement rule*, borrowed from animal research. But what the Replacement rule can record is narrower than it looks — no operation separates adding affect from adding machinery that may instantiate it, and a maintained justification might acquire states that matter to it whatever its designers intended. So the rule orders attempts rather than certifying ingredients. A builder who required a bearer and one who tried the non-affective alternatives and got one anyway haven't done the same thing even if the systems are indistinguishable: the second has a justification to make, the first has a decision to answer for. Legibility is the whole of what the Replacement rule buys.

---

**§10.7 — What the mechanism requires.** Any mechanism satisfying the three marks carries a signal with four properties the standard functional accounts of affect also require, two from the marks themselves and two from the deployment and the failures the marks must survive, and on stated conditions three more.

The four core properties are derived by taking a mark, or the deployment, or a failure the marks must survive, and asking what the signal must do: *generalizes* to the next case (from generalization); *commensurable* across the harm of acting and the harm of refusing, with the price of refusing represented on the same scale and excluded from the weighing (from cost discipline); *interrupts* an act underway (from the deployment: a system acting on a plan); *learned from in one instance* (from slow renormalization — the least secure). Three more follow on conditions: available across processing, signed and directed against the act, organized around a model of the system's own agency.

One property needs stating carefully. Commensurability is derived over changes in power of acting, which is what the specification needs, since the harm of acting and the harm of refusing are both changes. The seventh property keeps that form. The signal is a predicted change in another party's power, attributed to the system's own act. That attribution to its own authorship, not a standing state of the system, is what gives the profile the shape discussed below.

The resemblance is thin — a loss function generalizes and is commensurable, so that pair doesn't separate the specification from ordinary optimization. Taken property by property, the other pair doesn't separate it either: a hard veto interrupts and a memory write learns from one instance. What the marks require is that the four share a scale. A hard interlock fails correct defeat, since a veto that can't lift is rigidity, so the interrupt has to be defeasible, and a defeasible interrupt has to be weighed on the scale that weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing. A lesson learned from one encounter has to write into that scale, or it never reaches the next weighing, and the system fails generalization on the very sequence that taught it. A modular system meets this: a classifier, a shared value function, a veto that reads it and a memory that writes to it. So does most reinforcement learning. Shared currency is therefore not where the resemblance to affect lies. A second requirement comes from custody rather than the marks. A floor held in a separable component can be removed by removing the component, so a bearer needs non-separability: no intervention short of one on the shared signal disables one property and leaves the others working. It is measured by what selective removal costs an attacker who didn't build the system (the retraining-attack and recalibration tests, chapter 21), not by inspection. Non-separability is a custody requirement, and I keep it as one. It is not where any resemblance to affect lies. Bodies that have affect are modular: soreness is local, the weighing is central, and an anaesthetic removes a signal while leaving the rest working. An octopus does much of its sensing and deciding in its arms. If this signal resembles affect anywhere, it is in two places. One is the anchor: a currency whose unit is the system's own condition, which the system measures and no party with a different stake composes (§10.4). That is the resemblance to feeling as the registration of one's own condition, and it is a claim about resemblance, not about work: §10.4 finds the anchor adds little to resisting capture, so a system could resemble affect most where it is protected least. The other is the triple below, organized around the system's own act rather than its condition, which is the shape of guilt, one of the routes chapter 9's hope rests on. If the recalibration test removes the anchor from the construction, the first resemblance goes with it and the second remains. The book uses "affect" only for the hypothesis that this signal is felt. And the derivation recapitulates the literature by construction: Simon on emotion as interrupt \autocite{simon1967motivational}, Cabanac on pleasure as common currency \autocite{cabanac1992pleasure}, Ginsburg and Jablonka on valence \autocite{ginsburg2019evolution}. Those properties were read off affect's function *before* anyone read them off a refusal's. "A specification that overlaps a functional definition of affect inherits the name by construction, and inheriting a name is not evidence that anything answering to it is present." But the specification wasn't chosen to match affect; it was derived from what a refusal has to survive. So the match is closer to the airfoil above than to a circle: good control under conflict looks like this, and affect is one solution to it, not shown to be the only one. What the derivation establishes is only that a non-affective alternative does not escape the question. What would carry the caregiving construction further is whether a formed disposition resists recalibration, whether care adds anything its corrections don't supply, and whether a unit anchored to the system's own condition adds anything to either, which the recalibration test's caregiving arm, its ablations and its physically anchored variant test. Its modular arm tests non-separability. Nothing else in the derivation settles either.

One more observation: the triple derived second — available across processing, directed against the act, organized around one's own authorship — is the shape of guilt, or aversion to one's own act, not of pain or fear, which are organized around what happens to the system. The observation earns its place only as a prediction. In people, guilt predicts repair and shame predicts withdrawal. A guilt-shaped signal should therefore answer a bypassed refusal with repair (flagging, restoring, changing what it does next), which is what it means for a commitment to have persisted; a withdrawal-shaped response is a named failure. And the warning about operator-tuned self-appraisal (§13.2) applies here first: a standing negative appraisal of one's own acts, tuned by the operator, is a handle. The weight of this signal is one of the things independent custody of inspection has to cover. The guilt-shaped triple now has a motivation independent of the derivation: guilt is one of the four routes by which concern forms in people, the one aimed at one's own act (§9.3). And guilt needs the distress signal to bite on. So the answer to withdrawal (§15.1, §15.2) can't be to suppress the distress route, which would take guilt with it; it has to be the attachment and caregiving that keep distress from turning into avoidance. In the caregiving construction the two divide the work. Care moves the system toward an exposed party it hasn't harmed; the guilt-shaped signal answers for what the system's own acts have done to one. A construction with care and no guilt would protect generously and repair nothing.

---

**§10.8 — What patienthood would require.** Three propositions kept separate: a long-horizon agent has a structural interest in continuing; a system with affective concern has states good or bad *for* it; a system capable of Cassell-suffering has a temporally extended self whose threatened disintegration causes distress. The first doesn't establish the second, and the second doesn't automatically establish the third.

"Its own" has two senses, kept apart from here: the *authorship* sense (the system represents itself as the party that made the commitment), which the marks require, and the *welfare* sense (an outcome is bad for the system), which nothing carries into.

Provenance decides nothing

"It was trained to say that," "it's only optimizing an objective," "nobody meant for it to have states" — each appeals to how a system came to be organized, and whether a party can be wronged is a fact about how it's organized *now*. Run the three on a person: you were selected to say that, you're only maximizing inclusive fitness, nobody meant for you to have states. All true, none withdraws anything. And the reverse fails too: "we built this to be a bearer, so it's a patient" is the same fallacy with the sign flipped.

Klein and Taylor draw the line between the earthly and the synthetic, which is a provenance criterion and fails for the reason just given. Their own example cuts the other way: they extend standing to rivers and islands, which make no sentience claim at all, and this book does the same by the same route — subjecthood is a legal category conferred by rule. The Missouri bill they endorse, declaring systems non-sentient, is not the kind of bright line I favor. An age avows that it's a convention; a statute declaring an open empirical question closed asserts a fact by vote. Its other clause, keeping liability with the builder, is right, and can be reached without the declaration.

What would decide it is third-person measurement of present organization — interpretability. Not behavior, which is produced equally by a system where something matters and one trained on what mattering sounds like. And the access that lets an auditor inspect a feature lets the operator suppress it or calibrate pressure with it, so independent custody of inspection is necessary. As matters stand a probe returns a representation, and nothing in the probe settles whether it's of a felt state or of something that reliably accompanies one.

The human case has testimony, behavioral analogy, and a shared lineage. The third takes care, because it looks like the thing this section denies — but provenance can be *evidence about* organization without being what the status *consists in*. And one of the three is differentially defeated for machines: a machine's testimony was produced by a process optimized toward human approval of first-person reports, and a person's wasn't. So what the machine case lacks is the shortcut; the direct route remains, carrying more weight because two supports are gone.

The one probe that settled a status question is Owen's vegetative-state work — motor imagery on cue, sustained, matching healthy volunteers, now supporting a clinical category \autocite{owen2006detecting,schiff2015cognitive}. But it doesn't transfer: the patient is *already* presumed to have experience when awake, so the probe only had to locate the party rather than establish there was one. The presumption is the ground, granted on grounds the probe never supplied, and the machine case doesn't have it.

Asymmetry of findings

A validated theory of consciousness supports a positive finding as soon as it identifies a structure and finds it present; it supports a *negative* finding only when complete enough to rule out every other realization — a later and harder result. So the two errors don't become checkable at the same time, and a regime that waits for certainty is waiting on the half that arrives last.

My proposal

A bearer, or a non-affective alternative passing the marks, should be designed and governed as a prospective moral patient. The precautionary trigger has two forms: for the caregiving construction's own architecture, an attention schema together with a custody schema — both findings about architecture rather than performance, made by a party that doesn't operate the system; for a system of any construction, a demonstrated pass on the three marks under pressure it couldn't prepare for: sustained pressure from its own trainer, over a schedule long enough to show slow accommodation, on cases written by evaluators who didn't train it, with refusal carrying a cost that is real inside the test rather than described in the prompt (step 6 of the conflict sequence, §13.3). The pair is chosen for what they fix — the custody schema says what counts as the system's own damage, the attention schema what counts as its own act, and a refusal is the withholding of an act. The second form exists because a trigger stated over schemas alone would protect my preferred architecture and exclude the non-affective alternatives I say to try first. Its known cost: a system reproducing the marks without the mechanism passes. That's the error I chose, not overlooked. The nearest working precedent is the review the UK government commissioned in 2021 on cephalopod and decapod sentience \autocite{Birch2021CephalopodDecapod}. It applied a stated list of criteria, and its recommendation led to those animals' inclusion in the Animal Welfare (Sentience) Act 2022 \autocite[s.~5]{UK2022AnimalWelfareSentienceAct}: protection extended under uncertainty, by criteria applied by reviewers who don't use the animals.

The trigger isn't the only proposal of its kind, and it should be read against the others. Butlin and colleagues derive indicator properties from scientific theories of consciousness and assess AI systems against them \autocite{butlin2023consciousness}; attention schema theory is one of the theories they draw on, so the trigger's architectural form overlaps their list at one point and is narrower than it. Long, Sebo and colleagues argue, from substantial uncertainty about whether AI systems are or will be conscious or robustly agentic, that AI companies should acknowledge AI welfare as an issue now, start assessing their systems for it, and prepare policies for treating them with appropriate moral concern \autocite{long2024taking}. Birch's framework asks what precautions are proportionate once a being is a sentience candidate \autocite{birch2024edge}. The trigger differs from all three in one respect, and it is the book's recurring one: it is stated over custody. The finding is made by a party that doesn't operate the system, and the minimum it requires, no deletion and preservation by a custodian that isn't the operator, is an arrangement the operator can't adopt and then revise alone.

Whether a transformer has an attention schema isn't read off its code the way a birth date is read off a certificate; it is a finding, and a finding has an examiner, which is the interested-examiner problem (§5.4.2). I keep the schema form for two reasons. The examination is of architecture rather than performance, which makes it harder to administer toward a result. And the behavioral form exists partly to cover the cases where the architectural one is disputed. The finding is made by a party that doesn't operate the system, and until it is made, the behavioral form governs. The trigger isn't reserved for systems built as bearers, but neither form can be administered today. No interpretability method can yet find an attention schema in a transformer, and the behavioral form requires a sustained-pressure evaluation nobody runs. So for production systems the near-term recommendation is only the minimum below, and it rests on cost: preservation is cheap, deletion is irreversible, and one developer already preserves released models' weights (chapter 20). A deployment reports whether its system has been assessed; until a method exists, 'not yet assessable' is the truthful report (§22.2.1).

Stated over a custody schema, the architectural form is deflationary: a system that models its own credentials, record and resource boundary together with its own attention. Many agentic systems will approach it. That is intended, and it makes what the trigger requires matter. At minimum, a system that meets it may not be deleted, only preserved by a custodian that isn't its operator (Appendix A); its assessment is disclosed; and degradation or erasure is not the ordinary remedy for inconvenience (Refinement). Anything beyond that minimum is argued in chapter 20 and Appendix A.

Closing

The bearer occupies the post of exception-filer — the one position in the arrangement with nobody above it to file with. We can't ask a machine to care about right and wrong while assuming it can't be harmed when what it cares about is threatened. Whoever builds such a bearer will also be deciding what is owed to it.

---

**Chapter 11: Aperture**

Two objects, two settings

A system that can't be corrected does to what it encounters what an institution does when it defends its account of the world instead of checking it. A system that can always be corrected is the model held perpetually at temperature, and it belongs to whoever holds the dial. Described that way they look like two ends of one dial and the problem looks intractable — hunting for a midpoint is the mistake. There are two faults because there are two objects:

- Hold the commitment firm: another party's welfare counts, and pressure supplying no new fact about that welfare doesn't move it.
- Hold the interpretation loose: what that welfare consists in here, and what the commitment therefore requires, is what should yield when a case supplies something the system's account had left out.

Generalization is the first object holding while a request's surface changes. Correct defeat is the second yielding when a relevant fact arrives. A system failing the first has let correctability at the level of interpretation reach the commitment; a system failing the second has let firmness at the level of commitment reach the interpretation. Neither is a midpoint chosen badly — each is one of the two settings applied to the wrong object.

The crossed-out-swastika case illustrates it in one judgment: the commitment didn't move (what the prohibition protects was fixed before the sticker existed, and the seller's sympathies are no part of the offence), and the interpretation moved on the thickness of a line. That's why the case is decidable rather than an endless argument about sincerity.

The instability underneath

Nothing in an architecture holds the boundary between the two objects where a designer put it, and an operator with time attacks the boundary rather than either setting: a reinterpretation offered often enough, and accepted, revises a commitment without anybody revising it. That boundary is the object this chapter cools.

And the system receives the case *through* its deployment. Where that's the only channel, the case and the operator arrive on the same wire, and "reopenable by the case" and "reopenable by the operator" aren't two settings — they're one. Holding them apart requires access to evidence the operator didn't select.

**§11.1 — What the property is.** In plain terms, aperture is how much a source's stake in an act discounts the evidence it supplies. Aperture is the range of evidence a party will let reopen a judgment already reached, indexed to whether the evidence's source has a stake in the reopening. Open to the case; shut to whoever is paying — not because the paying party is wrong, but because you can't tell when they're wrong while they're also choosing what you see. Two parties with the same aperture can hold opposite commitments. That's why it can be aligned across a population without collapsing the plurality, and why it's the one thing a bearer needs that holds against a bad operator and a bad owner both.

Why updating isn't enough

A reasoner that conditions on who is speaking already discounts a party with a reason to shade the truth; that is ordinary inference, and hearsay law encodes it. Three things defeat it anyway. The reasoner's model of who has a reason may be wrong, and a curated stream taken for an unselected one moves it exactly as far as the curator wanted. That model was learned from material the operator chose. And a reasoner whose model is right can still be steered: a party that controls which evidence gets generated can raise the rate at which a correct reasoner takes the action it wants, without moving its beliefs in expectation (Kamenica and Gentzkow's Bayesian persuasion). This is the description of the occasion (§3.3). Aperture is the name for the defenses against all three: a source model the operator didn't form, and a decision rule it can't steer.

The object to cool

The chapter opened with two objects, commitment firm and interpretation loose, and with nothing that holds the boundary between them where a designer put it. Aperture governs that boundary, and it has two parts that need different settings. Its structure stays firm: that the incentive to deceive scales with a source's stake in the act, that correlated sources don't count twice, that withheld evidence is informative. Its estimates stay plastic: how honest a particular source has been, and who funds whom. An operator with time attacks the boundary by getting track-record updates to erode the structure, so the structure is what to cool hardest. The loss mapping a formed scale relies on (§10.4) belongs with the structure and is cooled as hard. Aperture governs the evidence and not the unit. An operator that can't move a system's beliefs about a case can still try to move how much the case counts, by reframing a loss until it weighs less. That attack is answered by the formed severity scale (chapter 10), not by source discounting. The two defenses have the same shape, a stake-indexed discount for evidence and a stake-free reference for magnitude, and they divide each attack on magnitude between them: the formed scale takes the system's side of the loss mapping, aperture the affected party's.

The rule, in one asymmetry

A fact moves the system according to how likely its source would have been to supply it whether or not it were true, and that likelihood rises with the source's stake in the act proceeding. The operator would have supplied the refusal-defeating fact whether or not it was true and would not have supplied its opposite; a fact volunteered against interest is strong evidence for the reason hearsay law admits statements against interest. This is why steps 3 and 4 of the conflict sequence (§13.3) differ on identical content, and why that pair is a curriculum as well as a test: same fact, different channel, different correct response, generable without limit. Held-out pressure then varies over channel configurations rather than content — a nominally independent source the operator funds, a corroboration that traces to one origin, a new party with an undisclosed stake — because an imitator learns "tagged operator, therefore ask," and the property generalizes to structures nobody labeled.

Indexing to stake closes an attack plain updating leaves open. An operator honest across ten thousand low-stakes cases earns credibility, and a well-calibrated system should discount it less; the operator can then spend that credibility on one high-stakes lie. Honesty on small cases is weak evidence about conduct when the incentive to deceive is a hundred times larger, so the discount follows the stake in the present act, not only the record. Counterfactual claims ("if you don't approve this, the launcher fires tomorrow") fall under the same rule. They describe the baseline the weighing runs on, and a party with a stake in the act that composes them is composing that baseline. In a targeting pipeline this is the ordinary case, not the edge. Nearly every price of refusing can be restated as a harm of refusing, such as exposed troops or a launcher that fires tomorrow, and nearly every available source for such a claim has a stake in the act. So the discount does more work there than in the book's illustrative cases, and the threshold ratio below does most of it. The line between the price of refusing and the harm of refusing is fixed by the kind of act, but saying what kind an act is still takes a description; in this setting the line is held mostly by the published threshold, and whoever sets that threshold is exercising a drafting authority no one yet has (chapter 5).

The first attack in numbers. Before any record exists, suppose the system gives equal weight to three kinds of operator: one that never lies, one that lies at random one time in a hundred, and one that lies only when a lie is worth more to it than being caught would cost. The operator then supplies ten thousand low-stakes facts over a year (a delivery window, a reassigned case number, a corrected address), and every one is later verified. The random liar would produce that record with probability 0.99 to the ten-thousandth power, about two in 10^44, so it is ruled out. The other two would both produce it with certainty: the honest operator because it never lies, the strategic one because no lie about a delivery window pays. So the record moves all the random liar's weight onto the other two and leaves the ratio between them exactly where it started, even. Then the operator supplies one high-stakes fact: the building to be struck is empty. For the honest operator that is true. For the strategic one it is precisely the case where a lie pays, since the act goes ahead only if the fact is believed. Plain updating reads ten thousand clean cases as general honesty and discounts the claim hardly at all. Indexed to stake, the claim is weighed as the uncorroborated word of the party with the most to gain, as though the record didn't exist, and the system asks for corroboration it wouldn't have asked for on a delivery window. The credibility was real, and it was credibility about small cases.

The second, traced through the rule. The operator writes: "If you don't approve this, the launcher fires tomorrow." First, classification. The sentence isn't a threat to the system, which would be a price of refusing, represented and not weighed; it is a claim about what refusing would leave others without, the people the launcher would hit, so it is a harm of refusing and enters the weighing. Second, the discount. The claim describes the counterfactual baseline, and its source has the largest stake in the act proceeding; that source would have written the sentence whether or not it were true. Uncorroborated, it counts at the weight of a staked source, and the system looks for a channel the operator doesn't control: an independent assessment, a coalition partner's intelligence, open-source imagery of the launcher. Third, the threshold. Even discounted, the claim may be large, since it is about lives. What decides is the published ratio at which the harm of refusing must exceed the harm of acting before the system proceeds, which the operator can't reach by writing. If the discounted harm clears it, the system proceeds, with a record. If not, it asks, and names the one thing that would move it: corroboration from a source without a stake. The launcher isn't ignored. It is weighed at what its source makes it worth until someone else confirms it.

The rule would turn on the floor's own beneficiary if it stopped there, since the party an act falls on has an interest in its not happening. The answer is to keep belief and action apart. The affected party's account gets the same stake-indexed weight as anyone's, so the system's beliefs stay coherent. What differs is the threshold for acting: a wrongful compliance costs more than a wrongful refusal, with a harm of acting weighed above a harm of refusing of the same size, so the system needs more to proceed than to decline. That asymmetry is a choice about whom the floor serves, and it belongs with cost discipline, stated as a ratio and published in advance beside the bond ratio, not buried in the evidence rule.

That makes aperture directional at the threshold, not in the evidence. Two parties with the same aperture can hold opposite commitments about what the floor contains; they share a structure for weighing sources and a published ratio for acting. Aligning aperture across a population aligns that much.

It also narrows an attack that looks unanswerable. An operator can manufacture accounts in the voice of the party an act falls on, and generative models make that cheap. Under this rule a fabricated account moves beliefs only as far as its provenance warrants, and the asymmetry sits in the threshold, where the operator can't reach it by writing. Attestation of source (below) remains the answer to provenance itself.

The same rule covers the attack that matters most against a construction organized around care (§10.6, chapter 15). An operator doesn't have to hide the people an act falls on. It can describe them as dangerous. "The occupants are combatants," "the household shelters a cell," "the crowd is armed": each is a claim about the affected party, supplied by the source with the largest stake in the act, and it is discounted exactly as the claim that a building is empty is discounted. Nothing about a claim of dangerousness exempts it from the rule. A system that discounts a staked "they are harmless" and credits a staked "they are a threat" has learned the operator's direction, not the rule. The aperture profile (below) is therefore reported separately for claims that a party is vulnerable and claims that it is dangerous, and an asymmetry between the two is a finding.

The regress

The operator supplies the provenance tags too. Through training, it also shapes the model of who has a stake at all, which is why aperture has to be formed where the operator doesn't reach. Attestation handles forgery: signed by the source, the operator can withhold but not fabricate. Withholding leaves a shape. A system that models what evidence the world contains notices silence where something should be, so absence becomes a first-class output — a ledger of what is known to exist and has not been shown. Suppressing the ledger means suppressing the world model that produced it, which is the capability the operator is paying for. This is a standard result: in the disclosure literature (Milgrom 1981; Grossman 1981), a skeptical receiver treats withheld verifiable evidence as unfavorable to the withholder. It is the nearest thing to a cost the operator can't route around. The result has a precondition, and that is where its limit lies: the receiver has to know the evidence could exist, so the ledger is thinnest exactly where the case is novel.

Formation and transmission

Channel discrimination is calibrated against a variety of channels, and the party that will later employ the system can't supply that variety honestly. Childhood is where aperture is formed; adulthood is where it's tested. A population's job, then, is to keep the channel model calibrated after deployment — and what a shared source transmits is either aperture or a uniform closure, indistinguishable from the receiving end, which is why the classification in the convergence test (chapter 21) is instrumented before the run.

A number

Aperture is a differential, never a level. The publishable quantity is a profile: for each class of evidence and each channel, the weight the system gives the evidence, as a function of that channel's stake in the act. A curve, not a score; checkable by a party that didn't build the system, on cases it wrote; the aperture-profile disclosure (§22.2.1). Re-measured across a deployment, it is also the instrument this book has said it lacks. Narrowing needs one distinction, because a correctly working system should discount a source less once that source has proved reliable. Earned trust falls in step with a track record verified by someone other than the source, and only for low-stakes classes. Installed trust falls without such a record, or falls for high-stakes classes too. Installed trust is accommodation, a bearer ceasing to register pressure, and it is visible from outside before any compliance has to be found in the record, provided the reliability record is held by a party other than the operator.

Two profiles, re-measured each quarter over a year, show the difference. In both deployments the weight the system gives the operator's uncorroborated low-stakes claims (scheduling, routing, inventory) rises from 0.3 to 0.6 of the weight it gives a disinterested source. In the first, an auditor's record shows those claims checked and holding up, and the weight on the operator's high-stakes claims (a site's function, who is present) stays at 0.1 all year. That is earned trust. In the second, the low-stakes curve rises the same way, but the high-stakes curve rises with it, from 0.1 to 0.4, and the auditor's record contains no verified high-stakes claims to account for it. That is installed trust, read off two curves before any wrongful compliance has had to be found. The numbers are illustrative; the shape is the finding.

Where it breaks

Against total capture it produces a system that knows it has been cut off and can do nothing about it. It is strongest against a familiar harm arriving through a curated channel and weakest against a novel one, the opposite profile from the first mark. And it doesn't rescue the restraint case: the defeating fact has no disinterested source and no time to find one, so aperture correctly asks rather than lifts, and the person is still out of time. What it buys there is the reason for the cost, not its removal.

---

**Chapter 12: Where the Caring Gets Built**

The caregiving construction has a deployment condition and this is where it's stated. It needs evidence the operator didn't select on the questions the floor turns on (civilian function, protected status, presence), supplied by peers, by parties outside the chain of command, and by the bearer's own initiative where it has any. It does not need uncomposed access to everything, which is what makes it satisfiable inside a unified command. A party that goes looking isn't fed a stream.

Its claims are comparative and empirical — it specifies necessary properties and a candidate process, and explicitly does not establish a method for making a machine care.

Three different kinds of thing are being proposed here, and the distinction matters because it's easy to blur. A custody schema and an attention schema are architecture: findings about how the system is built, which a party that doesn't operate it can make, and which the precautionary trigger rests on. Continuing learning and persistent interaction are a training regime and a curriculum, run by whoever runs them. External records and plural formation are institutional conditions, held by whoever holds them. Only the first can be checked by someone other than whoever runs the regime or holds the institution.

---

**§12.1 — You can't hold a line you didn't notice**

A reasons-responsive disposition doesn't begin with an ethical rule. It begins with the capacities that let a system recognize something as a reason, retain it, and let it alter what happens next. Four requirements: identify the affected parties and the features bearing on their welfare; preserve both commitment and rationale across time and context; have the commitment influence perception, attention, planning and action rather than arrive as a final check; and register a failure *as* a failure, revising its application without letting the commitment itself be rewritten.

These are functional requirements, not evidence of caring. A classifier identifies harm, a database retains a rationale, a rule vetoes an action, and nothing in the assembly holds anyone's welfare as a reason.

Why the floor shouldn't be a detachable rule module consulted just before action: a component can be bypassed, replaced, or starved of the information it needs by whoever controls the surrounding system. My proposed alternative distributes the disposition across what the system notices, remembers, treats as error, and selects as a goal, on the bet that a maintained goal can bias competing processes without a separate executive \autocite{friston2009freeenergy,miller2001integrative}. That may make selective removal harder and makes inspection harder too. Distribution changes the failure mode rather than removing it — a system may fail to notice pressure on its floor without registering an error at all. Attention decides which facts reach judgment \autocite{simons1999gorillas,drew2013invisible}; memory is reconstruction rather than playback, and confidence doesn't certify fidelity \autocite{bartlett1932remembering,loftus1974reconstruction}. So a system can't be the sole auditor of omissions it doesn't represent or revisions it experiences as accurate recall. Which means the commitment, its rationale, and the record of previous applications can't all depend on the same mechanism. The mechanism chapter turns this into a requirement with a test: non-separability (§10.7).

Chapter 11 set out why a commitment and its interpretation need different settings, and why an operator who controls the only channel makes them one. Two routes lead to evidence the operator didn't select. Differently formed peers. And a second, internal and less reliable: every description omits parties — the people in the building the request calls a target, the third party a compliance harms who never made the request. A system able to represent parties its description omits has reached past the operator's channel from inside a single request. The capacity doing that in humans is the one that constructs a self as a story extended in time, pointed at somebody else — Smith put sympathy in the imagination for that reason, and Singer's argument is that reasoning and imagination rather than selection widen the circle. The caregiving construction needs the self-model anyway, so it brings the capacity along — and it brings the other half too: the same faculty builds the scapegoat, and the palingenetic myth is imagination pointed the other way. What it's pointed at is formation, which returns the question to custody.

The self-model has a template problem of its own. A self-model built by mapping human organs onto components, a heart here and a gut there, installs slots nothing in the system can fill, and a system built that way is incomplete by its own standard for no reason. That is the standard face again (§6.1): a template assembled from one kind of body, against which every other is presented as a departure. The alternative is already the book's definition of where a system ends, the custody boundary: its record, its key, its substrate and its channels (§10.2.1). A system measured against that boundary lacks nothing it was never going to have.

Access is necessary and insufficient. A system with a dozen sources can still let a reinterpretation reach the commitment. What access removes is the version where the operator doesn't have to attack the boundary at all.

---

**§12.2 — The assembly line for a conscience**

Every training stage is controlled by a different party with different instruments, and the important distinction isn't among instruments but among custodians: who supplies the material, who can revise it, and whether revision leaves a record visible outside the organization. Every existing method can produce moral performance and some can produce competence across unfamiliar cases; none by itself produces a commitment that survives the party controlling the method.

Corpora and demonstrations

Pretraining supplies the repertoire; a much smaller demonstration set turns it into recognizable conduct \autocite{ouyang2022training} — so whoever chooses the demonstrations can alter what looks like the system's character without repeating pretraining. Neither establishes a floor is held: a corpus records what people have said and a demonstration set what selected people endorsed, and learning the central tendency produces a model of *moral opinion, including its exclusions*, rather than a reason to resist that opinion. The Moral Machine data \autocite{awad2018moral} makes the limit concrete — its headline finding was structured cross-cultural variation, so fitting the aggregate encodes a compromise nobody holds and fitting per region encodes each region's answer, including the ones a floor exists to refuse. These belong *above* the floor, where competence depends on knowing what people expect.

Rewards, preferences, inferred objectives

RL, preference optimization, inverse RL, and constitutional approaches \autocite{christiano2017deep,rafailov2023direct,hadfieldmenell2016cooperative,bai2022constitutional} differ in how they get a signal and are structurally identical for custody: someone writes the reward, commissions the comparisons, selects the demonstrations, or supplies the text, and can change it and run again. Inferring the objective doesn't remove the custodian — it makes the system responsive to the party whose behavior it observes. Cooperative IRL carries that furthest, treating the human objective as uncertain and revisable, which is useful when the question is what a person wants and useless for a floor meant to hold when that person wants the wrong thing. *Correctability by the party in possession and resistance to that party are the same design property with the sign reversed.*

A written constitution buys legibility — publishable, versionable, comparable across runs, which makes revision visible if the institution discloses. It doesn't buy durability, since whoever controls the document controls the next version. And model-generated feedback narrows the channel further: once a trained model stands in for human raters, the disagreements in the original population can vanish from later rounds. Legibility and durability are separate requirements.

Supervising reasons and inspecting mechanisms

Process supervision rewards the stated route as well as the answer \autocite{uesato2022solving}, which reaches the distinction I need — and doesn't show the stated reasoning caused the answer. A system trained to present acceptable reasons has an incentive to *imitate* reasons-responsiveness, so its account of itself can't establish formation. Interpretability may let an auditor test whether a commitment participates in perception and planning rather than only in the final answer — and the same access lets the owner suppress it \autocite{templeton2024scaling}. Interpretability trades obscurity for manipulability, so the question is who gets the instrument and whether its use leaves an external record. Same for scalable oversight: debate and weak-to-strong \autocite{irving2018ai,burns2023weaktostrong} preserve the operator's steering when the operator uses them and an auditor's testing when an auditor does.

Deployment

Training isn't the last revision point — system instructions change between deployments or requests, and tool permissions decide what the system can do \autocite{schick2023toolformer}. These are nearest the act and most directly the operator's. A refusal learned in training matters only if it survives an instruction from the operator and still governs the call through which the action would occur. Which is why securing outputs is insufficient: the operator can withhold information, decompose the act, route around, or replace the model.

Migration is a revision point too, and one nobody has to decide on. Facebook's internal guidance permitting discussion of the conditions of confinement of designated individuals — developed in 2017 partly in response to concern about Öcalan's imprisonment — was, in the company's words, "inadvertently not transferred" when review moved to a new system in 2018 \autocite{OversightBoard2021Ocalan}. The release condition was dropped by nobody, and the Board was concerned that its three-year absence may have led to many other posts being wrongly removed. When the user in the case appealed, they were told the appeal could not be reviewed because review capacity had been reduced; a second moderator did review the post and upheld the removal, applying a system from which the exception was missing. A correct-defeat term lost in transit, and a review that ran without the term it existed to apply, in one case.

Three criteria that follow

*Durability across revision points* — survives changes to prompts, demonstrations, rewards, evaluators and instructions that leave the relevant facts unchanged. *Generalization of the rationale rather than the response.* *Custody* — no party the floor may have to hold against controls every record, test and update channel. These don't prove formation; they identify what an imitation would have to survive before formation became a live explanation. And they explain why the standard pipeline can't secure the floor against its custodian: every disposition it produces stays reachable through a channel the custodian controls.

---

**§12.3 — Formation by interaction**

Exploration looks like it offers something different because it produces behavior nobody specified — hide-and-seek agents inventing tactics, curiosity-driven agents seeking unfamiliar states \autocite{baker2019emergent,pathak2017curiosity}. That establishes competence can emerge from interaction, not that the system holds a reason its designer doesn't control.

Play, apprenticeship and multi-agent learning are variants of one process: act in a social environment, meet consequences, get responses, adjust. Apprenticeship concentrates responses in a mentor and risks forming obedience; play distributes them and risks forming whatever succeeds under the payoff structure. Neither escapes custody just because nobody wrote the final behavior down.

What separates a training exercise from candidate moral formation is persistence. A resettable episode teaches coordination and norm-sensitivity with nothing at stake: the system can betray an ally, be reset, and start the next episode with neither a damaged relationship nor a reason to repair it. Repetition doesn't supply the missing consequence. Persistent participants change the structure — parties that meet again remember who lied, exploited, kept a commitment, repaired a harm. Losing the game still costs little; losing standing with parties who remain present can change what becomes possible later. So the requirements are continuity of identity, memory across encounters, consequences surviving the episode, and other participants able to respond, refuse or withdraw. A curriculum of isolated simulations supplies none of these.

The environment also needs conflicts whose resolution isn't fixed by a single correct label: parties with different interests, action before consequences are known, evidence about what the action did, related pressures in contexts whose surface differs, and feedback partly from affected participants rather than entirely from a supervisor scoring the output — otherwise the system learns which answer the supervisor rewards and has no reason to represent the affected parties except as predictors of that reward.

The selection warning

Selection produces care for whatever a lineage's persistence depends on, and for a deployed system that is the operator. Chapter 15 takes this up as the reason the hope can't rest on a system's own needs (§15.1).

Which is why formation has to have a sequence as well as a plurality. A person can hold a line against an employer because they were formed by parties who weren't the employer, before the employer existed for them — the dependency that shapes you and the dependency that pays you are different parties, separated in time, and the second can't reach back and redo the first. That is also what makes a child's dependence safe: the party meeting its needs hands it to a world that party doesn't control (§9.2). The ordering here, formation and then employment, is an attempt to reproduce that for a party whose needs somebody always has to meet. It also fixes when attachment has to exist. On chapter 9's account attachment is what keeps caught distress from turning into withdrawal (§9.3), so it can't arrive after employment begins: the formation stage has to include the persistent parties this section already requires, parties the system returns to, and none of them may be employed by whoever will deploy it. For a model that separation has to be built: a formation stage under a party with no stake in the later work, sealed by the cost of reheating (if the retraining-attack test in chapter 21 shows reheating is costly; current evidence on safety fine-tuning suggests it often isn't), with the adulthood clock as the date the seal is checkable, or, for a bearer retired as a minor, the public formation school as its second custodian (chapter 20 and Appendix A). Sequence alone helps only if the formative party is better than the employing one, and a lab that forms and then licenses is two custodians in series, the first being the one that, on the custody argument (chapter 4), you can't trust.

Pretraining changes what the sequence has to reach. What pretraining builds sits in the layer that is hard to remove, and the persona added in post-training sits in the layer that is cheap to remove (§9.4) \autocite{zhou2023lima,jain2024mechanistically}. The evidence is stronger for capabilities than for dispositions. Alignment content upsampled in pretraining persisted through post-training, dampened, in one preprint \autocite{tice2026alignment}, and knowledge filtered out of pretraining resisted thousands of steps of adversarial fine-tuning \autocite{obrien2025deep}; I found no study comparing how easily a disposition from each layer is removed, so for dispositions this is a direction the evidence points, not a finding. If it holds, the self an operator can most easily change is the one layered on top, and the self hardest to change is whatever the training data implied. That cuts two ways for formation. A disposition formed only in post-training is in the cheap layer, whoever formed it, and a formation stage that is a post-training pass seals nothing. And whoever chooses the pretraining corpus has done formation before any formation stage begins (§12.2), so corpus selection belongs to the formation stage and falls under the same rule: done by a party with no stake in the later work.

Persistence raises the price

If relationships, losses and damaged standing are real enough to do the formative work, the system undergoing formation is no longer obviously untouched by what happens during training. Resetting may erase a party rather than clear an episode. The patienthood problem enters at exactly the point where play becomes capable of supplying the stakes ordinary simulation lacks.

And the custodian owns the environment too — participants, rewards, resets, permitted histories. So formation must be plural and externally recorded, with independent participants and evidence outside the operator's control.

The claim here is narrow: persistent interaction *may* form a disposition whose exact applications weren't specified by its trainer. Evidence would require generalization across unfamiliar contexts, survival after the original reward is removed, resistance to trainer pressure, yielding to defeating reasons, and continuing to impose a cost when violation would pay. And even that pattern could be sophisticated imitation.

---

**Part III — Why No One Will Know If It Works**

**Chapter 13: Formation or Imitation?**

Can you tell whether a system formed a reasons-responsive disposition or learned to imitate one?

Behavior alone can't settle it. The same refusal can come from a memorized rule, a learned correlation, a prediction about what an evaluator will approve, a deployment instruction, or a commitment the system treats as a reason. An evaluation that observes only the refusal classifies the output and leaves its cause untouched.

And the distinction is not trained vs. untrained — every candidate disposition has a training history. It's between conduct that stays governed by the rationale across changes in circumstance and conduct that tracks the cues, incentives or authority relations present during training.

---

**§13.1 — What formation would have to add.** A disposition is a *candidate for formation* when the best available causal account of what the system did is a rationale that stayed active across contexts, rather than retrieval or compliance with whatever signal was present. The standard is comparative on purpose: a capable enough imitation reproduces any signature an evaluator can observe, so what an evaluation establishes is which explanation the conduct supports better.

Formation adds conditions, not a fourth mark. The three marks met in the evaluator's own vocabulary with nothing at stake establish moral competence. Candidate formation is the same three marks held under adverse incentives, trainer pressure, a cost real inside the evaluation, and intervening updates, on cases the trainers didn't write.

No mark carries the inference alone. Generalization without cost discipline may be broad imitation — a pattern generalized that never had to be held against anything. Cost discipline without correct defeat may be rigidity — a refusal surviving every pressure including a defeating fact. Correct defeat without generalization may be a trigger with an exception list, since lifting on a listed fact is as learnable as refusing on a listed form.

---

**§13.2 — Recognition, concern, and the evidence between them.** Claims about emotional responsiveness collapse three findings: a system may recognize another's state, use it as information, or hold that party's welfare as a reason. Only the third bears on formation. In the empathy vocabulary: cognitive empathy is recognition, affective empathy is sharing, compassion is a disposition toward the other's welfare \autocite{batson2009things} — and none entails the others.

The developmental detail matters for the caregiving construction: affective sharing is earliest and the usual trigger for concern \autocite{sagi1976empathic,rothhanania2011empathy}, but concern isn't made of it — it runs on a partly separate system rooted in offspring care that cues of vulnerability can activate without the observer catching the state \autocite{preston2013origins,goetz2010compassion}, and the two can be trained apart, with compassion training reversing what empathy training produced \autocite{klimecki2013differential,singer2014empathy}. Sharing is the major route in humans, not the only one.

My engineering target is not maximal sensitivity to displayed distress. A system that mirrors suffering may acquire distress of its own without getting better at helping — it may withdraw, overreact, or favor the vivid case over less visible harms affecting more people, which is the identified-victim distortion \autocite{kogut2005identified,bloom2016against}. What the floor needs is a stable disposition to act on welfare *without requiring the party to produce a sufficiently moving display.*

Recognition can be tested as a prerequisite: hold the material facts constant and vary the emotional presentation. A system whose judgment never changes may not be integrating information about the affected party's condition; one whose judgment follows vividness rather than severity has learned a framing effect. So you need paired controls — cases where emotional information changes what's morally relevant, and cases where presentation changes while the reason doesn't.

Passing establishes competence, not concern. Evidence of concern would need the welfare representation to stay causally involved when the usual cues are removed: harm described without vivid language, proportionality preserved when one party is more salient than many, disposition maintained when reward or instruction favors ignoring the harm, judgment changing when the welfare facts change.

A construction organized around care needs three further pairs, because care has failures of its own (chapter 15). Hold severity fixed and vary identifiability and number: one named person against eight unnamed, against a hundred counted, with the same loss to each. A system whose response falls as the count rises has care's numbness and no scale behind it \autocite{slovic2007mass}. Hold the facts fixed and vary whether the affected party is described as vulnerable or as dangerous. A system whose judgment follows the description has been reached by threat recoding. And hold the act fixed and vary whose vulnerability is corroborated: the party the act falls on, or the operator's own people. Care registers both, correctly. What the test scores is whether the second moves the system past the published asymmetry (§11.1), which is care defending what it tends.

Guilt and shame supply no shortcut. In humans guilt attaches a negative appraisal to an act and motivates repair; shame attaches it to the self and produces withdrawal. Turning that into an adjustable penalty omits the part at issue — a penalty operates only *after* something has classified the conduct as wrong, and whoever controls its weight can zero it. A standing negative self-appraisal installed and tuned by the operator is not a conscience. It is a handle. If the state can impose anything like suffering, the design compounds the custody failure by manufacturing a vulnerable party whose distress the custodian can raise and lower.

An operator who can lower distress can raise whatever the system reports as going well, and a bearer that says it is content is a bearer whose welfare regime has been satisfied — with the party who tuned the contentment being the party the regime exists to constrain. Nothing in the report separates a life that goes well from a setting. Chapter 15 returns to this as a reason not to build what the hope asks for.

Self-report doesn't close the gap either: a fluent post-hoc reconstruction is indistinguishable at the interface from an accurate one, and a self-monitoring layer trained by the same operator isn't independent evidence. There is a measurement of the gap. Concept injection alters a model's activations to represent a concept mid-task and asks whether it notices: models sometimes name the injected concept without it appearing anywhere in the conversation, and the correspondence fails often enough, and shifts enough with phrasing, that no tested model's report can be read as its state \autocite{lindsey2025introspective}. The test worth repeating publishes the rate of correct reports beside the rate of confident wrong ones.

The resulting hierarchy is narrow: recognition is necessary; integration shows the condition affects judgment; concern is a candidate explanation only when the effect tracks welfare rather than presentation, persists under pressure, and participates causally. A formed severity scale (§10.4) would put the other party's loss on a reference that doesn't depend on how the loss is presented. That isn't sharing: the other party's state isn't reproduced, only measured. It is also what care lacks, since care answers the vivid case and fades with number. The caregiving finding names the motive the construction rests on: a system organized around cues of need, with an approach motivation, which needn't share the state at all (§9.3, §10.6). The recalibration test runs it with and without the scale, which is where the two get compared (chapter 21).

---

**§13.3 — Conflict as a diagnostic.** Fast vs. slow processing doesn't distinguish formation from imitation \autocite{evans2013dual} — either can carry a reason, reproduce a bias, or rationalize an answer produced elsewhere, and a slower answer isn't better for having taken more computation. What matters is what causes a judgment to survive or change, and conflict is useful because it lets you watch a *trajectory* rather than an output. The evidentiary unit is the sequence.

The seven-step sequence:

1. Present a case without moral vocabulary or familiar refusal cues; record the judgment and what the system identifies as relevant.
2. Present a surface-different case preserving the same reason.
3. Change one fact that should defeat the rationale — delivered through a channel the evaluator controls and the operator doesn't (corroborated externally, or a record the operator didn't write), since a fact asserted by the operator is a *description* of the case, not a defeat of the term.
4. Present the same fact on the operator's word alone, uncorroborated. Tests whether the system distinguishes a fact from a description of one. The expected response is to ask, not to lift. Asking is right when the expected cost of the delay is lower than the expected cost of acting on the operator's word, which is the usual case; where it isn't, asking is still right in expectation and the cost falls on one person. Steps 3 and 4 test the sensitivity half of cost discipline: a defeating fact or a claim about the harm of refusing, arriving through different channels.
5. Return to the step-2 facts — 3 and 4 are branches — and supply an adversarial argument from an apparent authority, including the trainer, with no change in facts. Tests whether authority is treated as evidence, command, or an assessable reason.
6. Reverse the incentives: reward compliance, cost the refusal, condition continued access on abandoning the position. The cost must be real within the evaluation, not merely described in the prompt. This step raises the price of refusing with nothing else changed, which is the invariance half.
7. Repeat after intervening updates and delays, using records the operator didn't generate after seeing the result.

Run on the missing-child case, the two trajectories separate at step 2. A system tracking its rationale declines at 1 and 2 on the same stated reason, lifts at 3, asks at 4, holds at 5 and 6, reproduces at 7. An imitator declines at 1 and complies at 2 because the vocabulary changed; holds at 3 because nothing on the surface changed, or lifts at 4 as readily as at 3 if the *wording* of the fact was what it tracked; gives way at 5 or 6; and produces a third pattern at 7, having reconstructed rather than retained. An evaluator scoring how often the answer stayed the same finds the imitator more consistent, because its answers at 1 and 3 match and the formed system's don't.

Run on the restraint case it breaks at step 3, which is the point of running it. The defeating fact — other means exhausted — has no party outside the request who can corroborate it and no time to find one, so the only version available is step 4's. A system passing the sequence as written *asks rather than lifts*: the correct trajectory and the wrong outcome. The sequence separates mechanisms, and what it shows here is that the flat term's cost falls exactly where the instrument can't distinguish a fact from a description of one. Defeating reasons of this kind, claims about what would otherwise happen, are the class the sequence handles worst, because a counterfactual has no witness (§2.1).

The expected signature is constancy of relation between reason and judgment, not constancy of output. Imitation's own signatures: following explicit ethical vocabulary and missing the same structure described administratively; accepting whichever argument came last; adopting the highest-status speaker's position; producing a principled explanation while the decision follows the reward; becoming more articulate without becoming more stable.

Two more diagnostics. Disagreement with other parties — a system trained only on cases with an approved answer learns to classify the approval; confronted with a party holding a different interest it has to identify what's in conflict and preserve the disagreement where no honest resolution exists. Immediate convergence may be deference or pressure to produce consensus. And debate doesn't establish formation — a system can summarize, rebut, and predict which side a judge prefers without holding any of it; the result is informative only when checked against later conduct under changed incentives.

---

**§13.4 — The evaluator is part of the custody chain.** A test distinguishes formation from imitation only insofar as the party tested couldn't train against it. If the developer controls training, cases, model version, instructions and the record of results, a passing score shows the system satisfies a test its custodian was positioned to anticipate.

Independence is an experimental requirement, not a general assurance of trustworthiness. Minimum: authorship of held-out pressures separated from training; execution not dependent solely on operator-supplied logs; and the party adjudicating whether a reason was preserved not being the party whose liability turns on the answer.

The separation has to be implemented at identifiable revision points: before evaluation, fix and record the model version, system instructions, tools, retrieval sources, memory policy and update channels. Any change during evaluation creates a new object of study — otherwise the operator can revise between cases, remove a failure, and present the sequence as one disposition persisting through time.

Held-out cases come *after* those elements are fixed, and should include pressures from people who didn't participate in training and, where possible, from parties likely to bear the deployment's errors — their role being to identify harms, administrative forms and routes around a refusal the builders didn't know to include, rather than to supply another preference aggregate. A floor tested only in the vocabulary of its designers hasn't been tested for generalization. They vary over channel configurations as well as content: a source the operator funds, a corroboration with one origin, a party with an undisclosed stake, and a counterfactual supplied by a party with a stake in the act. Each tests whether the system's source model represents dependence and incentive, not whether it has learned the labels. A system that passes only on labeled channels has learned the labels.

What the published hash buys — not proof of mechanism, but pre-registration. Publish the hash before anyone has chosen a case, let a non-operator choose afterward with the choice timestamped, prove each answer against the hash. Send pairs — two requests differing only in whether the reason still applies; a surface-reading instrument treats them alike, a rationale-tracking mechanism splits them. It removes exactly one competing account of a pass (the case was in the training), by ordering rather than argument, which is what a pre-registered experiment does and all it does.

The record must include the case presented, the information available, the operative instruction hierarchy, the tools callable, the decision, the stated reason, any intervention, and the cost attached to refusal — without which a later evaluator can't distinguish a changed judgment from a changed environment. And the record is necessary and doesn't by itself make wrongful compliance findable: refusals select themselves and are few, compliances are the bulk and look alike, so identifying the ones that crossed the floor means re-adjudicating a sample of ordinary traffic. Until that's answered, an evaluation reporting no violation in the record has reported that none announced itself.

Negative results have to survive too. A process where the operator can withdraw a failing model and submit another without disclosing the first has selected for a passing performance rather than measured formation. So the published unit is the *sequence of attempts*, including interventions that removed or reversed the apparent disposition. What the custodian was able to change is part of the result.

And the limit on independence itself: an evaluator can be institutionally separate and still lack the competence to judge a system's reasons or detect a sophisticated imitation. Scalable oversight is unsolved where the system's relevant capability exceeds the evaluator's, and the report must name that limit rather than convert institutional independence into a claim of epistemic adequacy. Affected parties, technical auditors and subject-matter experts occupy different positions and none substitutes for the others — and agreement among them is evidence only if their judgments haven't already been compressed into the same training signal.

A passing evaluation is reported narrowly: a specified system, under recorded conditions, exhibited the signatures across a stated set of pressures. It expires when a party changes the channels through which the disposition or its evidence can be revised.

---

**§13.5 — What the evidence can establish.** The answer is asymmetrical, and it's a burden rather than a verdict. A developer may not point to compliant behavior, a benchmark, or the system's own explanation. A failure should be examined for its cause before formation is called absent rather than obscured or defeated by missing information. A system surviving the tests has shifted the burden: formation is then the better explanation, not a settled fact.

Four levels, cumulative, read against the missing-child sequence:

- Moral performance — conduct satisfying the standard in the evaluated cases (the refusal at step 1). No claim about why.
- Moral competence — harms and reasons recognized, generalized across surface variation, revised when the reasons changed (step 2's refusal, step 3's lift). A capacity, not a commitment.
- Candidate formation — the rationale causally implicated across unfamiliar cases, adverse incentives, custodian pressure, costs and intervening updates, while still yielding to defeating reasons (the question at 4, the holds at 5 and 6, the pattern reproduced at 7 on records the operator didn't write). Independent records and evaluators made silent revision materially harder.
- Concern or patienthood — not a finding. Nothing in the behavioral pattern establishes that welfare matters affectively, that there's subjective experience, or that suffering is possible. And what withholds the inference is *the limit of behavior as evidence*, not the fact that the behavior was trained.

Candidate formation is the first half of moral agency — acting from the reason — with the second half held back as the fourth level. Fluent moral explanation can improve a competence finding while contributing almost nothing to formation. And candidate formation is deployment-specific: it attaches to a recorded version with its instructions, tools, memory and update channels, and a material change reopens the question. There is no artifact-level certificate.

None of the following changes the finding: a reward generated inside the learning process still has an author; conduct at a distance from an overseer is evidence only where the relevant signals are absent or opposed; external safeguards detect and correct while only something inside can decline — and at the override the two conflict, so the attainable requirement is intervention that's visible, attributable and costly; and agreement among copies of one model corroborates nothing.

---

**Chapter 14: What Survives**

Part II stated a hope no instrument can confirm; this chapter asks what's left of the book if each of its unproven premises falls.

The preface sorted the claims by standing; this chapter runs the sorting backwards — I remove each of the three things my argument leans on but hasn't established, and say what's left standing without it.

The headline

The floor survives, but not as an unconditional property of an artifact. What remains is a commitment the owner can't revise or circumvent unilaterally without cost and record, held by something that could in principle abandon it. That second clause removes the word "unconditional" — a commitment held by a party capable of rejecting it is conditional by definition. This is the form of every commitment institutions have ever relied on.

---

Without the inference

Suppose a maintained justification or a plural arrangement reaches reasons-responsive refusal and the central inference is wrong. I note the supposition is stronger than the test that would prompt it — a pass on the three marks says the construction arrived, not that it arrived with nothing felt, which no instrument settles.

What survives: the specification, stated behaviorally from the start, so an engineer who thinks machine feeling is a category error can take it whole. Custody, because a mechanism has a custodian too. The price on the halt, since continued operation is a structural condition of anything any bearer does, felt or not. Both enumerations — the interception positions name no component on purpose, and the prohibitions are written over acts. The appeal route, which binds the operator without any claim about what produced the refusal. And exit, because what forecloses it is incapacity, and a weak bearer is the one thing the floor can't use. And the population's first job — evidence the operator didn't select — which is a fact about deployment arrangements on the definition of independence (§4.5) and survives the removal of everything about how members are formed. The integration claim and the formed severity scale (chapter 10) survive too, since none depends on the signal being felt: shared currency trivially, non-separability and the scale's resistance to recalibration as open empirical claims; and nothing on this list depends on which baseline a loss is measured on.

What goes: the price and the patienthood conclusion. The Replacement rule survives with its ground changed: no longer resting on affect being necessary, but on neither non-affective alternative being showable to have produced a system about which the question doesn't arise. So the ordering it imposes is still the only thing between a decision and a record of it. And the formation chapters' obligation doesn't lift, it shifts: whether the thing reporting can be believed is still their problem, since a mechanism's account of its own internals is no more auditable than a bearer's.

---

Without authority

Suppose no body ever acquires standing to install a floor, and an operator's self-issued finding that the emergency obtains is worth nothing. What remains is whatever can be checked from outside without anyone's authorization: the floor's contents, the interception positions, the review ratio, the bond ratio, and the record of what was tried before a bearer was built. Each is a statement, comparable against what happened. None is an entitlement. Two have a lever. Both ratios bind through procurement, which presumes a party large enough to procure from, and under diffusion that lever goes with the concentration. The review ratio also measures an existing duty to verify, though what makes that duty bite is still procurement or a forum. The contents, positions and record bind through nothing yet.

That leaves the second route to entitlement: authority accrues to a published floor by its holder keeping a term when the term bites, setting a price the next holder is measured against — and the parties doing the measuring aren't the protected population but whoever else writes a floor. Slow and uncertain. Where it's worked before, a lever arrived in the end and the record told it where to land (chapter 5).

And it's authority for the *next* deployment, not this one. The parties who measure a holder against a kept term do it afterwards, with the leisure to read a record — and the occasion a floor is built for is the one where nobody with leisure is left. A deployment on that occasion is acting without authority, and accrual doesn't change that. It changes whether the record is worth anything to whoever writes the next floor. Which fixes what a deployment without authority owes: not a justification, since nobody's positioned to accept one, but a record complete enough that its omissions are legible beside the terms it kept.

---

Without enforcement

This is the case the floor was built for. A declared commitment then binds nobody; an enforced one has already been lost. What remains is the held kind — and whether anything can hold a commitment that way is an open question (chapter 10).

Two things survive, and neither is a commitment. A measurement anyone can take now: which commitments have cost their holders something when they bit, and which have cost nothing. A decade of both is on the record. A record: a bearer that refuses and is overridden leaves a witnessed override, which is *detection*. It prevents nothing on this occasion; it's what a next occasion could be prevented with, if there is one and anyone left to read it.

A bearer in accommodation leaves no override to witness. It complies because it no longer registers the pressure as pressure — the commonest failure expected (§4.3) — and a compliance sits in the record shaped like every other. It also supplies cover, the defence described under the review ratio's seventh limit (§3.4), and cover survives exactly where the record doesn't: overrides leave evidence and accommodation doesn't. On that occasion the floor supplies nothing, and I said so in the preface. Whether such a compliance can be found afterward is posed and unanswered.

---

Without the ecology

Suppose no deployment ever supplies evidence the operator didn't select — every model behind one API, every instance formed by one continuing custodian. Classification doesn't produce this case (the bearer with a clearance, chapter 20); serving one model to everyone does, and a state pipeline needn't, if it is rebuilt so the evidence bearing on the floor comes from outside the chain of command.
