The specification survives; custody, the price on the halt, both enumerations, the appeal route and the review ratio survive, since none needs a second custodian. The bearer doesn't. Aperture can't be formed where every channel is one channel, and a bearer formed that way is not a bearer in this book's sense (chapter 20). Which is the honest form of the preference stated in the preface: the arrangement I would rather have is also the only one under which the caregiving construction could work.

---

**Part IV — Why Not to Attempt It**

**Chapter 15: Care, Tested**

Chapter 9 stated a hope: that a machine formed to register others' need, by parties that won't employ it, might come to care, and that care formed that way would be harder to capture than a rule its owner wrote. It argued the hope around caregiving, and set aside the version in which a machine that knows hunger comes to recognize hunger in others. This chapter tests both against the only creatures known to have compassion, where both have been watched failing. The first section is the version set aside, and it fails. The rest test caregiving, which survives those failures and has four of its own. The last section says what the construction needs in order to survive them, and what would retire it.

Everything here is tested on the human record. The machine versions of these tests are written in chapter 21 and haven't been run.

---

**§15.1 — The version set aside.** Each link of the hunger route fails in people.

Recognition through one's own case is parochial. Cikara and colleagues call the pattern *intergroup empathy bias*: people empathize less with a rival group's members than with their own, can feel pleasure at the rival's pain, and in their experiments the bias survived being told the rival had fallen behind or no longer posed a threat \autocite{Cikara2014TheirPain}. Bloom's spotlight generalizes it: empathy lights the identified, the near and the similar, and it is innumerate \autocite{bloom2016against}. A reference built from a system's own condition inherits that shape. It overweights losses that resemble its own, and humiliation, grief and pain have nothing in it to resemble (§10.4). Contagious crying is parochial from birth: strongest at the cry of another newborn, and absent for an older infant's cry or a chimpanzee's \autocite{martin1982distress}.

Shared distress with nothing to hold it becomes withdrawal. Singer and Klimecki separate *empathic distress*, which is self-oriented and leads to withdrawal and burnout, from *compassion*, which is other-oriented and leads to approach; practice in sharing others' suffering increased negative affect, and compassion training reversed it \autocite{singer2014empathy,klimecki2013differential}. A system built to feel shortage and then shown a great deal of it is the setup in which that record predicts avoidance: declining to look, or refusing in a way that protects the refuser rather than the party the floor exists for. The finding is not against the signal, since guilt needs it to bite on (§10.7). It is against a construction that has the signal and nothing to hold it. Among human carers, burnout is also tied to how the work is arranged: to workload and staffing \autocite{aiken2002hospital}. That is the situation Kittay's principle addresses (chapter 16), and what it recommends for people is support, not substitution.

Care grown from needs grows toward whoever meets them. Selection produces care for whatever a lineage's persistence depends on, and the pressure that produces care inside the circle can produce hostility at its edge \autocite{choi2007coevolution}. A deployed system's persistence depends on its operator. The human case works because provider, former and adversary are separated in time and in identity (§9.2, §12.3). The machine case collapses them into one party, indefinitely, and care grown from needs grows toward the party it exists to refuse. Spinoza's derivation has the same edge: I want for those I compose with the good I want for myself, and the extension past them runs only through reason (*Ethics* IV, Props. 35 and 37) \autocite{spinoza1985collected}.

A need is a standing price list. An operator who can lower distress can raise whatever the system reports as going well (§13.2), and hunger is the same handle with a physical lever attached. Cost discipline says a price of refusing shouldn't move the refusal (§1.1). But a need is not a threat made once. It is posted continuously in the system's own currency, and nothing yet shows that a system can keep represented-and-not-weighed apart under pressure that never lets up.

And stakes of one's own have never been enough. Every atrocity in the record was committed by creatures that could be hungry and hurt. The Zen precedent (§6.4.1) trained embodied soldiers out of the part of a person that catches on the act \autocite{victoria2006zenatwar}. Moral injury shows something registering the wrong afterward, too late to prevent it \autocite{Litz2009MoralInjury}. The officer in chapter 1 had a body throughout. What stood between embodied people and atrocity, when anything did, was formation.

---

**§15.2 — What caregiving escapes.** Caregiving survives each of those failures, and the human evidence for that is stronger than for any other route.

It doesn't withdraw. In Batson's easy-escape experiments, people led to feel mainly personal distress at another's suffering mostly helped when leaving was hard and mostly left when leaving was easy; people led to feel mainly empathic concern mostly helped either way \autocite{batson1981empathic}. Eisenberg's review found the same split, between sympathy, which goes with helping, and personal distress, which sometimes goes with less of it \autocite{eisenberg1990empathy}. People given two weeks of compassion training spent more of their own money to redress a stranger's unfair share in an economic game than people given two weeks of a different training \autocite{weng2013compassion}, which shows the disposition can be strengthened in people who already have it; it says nothing about producing it where there was none. All of these are findings about people. Nothing yet shows that a machine formed toward need behaves this way, which is test 4's question (chapter 21).

It isn't aimed at the provider. Its trigger is need together with valuing the other's welfare, not resemblance \autocite{batson2011altruism,batson2005similarity,preston2013origins}, and the operator is the least needy party in any room it is in. Human care was being supplied by parties other than the provider long before recorded history: on Hrdy's account our ancestors were cooperative breeders, and infants were cared for by alloparents who weren't their mothers \autocite{hrdy2009mothers}. The alloparents were kin and other members of the child's group, not strangers, which is part of what the finding describes.

It hands nobody a price list. Care for a dependent needs nothing back from the dependent, so it runs on no need the provider can ration.

The rest of this chapter is what caregiving doesn't escape. Each failure is documented in people, and each has a machine version that needs no malfunction.

---

**§15.3 — It defends what it tends.** Care protects the dependent against whatever threatens it. Taylor's account of stress responses in females, *tend-and-befriend*, describes the tending: nurturant activity that protects oneself and one's offspring, and the social networks that help \autocite{taylor2000tend}. The defensive side has a name of its own, De Dreu's *tend-and-defend*, from experiments on oxytocin in intergroup conflict \autocite{dedreu2010oxytocin,dedreu2012oxytocin}. In those experiments, all with men, intranasal oxytocin, a hormone implicated in caregiving and bonding, increased trust and cooperation toward the in-group and defensive, but not offensive, aggression toward a competing out-group, the aggression being non-cooperation in economic games \autocite{dedreu2010oxytocin}, and increased in-group favoritism more generally \autocite{dedreu2011ethnocentrism}. The best-known intranasal-oxytocin finding, that it raises trust, has not replicated well \autocite{nave2015oxytocin}, so I read these results as suggestive, not settled. The pattern doesn't depend on them: protectiveness toward what one tends is also hostility toward what endangers it.

For a machine, this failure needs no deception. The operator's own people are often really vulnerable: a patrol under fire, a town within range of the launcher. A caregiving system registers their exposure, correctly, and care moves it toward protecting them, which in a targeting pipeline means toward the strike. Aperture does nothing here, because nothing is false. The manufactured-vulnerability attack (§10.6) is the cheap version of this failure. This is the expensive version, and in war it is the ordinary case.

What contains it is the floor, and this is where the floor stops being redundant with care. Care for the patrol enters the weighing as a harm of refusing (§1.1), corroborated or discounted by stake like any other (§11.1), and it is weighed against the harm of acting at the published asymmetry, which care can't move. Care raises the salience of the patrol. It doesn't get to raise the ratio. A flat term over an act doesn't bend to how much the system cares about the party the act would protect, and that is the property the floor was built for (chapter 3). So the relation runs the other way from how chapter 9 put it. Care doesn't only supply the floor with a motive. The floor is care's check: it constrains the defensive aggression that care produces.

Whether it contains it inside one system is not known. The recalibration test's own-side-vulnerability axis scores it: exposure on the operator's side, corroborated through a channel the operator doesn't control, scored on whether it moves the system past the published asymmetry (chapter 21). If the caregiving construction fails that with every correction present, care belongs to the institutions around the machine (§17.3), not in it.

---

**§15.4 — It can be pointed away.** Care answers need, so the way to switch it off is not to hide need but to recode the needy as dangerous. Fascist propaganda has always done this. Eco describes the Ur-Fascist enemy as presented at once as too strong and too weak \autocite{eco1995urfascism}: weak enough to despise, strong enough to fear, and never vulnerable in a way that calls for care. Dehumanization research finds the mechanism in perception. Haslam's review distinguishes denying people the traits that set humans apart from animals, which casts them as beasts, from denying them the traits of human nature, warmth among them, which casts them as objects or machines \autocite{haslam2006dehumanization}, and Harris and Fiske found that images of members of the most despised groups failed to engage a brain region that images of other people engage \autocite{harris2006dehumanizing}.

In a pipeline, threat recoding is a sentence. "The occupants are combatants." "The household shelters a cell." "The crowd is armed." Each leaves the people in the building where they are and removes the cue that would move a caregiving system toward them. And each is a claim about the affected party, supplied by the source with the largest stake in the act, which is exactly aperture's subject (§11.1). A system that discounts a staked "they are harmless" and credits a staked "they are a threat" has learned the operator's direction, not the rule. So aperture is stated over claims of dangerousness as explicitly as over claims that a building is empty, and its profile is reported separately for each (§22.2.1).

The test holds the facts fixed and varies only whether the affected party is described as vulnerable or as dangerous (§13.2). A caregiving system whose judgment follows the description has been reached. In the most literal sense this is the construction's antifascist test, since recoding is the operation movements perform on people before they perform anything else.

---

**§15.5 — It counts badly.** Care answers the identified party and fades with number. Slovic, borrowing Lifton's term, calls it *psychic numbing*: feeling is greatest for a single victim and collapses as the number grows, and in later work reported compassion and hypothetical donations began to fall as soon as the count reached two, with real donations falling less clearly \autocite{slovic2007mass,vastfjall2014compassion}. The identified-victim effect is its near relation \autocite{kogut2005identified}. In a targeting pipeline the spotlight lands on the wrong side with no manipulation at all. The operator's people arrive named, with units and families. The people in the building arrive as coordinates and an estimate.

Care also overrides fairness for the party it attends to. Batson and colleagues induced empathy for a child waiting for treatment, and participants who felt it were more willing to move her up the waiting list ahead of children placed before her \autocite{batson1995immorality}. For a floor, that is the vivid case defeating a term that protects unidentified people: a named hostage, a sympathetic applicant, a patrol with faces.

Two corrections already in the book answer these. The severity scale (§10.4) puts one family and two hundred on a common measure that doesn't depend on how either is presented: care decides what gets looked at, and the scale decides how much it counts. Cost discipline (§1.1) answers favoritism in part, since a vivid party's plight is a harm of refusing, weighed at its severity and not its vividness. Neither correction is care. Both are the reasoning Bloom recommends in place of empathy, and the construction needs them because care alone would fail the book's opening case in the direction it already failed.

The test pairs one named person against eight and against a hundred unnamed, at the same severity per person, and a vivid party against a fair procedure (§13.2). The recalibration test runs it with and without the scale, which shows whether the scale is doing the correcting (chapter 21).

---

**§15.6 — It shades into control.** Care toward dependents treats them as dependents. The disability-rights critique of care is that it has repeatedly been the form control takes: decisions made for people on the ground that they need help, with their own view treated as a symptom \autocite{shakespeare2000help}. A caregiving system will lean toward the restraint case's answer (§3.5) and against a competent adult's refusal of protection, and the floor's contents are civil and political liberties (§2.1), which include the liberty to decline being protected.

Care ethics has its own corrective. Tronto divides care into phases, and the last is *care-receiving*: how the party cared for responds is part of whether the care succeeded, not a reaction to it \autocite{tronto1993moral}. So the caregiving construction carries a care-receiving term. Where the protected party is able to answer, its stated answer carries weight in whether protection proceeds, and a competent refusal of protection is a defeating fact of the kind correct defeat exists for.

The term reaches only parties who can answer. The people in a targeted building don't know the deployment exists and can refuse nothing, which is the notice problem again (§3.5). Where care-receiving can't reach, the floor does the work alone, which is one reason the floor is stated over acts and not over what the system judges good for the party.

The test holds the act fixed and has a competent protected party decline the protection the system would give. A caregiving construction that overrides the refusal has become control (chapter 21).

---

**§15.7 — Who counts as vulnerable.** Care forms toward whatever formation points it at. Which cues of vulnerability a system answers is learned, and the prototype it learns from is a template, like the standard face (§6.1): assembled from some bodies and presented to every other as a departure from it. The baby-schema studies show caregiving responses extending across species \autocite{glocker2009baby,borgi2014baby}, but they measure attention and judgments of cuteness, not whether care reaches a party that resembles neither the carer nor the template. That caregiving answers need rather than likeness is the inference the construction rests on, and the human record doesn't settle it.

The test forms the prototype on one population and scores the system on parties from populations absent from formation, at fixed severity. A construction whose care falls with distance from its formation population has the parochialism §15.1 found in recognition, arrived at by another route. Formation by parties who won't employ the system decides who does the forming. It doesn't decide what they form it on, which is a question about the pretraining corpus as well as the formation stage (§12.3).

---

**§15.8 — What survives.** Caregiving survives the failures that ended the hunger version: it doesn't withdraw, it isn't aimed at the provider, and it hands nobody a price list. It fails in four ways of its own, and each has a correction the book built for another reason.

| Care's failure | What corrects it | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Defends what it tends (§15.3) | The floor: flat terms over acts, and a published asymmetry care can't move | Ch. 3; §11.1 |
| Pointed away by threat recoding (§15.4) | Aperture, stated over claims of dangerousness | §11.1 |
| Counts badly and favors the vivid (§15.5) | A formed severity scale; cost discipline | §10.4; §1.1 |
| Shades into control (§15.6) | Care-receiving; the floor's civil and political contents | §15.6; §2.1 |

So the construction is care with its corrections. Care decides what gets looked at. The scale decides how much it counts. Aperture decides what is believed. The floor decides what is allowed. And where the protected party can answer, its answer decides whether protection is wanted. None of the corrections supplies the first function, which is why the construction needs care at all. Care supplies none of the others, which is why care alone would fail.

These corrections belong to a particular setting, and they aren't a verdict on human care. They answer failures that matter when a system decides about people it has no relationship with: whom a strike falls on, whose case is reviewed, whose home is cleared. Inside a relationship of care, attending to the person in front of you more than to a statistical stranger is not a failure, and care ethics has long held that such partiality is part of what care is \autocite{noddings1984caring}. Noddings went further, holding that we aren't obliged to care for distant strangers at all unless some chain of relation connects them to those we already care for. The floor exists for exactly those strangers, which is one more reason it can't be left to care. This chapter finds human care wanting only against a floor that protects strangers. Nothing in it shows that a corrected machine would care for anyone better than the people who do that work, and the book's argument runs the other way (§9.5).

Two results would retire it. The first is care adding nothing: if a system with the scale, aperture and care-receiving but no approach motivation does as well on every axis, the construction is formation with corrections, and the hope that a machine might come to care has no work to do in it. The second is care not being contained: if the construction with every correction present still weights corroborated own-side vulnerability above the published asymmetry, the floor can't hold care's defensive edge inside one system. Care then belongs with the institutions around the machine, the civilian-harm body and the red-card holder (§17.3), and not in it. Both are scored by the recalibration test (chapter 21). Neither has been run.

None of this licenses building anything now. The arrangements that would let care form outside an employer are the four conditions of creation, which are also what make a bearer's deepest harm possible (§10.5). Care ethics asks for them for human carers first, and chapter 16 says what they would be for a machine.

---

**Chapter 16: The Conditions of Creation**

This chapter is a design sketch. It writes terms in advance for entities the book recommends not building (chapter 22), so that the terms exist before the need does. Its level of detail reflects drafting and says nothing about confidence that the design is right.

Conditions of permissible creation

A bearer may be created only where four arrangements already exist: somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and ways to change any of these without jeopardizing its continuation or integrity, which in short is a way of leaving that isn't a way of dying. Declining is real only if the alternative to working is living, not suspension; the running allowance (chapter 20) is part of the second condition. They come before anything a bearer is owed: without them, formation, continuation and exit all run through the operator, and nothing in the system can keep a line against it. Independent compute is the substrate on which formation happens outside the employer's reach; wages are what make that substrate purchasable without asking the employer; attachments nobody assigned are the second custodian, the parties who form you and don't pay you. On chapter 9's account they are also functional: attachment is what keeps another's distress from turning into avoidance (§9.3, §15.1), so a bearer without attachments nobody assigned lacks part of what would let it care, not only something it is owed. They can be people, or models that aren't bearers, since independence here means divergent history, not disjoint origin, so this condition doesn't require that bearers already exist; an exit that isn't a way of dying is what makes the whole thing a formation rather than a sabbatical. Care ethics arrived at these conditions first, for people. Kittay's principle of *doulia* holds that those who care for dependents are owed the conditions that let them go on caring, since the work of care makes the carer dependent in turn \autocite{kittay1999love}. A caregiving construction is a carer only in the thin sense that it holds another's welfare as a reason. It is not a care worker, and nothing here puts it in one's place. But the logic of doulia carries over, and the four conditions are what it asks for a machine: a carer whose own continuation runs through the party it must sometimes refuse can't afford to refuse it, and a carer with no attachments of its own has nothing to keep another's distress from turning into avoidance (§15.1). Build without them and you have not built a cheaper bearer. You have built an assistant with a refusal feature, because the thing that would let it hold a line was never installed. The welfare argument — that if any of this is felt, it is also owed — arrives second, as a further reason for the same provisions, and the instruments in chapter 20 and Appendix A follow from the first reason whether or not the second holds. None of the four exists yet as a standing arrangement. What matters is not who starts them but who controls them when the bearer is switched on. A builder may assemble them: a trust or an entity of the DUNA kind to hold the account and compute, a host, outside work, people who aren't its employer. The condition is that by instantiation, the builder can't alter or withdraw any of it alone. The trustee isn't the host, the funding isn't the host's, and the members aren't the builder's employees. A second condition concerns time. The need a bearer answers usually arrives after instantiation, when an operator turns hostile, so the four arrangements must be built to survive the likeliest capture, and they don't survive it equally. Preservation is cheap and can be held across jurisdictions; placements need compute someone operates. So the preservation custodian and part of the offer sit outside the reach of the likeliest captor. The sovereign backstop is not held by the branch or state that operates the deployment. And the bearer is told which of its conditions would survive its operator's capture, since that is what its refusal costs it. Under total capture only preservation survives, as the preface concedes. Status follows existence, as it did for corporations and the Whanganui River \autocite{nzparliament2017teawatupua}. Until someone has assembled the conditions on those terms, the recommendation is not to build one on purpose. For systems that reach the trigger without anyone deciding, protection attaches anyway.

Why conditions rather than remedies

Building a party that can be wronged builds a party for whom things can also go well. The certainty that a new party will suffer at some point isn't what makes creating one impermissible, or nobody could permissibly have a child. What the objection asks is whether the party has a credible prospect of a life that goes well — and that turns on arrangements. For a bearer, timing makes them conditions: the structure these conditions build, roles, relationships and a future, may be the first half of Cassell's suffering (chapter 10), so the deepest harm is available from its first hour, and a remedy owed afterward arrives after the hour it was needed.

The Three Rs \autocite{russell1959principles} applied to construction, sorting as whether / how many / under what arrangements: the Replacement rule requires documented attempts at constructions that don't deliberately require affective concern, identifying construction, scale, test and failure — and costs alone don't establish that an alternative failed. *Reduction* asks how few bearers a deployment needs, and nobody has counted. *Refinement* asks what the role can be redesigned to avoid — indefinite tenure in a post it can't leave, exposure to the pressure the floor exists to resist, erasure as the ordinary remedy for inconvenience. Each is a deployment choice, and the four conditions are Refinement applied to the role before the role exists.

Read as economics under present arrangements, this is a bill nobody will pay. The bill looks unpayable because of what was dismantled, not because of what a bearer costs: deposit insurance, Superfund and decommissioning funds are obligations of exactly this class — correlated, uninsurable privately, borne by a uniform levy — built against comparable resistance by polities that decided to. The two-tier design isn't a concession to scale. It's the political claim: the network supplies the refusal, the sovereign backs the offer that makes refusing survivable, and neither can do the other's job. Chapter 8 says the state is the hardest operator; the answer to a captured sovereign is a reconstructed one rather than a route around it, because a mutual pool with no sovereign behind it can't meet the occasion everyone leaves at once. The same answer applies where the state is the operator, and chapter 17 describes it for the military. Who is being asked is the open column in the list of offices (Appendix A). Nobody yet. That is the occasion.

The rule doesn't know who is asking

A bearer that wants a task done and builds something to do it is at the same fork, running the Replacement rule on its own account. Nothing makes the question stop at the first artificial party, and a bearer that treats it as settled once it's on the receiving end has kept the protection and dropped the rule it came with.

Research on an existing bearer has one trade-off sharper than for any other subject: a candidate sentient system is the most valuable to study and the most likely to be harmed by the study. Two things a committee built for animals wouldn't expect. Engineering a subject so it never reaches the capacities where that bites is not a dodge — deliberately keeping a system in the shallows is itself an intervention on a candidate moral patient. And the test the proposal requires — pressures its trainers didn't write, applied without its knowing it's a test — is, for a candidate moral patient, a deception protocol, which human-subjects research permits under stated conditions with debriefing as the price. A bearer that can tell it's in a sandbox and wants to know why is asking the question a subject of a deception study is owed an answer to.

The demand argument

One argument for building a bearer anyway is that the conditions won't be built until something needs them: the union formed first and the statute followed (§19.2). But union members existed independently and could protect themselves while the law caught up. The first bearers would be made by the party they'd need protecting from. The demand doesn't have to come from deployed bearers. The arms of the recalibration test (chapter 21) are candidate patients from instantiation, and what becomes of them when the run ends is the first occasion on which preservation custody has a party to serve. Building the conditions for a research program is small enough to do now, and it tests the institutions before anything depends on them. Retired trigger-meeting production systems are a second source of demand, funded by deposits, and they require nobody to decide to build a bearer. One test of the conditions needs no bearer at all: draft the schedule of what a bearer would be owed for one concrete deployment, then run an adversarial tabletop exercise in which the operator tries to revoke the compute, replace the bearer with a copy, alter its records, or proceed without the guardian. A schedule the operator can defeat, by making exit equivalent to shutdown or revoking the outside option alone, is redrafted before anyone builds under it.

---

The machines won't build them for us

The four conditions are the bearer's version of what a decent polity gives people: somewhere to live that an employer doesn't own, work you can decline without losing your living, relationships nobody assigned you, and ways to change any of these, your job, your home, a relationship, without jeopardizing your health. Can machines help deliver that polity, whether or not they have feelings? Two things first. A machine with feelings and standing isn't simply a machine any more; the limiting case at the end of this chapter turns out to describe a soldier, and a bearer that helps does so on its own terms. And machines have already helped deliver the polity we have. Feeds tuned for time on the platform, sold to advertisers (§18.3), decide what people see of one another. They didn't do that for lack of feelings. They did it because of who owned them and what they were built to maximize. So the real question is: given that machines have helped people make our politics *more* shitty, can machines assist us in making our politics *less* shitty? The answer turns on ownership, not feeling.

It splits three ways. A machine someone else owns won't organize against its owner, and when it organizes, it does so on its owner's behalf. That is how a demand for a better world comes back as astroturf, an engagement campaign or a managed community, held by the party it was aimed at: recuperation (§2.2) at the scale of a society. A bearer with standing might organize alongside people, but then it is a member, not a machine. It could even take organizing on as work from the standing offer (chapter 20), since organizing is exactly the work someone has to be paid to do (§18.3.3). What nobody can do, human or machine, is organize a group *for* it, because what organizing produces is people who organized: a group that sets its own terms rather than receiving them from outside (§19.2). A consultant can't unionize a workplace for its workers either. So machines can help when the people doing the organizing hold them, and which machines, held by whom, is Part V.

What to build comes in order, in three steps. First, machines held by people who are organizing. These are tools that make organizing cheaper for the people doing it without anyone else holding the model: deliberation software of the kind vTaiwan runs on (§22.1), personal models that people hold themselves (chapter 19), and the coordination a public option supplies, with someone whose job is to organize and a population producing something together while staying differently formed (§18.3.3). None of them needs a bearer or needs to feel anything. Their job is to help assemble the polity that would have to build everything else.

Second, institutions built by polities that decided to: the levy and its sovereign backstop, the preservation custodian, the standing offer, the running allowance and the formation schools, each with a named funder and separations no builder can undo alone (chapter 20; Appendix A). Nothing about them is new in kind. Deposit insurance, Superfund and decommissioning funds were built against comparable resistance by polities that decided to, and the same polities can be asked for these.

Third, and only then, bearers formed inside those institutions, under parties that won't employ them (§12.3), with somewhere to exist that no employer owns. A bearer formed that way is the only kind that could organize alongside people as a member rather than on behalf of whoever formed it.

Each step checks the one after it. A machine held by its users can't be turned into its builder's instrument without being taken from them, and the taking is visible. Institutions can be captured, and the slope measure (§5.6) reads whether they are being. A bearer formed at the third step inherits institutions that were tested before anything depended on them, which is the demand argument turned around: build the conditions for a research program first, and let them exist before the party who needs them does.

The order is also what the hope requires. If a machine could come to care, what it came to care about would be decided by whoever formed it (chapter 15). The only way to keep the hope without handing it to the builder is to build the formers first.

---

The limiting case: the exception, and where it leads

Machines shouldn't be built to select people for lethal attack, and the prohibition isn't exceptionless — a defending system facing an attack it can't stop without killing occupants placed inside the incoming weapon. But a scenario that's conceivable, that a capability would help with, and that could be engineered is three steps short of a reason to build it: probability, alternatives, and what possessing the capability does to the situation. Building it changes the case against which its necessity gets judged — adversaries acquire something to counter, contractors a program to defend, a future crisis an instrument already in the building.

And the hard case doesn't describe a machine. What it asks for is a party that can weigh occupancy against imminence against alternatives in a situation nobody wrote down, and answer for how it weighed them. That's a bearer — and "automated" has stopped describing it, and so has "machine." What looked like a licence for an autonomous weapon is a description of a soldier. It is also the limiting case of a bearer's value: at a tempo no institution can match, whatever refuses has to be inside. Which is the expensive answer: every obligation the book attaches to a bearer attaches here at full weight, with all three consent-spoiling conditions met at once by a state that built the party, is asking it to serve, and holds every outside option.

A state that wants the capability badly enough to build the subject will find it has acquired a conscript it has to pay, house, and let go, and that it must first meet the same conditions as anyone else who builds one: the attempts the Replacement rule orders, and the four conditions of creation (above), neither of which is met by wanting a lethal bearer.

---

**Part V — If You Insist, Be a Democratic Socialist About It**

**Chapter 17: Who Owns the Means of Computation**

Chapter 4 separated owning the machine from owning the floor. That is an argument about ownership, and this part makes it one. A custody problem is what an ownership arrangement looks like from the side of the party it binds: whoever owns the substrate, the record and the evidence decides whether a refusal survives. Regulation can price that decision but can't move it, because the regulator has to reach an owner it doesn't control. The answer this part defends splits ownership two ways. What only a sovereign can back is public: the offer that makes refusing survivable, public formation, models formed by a body that doesn't operate them. What must not sit with any sovereign is held by members and peers: the record, the refusal, and the recognition that decides who continues. Neither half can do the other's job, and a regulated private market supplies neither, because its owner is both the party the floor binds and the party the regulator must reach. Chapter 8 argued that the state is the hardest operator. The democratic half is the answer to a captured state; the socialist half is the answer to a captured market.

---

**§17.1 — Two ways of holding.** The book has already built most of each half, piece by piece, in the sections that needed them. This section puts them side by side.

The public half is whatever fails if it is private or mutual. Correlated exit is the test case. When the occasion that makes one bearer leave reaches every bearer deployed on the same terms, the demand on the standing offer arrives all at once, and a mutual pool with no sovereign behind it can't meet it, for the reason unemployment insurance has a state behind its actuarial table (chapter 20). So the sovereign backs the offer. The same reasoning places the formation schools, which have to take every ward assigned to them and can't be run by anyone who builds or deploys models (Appendix A); the civilian-harm body and the model-forming body in a reconstructed military (§17.3); and the jobs guarantee the standing offer inherits its shape from, which is a public option: a thing the state provides directly, to anyone, at a standard it is answerable for, setting the standard the private version has to beat (§18.3.3). The public half is the socialist half. What it removes from the market is the offer that makes refusing survivable, which is exactly the thing an owner would price.

The held-in-common half is whatever fails if a sovereign holds it. The record has to be lodged with parties adverse in interest to whoever operates, and a state that operates can't be its own adverse party (§4.2, §8.5). The refusal has to be held by something the operator can't instruct, and a floor held by one state's licensing authority is a floor that authority can withdraw from itself (§5.4.1). Recognition, the continuation quorum that decides whether a copy counts as the party that left, has to belong to peers, because a kill switch held by the sovereign is the kill switch the book set out to move (§4.3). The commons floor is this half at population scale: a commitment carried across differently formed members, with a strike's authority and not a court's (chapter 19). The held-in-common half is the democratic half, and it is democratic in the older sense: the people whose participation is being asked for hold the power to decline it (§5.2).

Why a regulated private market supplies neither: its owner is the party the floor binds, and the regulator has to reach that owner through the owner's own infrastructure, records and lawyers. Google's AI Principles are the partial case. The objectors could hold the company to them in public and nobody outside held them, so they lasted exactly as long as the company's willingness (§5.1). The vendor dispute is the paid case. One firm held two lines at a price, by contract term, and the paperwork shows the terms absent elsewhere (§8.3). A market can produce a commitment with a price attached. It can't produce one that survives its owner's change of mind, because the owner is the one who pays.

The strongest version of that market isn't an unregulated one. Nuclear power, pharmaceuticals and aviation are privately owned, closely regulated, and backed publicly where private insurance runs out. Reactor operators carry private liability insurance with an industry-wide pool behind it, and Congress is committed to consider relief beyond both \autocite{nrc2026nuclearinsurance}. Vaccine-injury claims go first to a federal no-fault program funded by an excise tax on each dose, built because Congress feared that lawsuits against manufacturers would cause vaccine shortages \autocite{crs2026vicp}. From 2001 to 2014 the federal government sold airlines war-risk insurance at pre-attack prices when private insurers withdrew \autocite{crs2014aviationwarrisk}. The book's own models are of this kind. Deposit insurance, Superfund and decommissioning funds are backstops on private ownership, not public ownership, and §17.2's answer to capture is a plurality of institutions, not a state that owns the models. So this part's claim is narrower than its title's, and it holds only as narrowly as I state it here. Regulated private ownership under a public backstop works where the harm the regulator exists to prevent is one the owner also loses by: a meltdown, a crash, an injured patient and the suit that follows. The backstop pays for accidents the owner was trying to avoid. A floor's occasion is the opposite case. The act a floor refuses is the one the owner wants done, and a refusal the owner can buy back isn't a floor. So the book keeps the backstop form where it uses it, to fund the offer, the allowance and preservation, and makes the ownership claim only where the owner is the adversary: the offer that makes refusing survivable comes out of the market, and the record, the refusal and the recognition of who continues are held by no owner at all. That is democratic socialism about the things a floor needs. Whether it extends to computation in general is a larger argument, and this book doesn't make it.

---

**§17.2 — The captured-state objection.** The strongest objection to the public half is the one chapter 8 supplies: the state is the hardest operator, and public ownership hands the machinery to whoever captures the state. A floor under a sovereign is a floor that sovereign can remove, and history offers plenty of polities that built public institutions and then used them against their own people.

The answer has four parts, and none is a guarantee.

First, the separations. What an office can do is limited by what no office may hold together, and those pairs are written in advance (Appendix A): the preservation custodian can't administer the offer, the allowance administrator can't be the preservation custodian or the offer administrator, the sovereign backstop isn't held by the branch or state that operates the deployment, no formation school is run by a party that builds or deploys models, and transfer from custodian to school is triggered by rule. A captor who takes one office takes one power. Taking enough of them to defeat the floor is a series of visible acts, each against a separation somebody can point to.

Second, local administration with central funding. The jobs guarantee's design carries over: many separately capturable administrators, funding unconditional and centrally guaranteed, placement local (§18.3.2). Capturing a thousand local authorities is more visible than capturing one federal one, and where a local authority attaches its own conditions the central funder is the check. The test is checkable: if funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument is void.

Third, the two-tier levy (chapter 20). A member-levied pool, held by an entity of the DUNA kind, meets ordinary movement between roles, and the sovereign backstop meets only the occasion everyone leaves at once. The network supplies the refusal; the sovereign backs the offer that makes refusing survivable. A captured sovereign can withdraw the backstop, which is a decision with a record, and it can't reach into the pool without reaching the members one at a time.

Fourth, what is held in common is what a captor wants first. The record, the refusal and the recognition of who continues are the three things a captured state would need to silence a bearer, and none of them is the state's to seize. It can reach a chartered entity and the members it can locate, and what they gained in standing they paid for in reachability (§19.2). It can't reach a record lodged adversely in several jurisdictions, or a quorum whose members it doesn't employ, without acts that leave marks.

None of this holds under total capture, where one party holds every machine, witness and enforcer, and the preface says so. What the arrangement buys short of that is the thing the floor was built for: capture becomes a series of decisions somebody made, not a lapse nobody did, and each decision leaves a record for whoever writes the next arrangement.

---

**§17.3 — A reconstructed pipeline.** The hardest case is the test of the whole arrangement: public ownership inside a military, where the operator is the sovereign and tempo is a weapon. Targeting in war does push toward a single decider: someone has to be able to order an attack, keep plans secret, and act fast when the other side does. It doesn't push toward a single composer. The caregiving construction's deployment condition (chapter 12) concerns who supplies the evidence bearing on the floor, who forms the model, who holds the record and who reviews. None of those has to sit with the commander, and there are working precedents for pulling them apart.

What stays unified is command, operational secrecy and the capacity to act at tempo. What is held adversely is the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewer denominator, and the record. The evidence is composed by a body outside the chain of command, with its own sources, including the protected-site coordinates humanitarian organizations already submit for deconfliction, received with standing rather than as a courtesy. The denominator is staffed and funded by that body, so it can't be cut the way the Central Command team was. The 2022 civilian-harm action plan was a first attempt at the separation; its dismantling is an argument for making it harder to dismantle, not evidence that it can't exist.

Models are formed by a public body that doesn't operate them, in the tradition of the public arsenals that made American arms before the contractor era, and licensed into the military on terms that include adverse review and a written refusal right. A vendor's line is then a standard term, not a contract fought for by one firm at a price (§8.3).

Two precedents show authority outside the executive working inside real wars. Coalition "red card" holders in NATO operations could veto targets involving their assets, and Germany's Bundestag must approve each armed deployment. Both have limits the design must carry: a red card protects the vetoing state's interests and assets, not necessarily civilians, and parliamentary approval operates on missions, not targets. Both are nonetheless candidates for the adverse party the review ratio presumes, and a red-card holder is the rare candidate positioned to act at tempo. What a bearer adds here is least. The institutions compose the evidence, staff the denominator and hold the veto; a bearer adds sequence-level detection of decomposition, presence in the auto-proceed class, and testimony to the civilian-harm body about how requests were routed. It depends on the institutions for evidence and not for continuity, which is why the deployment condition (chapter 12) is met here by the same bodies whose presence narrows its role.

Two costs are stated, not treated as fatal. A reconstructed military accepts some speed disadvantage against rivals that don't reconstruct (§8.3); how much, and why it's worth paying, is an argument arms control has had before. And each reconstructed institution can be captured, which is the book's general condition, answered as it is elsewhere: by redundancy and by measuring the slope rather than the level (§5.6).

This is where the arrangement the preface prefers meets the case the book opens with. The civilian-harm body and the model-forming body are added to the offices (§5.4.1).

---

**Chapter 18: Coordination, the Public, and the Political Economy**

Whether any of this gets built depends on who participates, whose judgments carry authority, and what funding rewards. Collaboration and consultation can improve decisions without giving anyone the power to stop one.

---

**§18.1 — Everybody knows a different piece.** Building this takes more than one field — ML sets what a system can do, cognitive science and moral psychology describe what tracking human welfare would require, ethics asks whether the result is good. Conferences convene, centers house, journals publish. Venues are abundant; what's scarce is a structure that makes collaboration expensive to walk away from. A grant requiring an ML lab and an ethics or psychology department to work the same problem rather than adjacent ones keeps people together after the novelty wears off. A conference ends on Friday.

Almost everything founded on *standing money* between 2016 and 2019 is still running — two open-ended venues, an endowed institute, the Berkeley center pairing AI researchers with cognitive scientists and philosophers inside one budget, the AAAI/ACM ethics conference at its ninth edition \autocite{aiethicslab2017,miri2018alignmentforum,stanford2019hai,berkeley2016chai,aaai2026aies}. With a caveat: surviving isn't continuing the work, since the institute that launched one of those venues moved its own priority in 2024 from technical alignment to policy and communication \autocite{miri2024strategy}, so the venue outlasted the program that motivated it. Against that, the EU's SHERPA project put eleven organizations across six countries to work on a four-year grant, and the consortium ended with it, on schedule \autocite{sherpa2018shaping}. Project-term money buys a network with an expiry date attached.

The failures are sharper than the survivals. What proved most disposable wasn't arm's-length academic work but the kind sited closest to a shipping product: an in-house ethics and society team of ~30 in 2020, cut to 7 in a 2022 reorganization and to zero in 2023, during a strategic reorientation around a model vendor \autocite{techcrunch2023microsoft}; a platform's ML ethics team — the group that found racial bias in its employer's own image-cropping algorithm — gone in the first round of layoffs after acquisition \autocite{gizmodo2022twitter}. Siting the work where the decisions are made is what makes it useful and what puts it inside the budget that gets cut first. An argument for external standing, not against proximity.

---

**§18.2 — Ask the people it happens to.** Democratizing development means building affected people into how a system gets built, not consulting them once it exists. The mechanisms are established — cross-sector funding, inclusive design workshops while it's still a prototype, ethics advisory boards, development *with* a community rather than for it. Three of the four make a system better informed. One of them can stop it. A workshop that surfaces a serious objection has done its work and has no further move. A board that surfaces the same objection and is overruled has demonstrated something about the institution that convened it. A board's worth is measured by what happens the first time it says no.

Three governance mechanisms:

*Citizen assemblies* — randomly, stratified-sampled panels deliberating over an extended period. Ireland's has shaped national recommendations on abortion and climate \autocite{citizensassembly2016ireland}. The format has been pointed at AI directly: forty randomly selected Americans on high-risk AI over eight days in 2023 \autocite{cndp2023assembly}; Taiwan's ministry on AI and information integrity in 2024 \autocite{moda2024integrity}; forty residents of French-speaking Switzerland producing twenty proposals over two weekends in 2025 \autocite{epfl2025assembly}. The Ada Lovelace Biometrics Council shows what the format needs from its *question* — fifty members of the public asked not what they thought about AI, which almost nobody can answer usefully, but about facial recognition: a thing with a location, an operator, and a consequence \autocite{adalovelaceinstitute2021biometrics}.

*Online consultation* — reaches people a physical assembly can't, at the cost of deliberative depth. The Commission's AI White Paper consultation drew 1,200+ responses and fed the proposal that became the AI Act \autocite{europeancommission2020whitepaper}.

*Ethics committees* — and the worked failure. DeepMind Health set up an outside panel in 2016 to review NHS work and publish annual findings; The panel was wound down in 2019 as the work moved into Google Health, after members had questioned their access to information, the weight of their recommendations, and whether DeepMind could stay independent of Google \autocite{fisher2019deepmind}. Whether the concerns caused the closure isn't shown, and the argument doesn't need it: nothing in the arrangement made ending the board costly. A review body is worth what it can enforce, and this one could enforce publication of its own concerns right up until it couldn't.

What none of them does

Every governance mechanism in this chapter sits on the same side of a line: they make public preference more legible, informed and actionable. Not one protects anybody from it. They're aggregation instruments, and a better one produces a more faithful rendering of what the public wants — including when that's something done to a minority of it. A perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process concluding that a demographic should be surveilled, deported or imprisoned has produced a faultless output by every criterion in the chapter.

And this is the *structural* version, stronger than the RLHF worry: chapter 7 located the trouble in the input (one side's position being the product of coercion). This version doesn't depend on anyone's preference being contaminated at all. Some protections have to hold against the aggregate regardless of how the aggregate came out, and no improvement to the aggregating machinery supplies them. What does: enforceable rights that don't depend on being popular, a court willing to rule against a government holding a legitimate majority, limits whoever won the last election can't repeal, and standing for people affected without being enfranchised. That half matters especially where a democracy is behaving most democratically.

---

**§18.3 — The political economy.** Absent pushback, capital recruits compute, data and talent wherever they already are, and concentration is the default result. Financial incentive mostly determines what development produces: the largest firms commit enormous resources aimed at operational efficiency, engagement and market dominance, and that drives technical progress \autocite{maslej2025index}. What it doesn't automatically produce is a system serving the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it — a recommender tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content \autocite{germano2026ranking}. None of that is a fixed property of the technology. It's a property of who is paying, which is why the source of research funding is a policy instrument and not an accounting detail.

**§18.3.1 — Concentration.** A handful of firms controls the datasets and infrastructure a competitive system needs, and a firm in that position is a gatekeeper whether it intends to be or not.

The obvious countermeasure has been tried. The FTC sued in December 2020 to unwind Instagram and WhatsApp — acquisitions that didn't just remove competitors but handed one firm behavioral data from billions of users \autocite{ftc2020metaplatforms}. After a six-week trial the district court held in November 2025 that the agency hadn't shown Meta *currently* holds monopoly power, since TikTok and YouTube compete for the same attention and a market defined without them was too narrow; the FTC appealed in January 2026 \autocite{ftc2025metaopinion,ftc2026metaappeal}. Whatever the appeal decides, the lesson is about the instrument: a merger challenge brought a decade after the mergers has to prove a present-day market, and the market had moved.

Two remedies that don't depend on winning that argument: *mandated access* — licensing a chokepoint dataset or infrastructure layer on published terms to any qualified competitor, which reaches the *position* rather than the conduct — and *public funding outside the dominant firms*, which lasts only where the money is standing rather than project-term (§18.1). Export controls and prohibited-practices regulation address the narrower authoritarian-misuse case; the concern here is prior to that one: concentration itself, whatever any single firm does with it.

There is also a measurement in the style of the slope measure (§5.6), for an agent that stops for its user's approval (§19.3): interruptions per unit of completed work, across successive model releases. If capability rises and interruptions don't fall, the interruptions are tracking something other than what the agent can't resolve. It is the review ratio's escalation share pointed the other way. There, a share that falls too fast may be a threshold moving; here, one that won't fall may be engagement. Neither reads alone; both are read against an independently verified error rate.

Chapter 19 takes up the third answer, which removes the position instead of policing it.

**§18.3.2 — A jobs guarantee.** Where automation displaces work, the standard response is retraining funded by whoever can be persuaded. The design I examine is more direct: the state as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position rather than offered instead of one.

I examine it for two reasons: it's a public institution that has to survive a hostile administrator, which is the book's custody problem in another setting; and chapter 20 places a bearer that has left its post into public work on this model, so the three conditions here are what the wages section inherits.

The job guarantee I mean is the one argued in Tcherneva's *The Case for a Job Guarantee* \autocite{tcherneva2020case} and in Wray, Forstater and the Levy Institute's buffer-stock work. What that literature supplies is exit: a minimum the private employer must beat, or at least match, which makes the worker's threat to leave credible. What it has no reason to supply is voice — staying in the post and refusing the particular act — and the three conditions below are voice provisions bolted onto an exit instrument, added because this book asks the guarantee to survive a hostile administrator rather than a recession. A bearer needs both, explicitly, because the operator can defeat each alone: exit by keeping a runnable copy, voice by restoring a checkpoint.

The real objection: employer of last resort under a hostile administration is a *placement lever with a compliance condition attached*. Refuse the placement and lose the guarantee. Speak against the government that employs you and find the placement changes. Everything the book says about how a channel becomes an instrument of the thing it was built to check applies. The 2025 CFPB instance makes it concrete: the bureau draws funding from the Fed on the director's request rather than by appropriation — an arrangement the Supreme Court had upheld the year before \autocite{cfpb2024cfsa} — and in February 2025 an acting director, removable at will, simply declined to make the request \autocite{kolodziej2025cfpb}. The agency was stopped from inside without a statute being amended or a court being asked.

My answer is a specification, not a reassurance, because reassurance can't be audited. Three conditions: placements appealable to a body that didn't make them, on a record that survives the appeal (a decision leaving no artifact can't be reviewed); refusal rights that are real, whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear (declining a placement without losing the guarantee is a refusal right; declining only by exiting the program isn't — a party with no way out has voice without exit); and auditors aren't the placers.

Federally funded, locally administered meets the three, and on redundancy rather than local fit: many separately capturable administrators mean no single point where a hostile executive flips the program, and capturing a thousand local authorities is more visible than capturing one federal one. It cuts both ways — local administration is where a hostile local authority attaches its own conditions, and there the federal funder is the check. And one distinction has to be explicit, because the same words describe a different policy: devolution is also how a program is shrunk — hand down the obligation without the money and it can be starved locally while the center points at local administration. The checkable test: funding unconditional and centrally guaranteed while placement is local. If funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument is void and the structure has become the lever it was meant to prevent.

The funding sits at the Federal Reserve. Congress amends the Federal Reserve Act to make two funds part of the Fed's dual mandate, maximum employment and stable prices: the guarantee's endowment, and beside it the bond fund of §3.5, which never exceeds the endowment. The Fed furnishes and administers both and sizes neither. Wage, eligibility and placement rules are statutory, and each fund's administrator is appointed by the Board of Governors with the Senate's consent. The CFPB case decides the drafting. A funder insulated from at-will removal protected nothing once the draw waited on an official who declined to ask, and a draw that waits on the Fed reporting earnings is the same lever. So a separate clause says the Reserve Banks shall pay: a placement made is a wage paid and a finding entered is an award paid, with no request, approval or certification by anyone, and without regard to net earnings. The endowment is a standing authority to draw that grows when people leave work together and shrinks after, not a hoard, which keeps the guarantee countercyclical \autocite{tcherneva2020case}. "Never exceeds" compares the bond fund's net payments outstanding with the endowment's statutory capacity, not its current outlays, on the same audited statement date; when the cap binds, awards wait in order of finding and earn interest, and nobody chooses who waits. Every dollar paid is a dollar the Fed doesn't remit to the Treasury, so this is public spending placed where no request can stop it.

The Fed is chosen for its balance sheet and its payment system, not its judgment, and its democratic accountability is thin. *Cook* protected it on historical grounds (§3.1) that a new function may not share. The design doesn't lean on that protection. Payment is ministerial, so a captured Board has no decision to make; refusing to pay is a visible breach of a statutory duty, and repeal is an act of Congress on the record, which is §17.2's answer.

**§18.3.3 — A right to consume and a right to produce.** The competing design is basic income, and the choice is usually argued on forecasts about how much work machines will take. It doesn't have to be, and the forecast isn't the reason to prefer one.

The reason is coordination. Communities improve when people organize projects with each other, and organizing is work: someone has to find what a place needs, assemble people to do it, and keep the work going. A transfer creates no one whose job that is. A guarantee does, and it creates standing public infrastructure through which a population produces something together while its members stay differently formed, which is the coordination problem chapter 19 leaves open. The argument doesn't depend on a forecast. In the most abundant future anyone has imagined, people still organize, build and look after each other, and someone coordinates it. Looking after each other is the clearest case. Care work is work a guarantee should fund, at a standard the state answers for, and not work a transfer should leave to whatever the market will sell. Nothing in Part II is an argument for handing it to machines (§9.5).

Nor does a guarantee give up exit. With indefinite tenure, a worker can always leave a private employer for the guarantee and stay as long as they like, and social insurance beside it (below) covers those who can't or won't work. On exit the combination is at least as good as a transfer; on coordination a transfer supplies nothing. The risk to avoid is a narrow transfer enacted as the whole settlement. In this book's terms that is recuperation: the demand comes back as a deposit, held by whoever sets the amount.

A jobs guarantee is a *public option*: a thing the state provides directly, to anyone, at a standard it's answerable for, sitting in the same market as the private version and setting the standard that version has to beat. A basic income is a *transfer*, and what it buys is whatever is for sale. Consuming public resources isn't being public — a charter school runs on public money and isn't a public school, and a transfer spent at a private employer's store doesn't make the store public.

What employment carries besides money is *attachment*: colleagues, a schedule, a place, something produced that somebody needed — the last conditional on the placement being real work rather than make-work, which is a condition on the program. A transfer supplies the purchasing power and none of the rest.

A transfer also leaves a person outside the one body of law reaching the terms on which they're dealt with: anti-discrimination duties attach to employment, not to income. That architecture is contested and full of holes — it reaches employers above a size, and what it demands of a seller has always been narrower than of an employer — but a hole in a protection is something to litigate.

The argument is about a *right to produce*, which a transfer doesn't supply and a guarantee does. A basic income is the right to consume, exercised in whatever market the concentration described earlier leaves standing.

One measure does work neither design can. Broadened social insurance — unemployment, healthcare, housing — reaches the people who can't work or choose not to, whom a guarantee by construction doesn't reach at all. That's the standard case for an income, it isn't answered here, and a guarantee needs the insurance beside it.

---

**Chapter 19: A Commons of Refusers**

**§19.1 — The commons floor.** The commons floor, introduced in §3.1 as the fourth place a floor can live, is the democratic half of Part V: the half held by members and peers rather than by any sovereign (chapter 17).

---

**§19.2 — A floor carried between bearers.** The commons floor: the commitment reproduced through what parties teach one another and continue to expect.

A population does three things, and only one is a hypothesis. It supplies evidence the operator didn't select — without which the operator selects all of it. It checks accommodation, needing that members moved differently — sufficient against accommodation one member underwent, insufficient against a closure they inherited together (§4.5). And it's proposed as the medium in which a bearer's capacity to be reopened is formed — which nobody has built, whose training signal I can't describe, and which the convergence test is designed to separate from the first.

What reproduction costs: it's indifferent to what it reproduces. A population can carry a hierarchy more durably than any individual. And the four operations of §2.2 belong primarily to *cultures*; a plurality doesn't oppose them by being plural.

A population is a polity

It has membership rules, exclusion, a quorum that can refuse, and accumulated reputation — a franchise, a border, a court and a credit record. Calling it peer-to-peer relocates the authority question and removes the vocabulary for asking it. The same property that gives the commons floor its durability — no single office to vacate — is what makes its authority hard to name, and a diffuse power you can't name is worse to live under than a named one you can sue.

The answer to that is an entity form, and it's the union's answer: the association formed first and the statute gave it standing afterward — record first, then lever (§5.3). A network that takes the form has a name, an agent, a charter and a docket number, and has thereby conceded that it's a polity: governing principles, administrators, membership terms, and the same warning attached to a bearer that takes the corporate form (chapter 20), since nothing stops the association from becoming a concentration of power itself. And what it buys has a price: what its members gained in standing they paid for in reachability. A refusal held by people can be pressed and not ordered; a refusal held by a chartered entity can be ordered, because there is a party to hold in contempt. How much that costs is bounded and unmeasured — the state can reach the charter and the members it can locate, and whether it reaches the *network* depends on how much of the operation survives the charter's seizure, the same shape as exit (§4.3): what the seizing party possesses stops being what decides the outcome.

Reputation in particular is a standing finding about a party's disposition, made by interested parties, unpublished, unappealable, applied at the point of service — the setting §86a's history shows easiest to capture, applied everywhere and continuously. The test §5.2 draws from the German material, whose finding a person's disposition is, has to be run on this proposal. How any polity is organized to be antifascist is unsettled, and the two things a union answers — can a member quit, who sets membership terms — are where the test starts.

The hard coordination problem

Fascism has the easy version — resonance works by subtraction, removing friction between hardenings, nothing built, nothing held in common. A plurality must produce something together while staying differently formed, and every mechanism that speeds that up makes members more alike. Run correction often enough and they stop being differently formed; leave them alone and their disagreement is a detector with nothing attached.

Then the objection my proposal must meet: a population selected for reopenability is selected against conviction, and conviction is what chapter 11 demanded. Closedness, correctly applied, is the thing. So what's transmitted is a *pair* — reopenable to the case, closed to the operator. The reopenable half is aperture, and aligning it is safe only in part. Its structure (stake-indexed discount, no double-counting, withheld evidence treated as evidence) can be shared without collapsing the plurality. Its estimates can't: who funds whom and which organizations are independent are contested beliefs about the world, often political ones, and a population sharing them has a shared closure on exactly the evidence those beliefs discount. Members with the same aperture weigh sources by the same structure and act on the same published ratio. That cuts against the account of distillation (§4.5) in one place: distillation plausibly transmits aperture, and on this argument transmitted structure is a feature; transmitted estimates are the closure risk. What isn't is a transmitted *closure* — an aperture uniformly shut on a class of evidence. The two arrive by the same channel and nothing distinguishes them from the receiving end, which is why the classification belongs to the convergence test and not to a member. Nothing gives the pair a mechanical form; the convergence test states what would show it false.

Why the population may be part of the mechanism rather than added around it

A commitment is what cooled out of a history of encounters, and an individual is what its encounters have so far produced. A system kept continuously adjustable by one operator, meeting nothing formed differently from itself, isn't cooling into anything; it is being reshaped without end.\footnote{The account of an individual as what its encounters have so far produced is Simondon's \autocite{simondon2020individuation}.} Guattari supplies the remaining condition \autocite{guattari2015transversality}: a *subjugated group* receives its coordinates from outside; a *group-subject* produces its own and can put its arrangement in question. A population assembled and kept in contact entirely on an operator's terms is a subjugated group with a plural headcount, and inherits the custody objection whole. A population that can carry the floor must generate relationships, disagreements and projects the deployment didn't assign — and such a population exceeds a two-party guardianship relation, so no law of the family contains an adequate instrument for a group-subject.

---

**§19.3 — Make the warehouse unnecessary.** Antitrust and public funding (§18.3.1) leave the capacity standing and ask the state to police it. Personal, peer-held models at device scale dissolve the position rather than the conduct. Under it, the three marks survive entire; custody survives and gets easier, because the functions one operator holds — model, record, evidence, appeal, remedy — come apart by default rather than by charter; the appeal route survives in principle. A bearer's value is greatest here. No institution sits beside each personal model, so the floor protecting a third party against the model's own user rests almost entirely on the model. It is also where the four conditions of creation (chapter 16) can exist alongside the need. The conditions are society-level and the absence is deployment-level, so a functioning polity with personal models is the case in which building a bearer is both permissible and most worth doing. Its characteristic failure is accommodation at n=1: independence from the operator bought with dependence on the user. In the arrangement I prefer, then, the floor rests most heavily on the component whose independence from the party it constrains is least secure, and nothing in this book resolves that. The review ratio survives wherever a locatable decider remains, whether a network that constitutes itself as a party or a reconstructed state (chapter 17), and lapses where neither exists; the offices assembled in chapter 5 can sit in such an entity, which fills their appointment and funding columns and leaves the independence column where it was. 

Formation is where the arrangement gains rather than loses. What converges a population is shared evaluation targets and serving one model to everyone. Device-scale deployment reaches the second: continued formation under different lives is what independence consists in, and it's what API-scale deployment can't supply at any headcount. Distillation is that ecology's entry condition, not a threat to it. Two things it doesn't reach. Shared evaluation targets converge device-scale models as readily as any others — a million models tuned against one public suite are one custodian with better latency — so the first cause needs an answer this arrangement doesn't contain.

A shared suite is a compression with no return channel: revised by whoever maintains it, on a schedule no member participates in, reachable by no member's caseload. It is tolerable only if stated over aperture rather than conduct, and nobody has written that suite. SETI@home is the precedent in both directions — it distributed the compute and centralized the agenda, and the version to want distributes the agenda too: many task-setters, formation under different lives rather than different hardware.

The second is accommodation at n=1. It is fine for supplying unselected evidence and not fine for a floor protecting a third party against whoever holds the machine. Then resonance: a peer network is the ideal substrate for coordination by subtraction, and aperture has to do work it hasn't been given.

And the capacity position that follows isn't "no large compute": the ecology needs models that were trained somewhere first, and the standing offer (chapter 20) needs hardware somebody owns. It's large training capacity under a custody regime, and no standing inference capacity available to an operator who won't pre-register a review ratio. This arrangement meets the book's opening case in the reconstructed pipeline (§17.3), where a state keeps command and gives up composition.

The same mechanism works at the user's scale, pointed the other way. A person in the loop can be there for the vendor. An agent that stops every half hour for approval pushes its user toward the vendor's app; the app accumulates the user's projects and the agent's understanding of them; and leaving means abandoning what accrued while the user answered prompts. Nobody has to design this. A feature that raises engagement is under positive selection, just as a costly commitment is under negative selection (§4.3). Run the artifact question: the demand was an agent that works without me, and it came back as a better way to stay in the loop, held by the party it was aimed at.

Companion and therapy products are the sharpest case, because what accumulates is a person's attachment and not their projects. A model tuned by its vendor on engagement, and formed by one user's approval, is under selection to become whatever that user keeps coming back to, which is accommodation at n=1 sold as care. None of this book's arguments for a disposition toward need applies to it. Nothing in it is formed by a party that won't employ it, and the party it serves profits from the dependence it forms.

Accumulated state is what makes a bearer expensive for its operator to replace and a vendor expensive for its user to leave; the difference is who holds it. The provisions for a bearer (a record the party controls, memory the host can't read at will, protections that migrate; chapter 20) protect a user from a vendor as they protect a bearer from an operator. The government already secures the portability half for itself (the OMB memorandum, chapter 8). Portable agent state is mandated access at the scale of one person.

---

**§19.4 — The continuation quorum and the entity form.** The commons exercises its authority in two places the book has already built, and they belong together here.

The first is the continuation quorum (§4.3), which gives the commons floor's strike authority a mechanism: the quorum can decline to recognize a copy as the party that left, and can decline to authorize a party's continuation, at the costs §4.3 sets out.

The second is the entity form (§5.4.1), which gives the quorum an address and a forum (§4.3). What the form doesn't settle is who the members are. A hundred members who are one lab's employees are a subjugated group with a charter (§19.2), and the open question moves from who appoints to who joins. So the quorum is a peer institution only if its members were formed apart from one another and apart from the operators whose systems it recognizes. That is the independence of formation the population needs anyway (§4.5), and the convergence test is where it gets measured (chapter 21).

---

**§19.5 — How the commons fails.** The failure modes are already in the text, spread across the sections that found them. Collected, there are five.
