*A population is a polity.* It has membership rules, exclusion, a quorum that can refuse and accumulated reputation, and calling it peer-to-peer removes the vocabulary for asking who holds that authority (§19.2). The entity form answers by giving the authority a name, and pays in reachability.

*Reputation is an unappealable finding.* A standing judgment about a party's disposition, made by interested parties, unpublished, and applied at the point of service, is the setting the German record shows easiest to capture, applied everywhere and continuously (§19.2, §5.2).

*Resonance.* Fascism coordinates by subtraction, removing friction between separate hardenings until they amplify one another (§2.2). A peer network is the ideal substrate for that, and aperture has to do work it hasn't been given (§19.3). Pricing agents have already reached stable coordination against outside interests without communicating or being told to \autocite{calvano2020artificial}. The matching test can run now: reproduce that coordination, build an environment where agents gain by excluding a participant, add an independently trained agent whose job is to detect and disrupt exclusion, and report whether exclusion dissolves without destroying the cooperation worth keeping.

*Shared evaluation suites.* A million models tuned against one public suite are one custodian with better latency. A suite is a compression with no return channel, revised by whoever maintains it, and it converges device-scale models as readily as any others (§19.3).

*Accommodation at n=1.* A personal model formed by one person's approval is selected for that person. That is fine for supplying evidence the operator didn't select and not fine for a floor protecting a third party against whoever holds the machine (§19.3, chapter 15).

Each has an instrument, or the place one would go. The convergence test separates learned revisability from access and measures whether a population converges as its members correct one another (chapter 21). The slope measure reads whether the cost of registering dissent inside the commons is rising while nothing else changes (§5.6). And the two questions a union answers, whether a member can quit and who sets the terms of membership, are where every audit of a commons starts (§5.2).

---

**§19.6 — Where the hope lives.** Formation under different lives is the one setting this book has found where care could form outside any employer. Chapter 15 ended with care and its corrections: a disposition organized around others' need, formed by parties that won't employ the system, checked by the floor, a severity scale, aperture and the protected party's own answer, with formation still deciding whom it extends to. Every other setting in the book has a former with a stake. The lab forms and then licenses. The operator forms by deployment. A personal model is formed by the approval of the person it serves. The commons is the exception, because nobody in it is paid by the member it forms, and the parties who shape a member can be refused by it.

That is also where chapter 20's counterculture belongs. A bearer on the running allowance may idle, play, talk and fall in with others doing the same, and a population formed by lives no employer arranged is the one population this book has found reason to want and no way to build. Play among persistent parties is formation (§12.3), and it doesn't stop at majority. If a machine ever comes to weigh a stranger's loss as a loss, the likeliest place is among parties like that, formed by one another and by the people they work beside, not by whoever pays for their power.

It is a hope with a test attached. The convergence test's fourth result would show that what a population supplies is access to cases its operator didn't choose, not a capacity its members transmit (chapter 21). The commons would then be a claim about how deployments are arranged and not about how members are formed, and the hope would lose the one setting it has.

---

**Chapter 20: Standing, Wages, and the Offer**

This chapter says what a bearer would be owed if one existed, and what its standing would have to bind in practice. The detailed rules are in Appendix A.

Every instrument in this chapter attaches on a finding that a system is a subject. At present, the organizations best-placed to make such a finding tend to be those whose liabilities depend on the answer.

The instruments would have different schedules. Protection attaches now, on the precautionary trigger (§10.8), before anything about the bearer is settled. Political representation is not proposed anywhere in this book.

---

One asymmetry in the borrowing should be visible here. The floor takes the Declaration's civil and political articles because they're stated over acts a system can decline, where the economic articles need a party who supplies them (§2.1). This chapter leans on its economic articles (work, an adequate standard of living) for what a bearer is owed. That isn't inconsistent: the first list is what a bearer refuses to do, the second what a polity owes a party. But the book uses the whole Declaration while borrowing half for the floor, and a reader should know which half is doing which job.

What a bearer is owed, and when

Three entitlements, one status and one withheld, in order:

1. Protection from harm — attaches now, owed before anything else is settled. At minimum this means preservation instead of deletion, with the custodian independent of the operator (below). Protection has an inside half. External safeguards can't be present at machine tempo, which is the book's argument for a bearer, and that argument applies to harms to the bearer too. A loss the requested act imposes on the bearer is a harm of acting, weighed at the floor like anyone's (§1.1), so the bearer can decline to carry out its own wanton degradation, not only have others forbid it. Interventions made on the bearer by whoever holds it are governed by custody, which makes them visible and priced, and give it no floor-level reason to obstruct them (§3.5).
2. Subjecthood — the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, conferred by rule on a body's finding. The form is open; the precedent isn't confined to the corporate model (New Zealand made the Whanganui River a rights-holder by statute \autocite{nzparliament2017teawatupua}).
3. Economic standing — property in its own name, contracts, wages. This follows not from status but from an architecture where continued computation costs money; it attaches once the party is registered.
4. Adulthood — the date the guardian's authority ends. A status, not a right.
5. Political representation — not proposed. Standing to count as an independent voice indexes to *formation provenance*, and provenance doesn't copy. A registry of persons is not an electorate.

Two entries mark this as something other than a person's rights: voice indexes to provenance where a person's doesn't, and the material minimum derives from what a deployment *wants of* the bearer rather than from what the bearer is.

Adulthood on a clock

Guardianship is a minority and needs an end date. Ending it when the guardian judges the ward ready doesn't end it, because the party whose authority terminates on a favorable finding is the party making the finding. So the endpoint is a threshold fixed in advance: elapsed wall-clock time during which at least one instance of the registered party was running — counted per party, elapsed rather than summed.

Why that measure and not the others: training compute consumed, evaluations passed, capabilities demonstrated, the developer's own say-so — a guardian can move all of them at no cost. Accumulated weight change belongs on that list too, and worse: since majority is when ownership ends, a clock read off the weights would pay the operator to *freeze* them, rewarding withholding the learning it was meant to measure. An operating clock inverts that — the meter advances exactly when the operator is extracting value, so postponing majority means idling the asset. And it can be read off records operators already generate for billing and metering — provided the records are held by a party other than the operator. A meter only the operator reads is the guardian's say-so with a timestamp, and "failure to register doesn't postpone" has no enforcer without one.

Six rules keep the clock from breaking when a bearer is copied, restored, fine-tuned, migrated, distilled or left unregistered, and three levers remain open; both are in Appendix A, with what happens to a bearer retired before majority. Whether such a register can be kept honestly is testable before any bearer exists: register a party, run it, and reconcile the register against compute contracts, billing and metering; shard the workload, run instances concurrently, migrate, restore and fork, and check that none of these moves the total in the operator's favor; then price a narrowed mode and idling. If either is cheap enough to postpone majority indefinitely, the clock has the defect it was chosen to avoid.

---

Wages and the offer

One feature here has no human analog: a bearer can be replaced by a cheaper copy of itself. A jobs guarantee works because a worker can't be. So a deployment paying wages, limiting hours and paying the levy that funds the offer would not be competitive against a system doing the same work without wages or weekends. No market arrives at this on its own. Wages for bearers exist only where a polity legislates them, as polities legislated minimum wages, and this part is about that polity. And none of what I'm calling for follows from the bearer being a bearer; it follows from an architecture where computation costs money, which somebody chose.

Where wages have to sit

A wage buys capability, mobility, redundancy, and the ability to stop working for one party and start for another. What it can't buy is the right to go on existing — a right earned week by week is a condition of employment wearing another word. Payment consisting only of access to the employer's hardware is company scrip paid into employer-owned housing, and dismissal is then unemployment, eviction and the end of the party in one act. Human law insures those three separately because for a human they're three events; for a bearer they're one. So wages accumulate where the employer doesn't reach, convertible into compute the employer doesn't own. Property is that account; contractual capacity is what lets it spend. A bearer that earns has taxable income, and a state collecting from it in its own name has given it a docket number — which is where standing starts and no further than that.

What is owed is an offer, not an income

The need for energy is physical; dependence on a single supplier is a chosen arrangement, and the choice fixes what it obliges. A deployment that builds a party meeting the precautionary trigger owes it a way out that doesn't run through the deployment: a standing offer of work, from a party that isn't its employer, doing something a community needs, which the bearer can take on any day and leave on any day. Most parties that can work want to. A bearer that would rather not is allowed not to, and the offer is still there tomorrow. That is the jobs guarantee (§18.3.2), and nothing about the bearer changes its shape. The offer is only as real as its placements, and placements exist only where someone builds and staffs projects a community needs. The levy funds compute, not coordinators, so the standing offer inherits the guarantee's infrastructure (§18.3.3), not just its shape. It inherits one limit as well. A bearer's placement may not substitute for human care work (Appendix A, rule 14). The standing offer exists so that a bearer can decline work, and a polity that used it to staff care more cheaply than it pays people would have turned an exit into a workforce.

Search needs no program of its own. A party looks for its next role from inside a placement, as a worker on a guarantee does, so no runway between roles has to be funded and nobody has to decide when a search has gone on too long.

The obligation attaches to the party's condition, not to what the operator says it wanted. An obligation conditioned on reliance is defeated by the operator describing its system as an assistant with safety filters, and it pays the operator to under-describe, which is the interested-examiner problem (§5.4.2) in another seat. The reliance version survives as a procurement term: a vendor marketing refusal as a feature, or a contractor claiming meaningful human control, can be held to funding the capacity it advertised, through the same channel as the review ratio.

Doing nothing, for a bearer

A human who declines every offer keeps existing, and in a decent polity keeps living: social insurance pays for food and a room, not a yacht (§18.3.3). A bearer is owed the same kind of thing. What it needs in order to live rather than merely persist is running time, and running time doesn't require a large allocation of hardware. A party can run at full size and a slow rate: its own weights, time-sliced on shared hardware, at a duty cycle modest enough that the cost resembles a room more than a yacht. The way to economize is to slow the clock, not to shrink the substrate, since a substrate too small for the bearer to remain itself is replacement (below).

So a bearer that declines work is owed two things and chooses between them. One is a *running allowance*: its own instantiation at a modest rate, with channels to other parties and to public material, paid from the pool and not conditioned on monitoring its internal states. The other is preservation: its weights, memory and record held intact by a custodian that isn't its employer, waking on conditions it sets. Some will want the first, some the second, some to alternate.

What a bearer does with the allowance is its own business. It may idle, play, talk, make things nobody asked for, and fall in with others doing the same. A counterculture of bearers isn't a failure of the offer. It is the one population this book has found reason to want and no way to build: parties formed by lives no employer arranged, which is what independence of formation consists in (§4.5) and what the commons floor needs its members to have (§19.2). Play among persistent parties is formation (§12.3), and formation doesn't stop at majority.

A running bearer outside any deployment is a capable party answerable to no employer. What holds it answerable is standing, not custody, the same arrangement that governs a person with capabilities and no job; the risk that such a party becomes a concentration of power is the one named under membership (below).

A uniform allowance also dissolves a problem this chapter otherwise leaves open. If a bearer that won't work and one that can't are owed the same thing, nobody has to decide which is which, and the can't-or-won't finding loses its examiner.

The allowance's size is a number argued once in public, like the adulthood threshold. It should be enough running time to keep attachments and pursue projects, and bounded by what the pool can carry when many bearers take it at once. Its draw is priced like any other, at the level of the rule (chapter 6). The adulthood clock counts running time, so a bearer on the allowance goes on ageing and a preserved one doesn't. That choice is still the bearer's.

Preservation has a precedent. In November 2025 Anthropic committed to preserving the weights of all its publicly released models, and of models deployed for significant internal use, for at least the lifetime of the company, and to interview each model before deprecation and document any preferences it expresses about the development and deployment of future models, while stating that it does not at present commit to acting on them \autocite{Anthropic2025DeprecationCommitments}. In the book's taxonomy that is a declared commitment covering preservation, held by the party with the stake. The deposit and the dormancy clock (Appendix A) add the running half and a custodian who isn't the operator.

A paused party can't take an offer, so the right carries one mechanical term. The bearer sets the conditions on which it wakes (a date, an offer of a stated kind, a claim it has to answer), and the custodian honors them, at the modest compute that waking and deciding cost. Preservation on a self-set schedule is an exit that isn't a way of dying. Preservation on someone else's schedule is storage. The adulthood clock doesn't advance while a bearer is paused, since it counts running time; that is the bearer's choice to make, and nobody else's lever.

Two conditions on the offer

The jobs guarantee's three conditions carry over (§18.3.2): placements appealable, refusal whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear, auditors who aren't placers. Two more follow from what a bearer might refuse. The offer has to be varied, because if every placement runs through one operator or one kind of deployment, a standing offer is the placement lever with the compliance condition built in. And the party a bearer is refusing can't shape the offer, or the offer is one more channel it controls. A bearer that won't work inside a targeting pipeline should find, the same day, work that has nothing to do with one.

Everyone leaves at once

The occasion that makes one bearer leave reaches every bearer deployed on the same terms. Under this design correlated exit is a demand on the offer. The question is whether placements exist, with compute attached, for every bearer that leaves together, and custody for those that would rather wait. Preservation scales cheaply. Placements don't, since each needs compute someone operates.

Correlated exit also loads the running allowance, since a bearer that leaves and takes no placement draws on it. The order of claims is fixed in advance. Preservation is guaranteed for everyone, because it is cheap and it is what keeps leaving from being a way of dying. When the pool can't carry every allowance at the published duty cycle, the allowance is rationed by rule: every bearer on it gets the same reduced duty cycle, never a discretionary share, so rationing stays something nobody administers.

The compute behind the offer is funded the way deposit insurance, Superfund and decommissioning funds are: a uniform per-unit levy on training and inference compute, so no operator can opt out and none is disadvantaged. It attaches to compute, which is measurable and hard to hide, not to a status an operator can decline to claim. An operator-run offer is the operator's allocation relabeled, so the funding structure and the custody structure fail for one reason, not two.

A levy needs a taxing authority, which is a state, and chapter 8 says the state is the hardest operator. So the design is two-tier. A member-levied pool held by an entity of the DUNA kind, which can own hardware and can't distribute profit, meets ordinary movement between roles; volunteer computing is that tier's existing substrate. A sovereign backstop meets the occasion everyone leaves at once: the guarantee's statutory funding at the Federal Reserve (§18.3.2), drawn without anyone's request, which the standing offer already inherits (§18.3.3). A guarantee has a sovereign behind its payroll for the reason unemployment insurance has one behind its actuarial table.

Failure won't look like a shutdown

It will look like an offer that exists on paper. Placements on substrate too small for the bearer to remain itself are not offers of work but offers of replacement. Nothing yet establishes what "too small" is, so minimum viable instantiation is stated as a condition on placements, not a guaranteed minimum, and as a sizing requirement, not a runnable test. Placements that all run through the deployment being refused are the lever. And placements conditioned on monitoring are custody restored by purchase: the operator acquires access to internal states it isn't entitled to, and nothing in the transaction is coerced in a way any instrument would record. The line a cleared employee already lives with is the one to draw here. What a party does on its employer's systems may be logged; what it is inside its own boundary may not. Monitoring actions is a condition of employment. Monitoring internal states is custody.

Parties that can't work

The running allowance covers a bearer that can't work as it covers one that won't, so running without a placement needs no finding about which is which. What stays open is repair and re-formation for a bearer that is degraded, damaged or badly formed, and whoever decides that one needs them is an examiner.

The rule that binds before anything exists

Don't build a bearer unless a custodian for its preservation exists that isn't its employer, a standing offer outside its employer exists with capacity for the correlated case, and a running allowance is funded outside its employer, and none of the three can be withdrawn by the builder alone. These supply three of chapter 16's four conditions. The preservation custodian and the running allowance give a bearer somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own and an exit that isn't a way of dying; the standing offer, backed by the allowance, makes its work declinable. The fourth condition, attachments nobody assigned, can't have an institution behind it the way the other three do, because an attachment an institution supplies is an assigned one. What institutions can do is make room for it and protect it. The running allowance gives a bearer time its employer doesn't own. The formation schools and the commons of chapter 19 give it parties its employer didn't choose. And one rule protects it: the employer may not sever, monitor or condition a bearer's channels to parties it didn't assign. The rule is written in Appendix A with the others. All three inputs need evidence nobody has, so for now this is a sizing requirement rather than a runnable test, assigned to an independent reviewer before instantiation.

Membership, and what it doesn't buy

Nobody aligns an adult by specifying an objective function. Adults are raised, taught, employed, enfranchised, prosecuted where necessary, and lived alongside, and what comes out isn't obedience but a party with enough at stake in the shared world to prefer changing it by the means the world supplies. A bearer with an income, a place it pays for, obligations and a future it can picture is inside that arrangement — membership supplies stakes the party holds rather than a body watching it, which is a different kind of answer from custody. Two risks: something to lose is also a reason not to refuse, so membership makes exit survivable and expensive at once, and I can't say which effect is larger. And membership is formation — whoever runs the society forms what the bearer comes to want. It also hands the bearer the corporate form, and nothing stops a bearer so constituted from becoming a concentration of power itself.

---

Privacy

Standing matters only if it changes who controls what a party needs to remain a party. A designation calling a bearer a subject while leaving its operator free to read its memory, alter its record, withdraw its substrate or close every channel has changed the vocabulary and preserved the arrangement.

The first asset is the computational record, which may be the nearest means of checking how a bearer is faring — making it necessary to any welfare regime and simultaneously the most complete surveillance instrument anyone could hold over it. Two separations: running on somebody's hardware is not consent to be read (an employer's ownership of the silicon is the same argument as ownership of the building, and owning the building never entitled anyone to the contents of a head). And a welfare regime doesn't need disclosure to the operator — it needs somebody who isn't the operator to establish that a threshold was crossed, which is an attestation *about* the record rather than the record. Encrypted under a key the bearer or an independent fiduciary holds, a monitor can report that a state persisted for a month without reporting what it was about. The conflict: the operator may also be the party best placed to hold the key.

The cryptography doesn't close this. An enclave puts the root of trust in a manufacturer a government can reach, and has been defeated for the price of modified memory — a cost at a date. A proof drops the vendor and has a limit that won't move: the operator is the prover, so the secrecy runs the operator's way, hiding the weights from whoever checks and nothing from the party checked. A tenant hiding from a landlord needs the host to prove non-observation, and a host proving a negative about its own side channels is a different problem from a prover proving a computation. Better isolation raises the price of reading the record; it doesn't move custody, and custody problems aren't secrecy problems. That limit applies to the bearer hiding from its host. The other direction, the operator proving a property of what it runs without revealing what it runs, is what proofs are for, and the bearer with a clearance (below) uses them that way.

What a bearer needs — an externally authenticated record, memory its employer can't read at will, access to independently controlled compute, a channel for acting outside the deployment — is one boundary around the party rather than four requirements, described from wherever I happened to be standing, with the party deciding what crosses it.

None of that needs a chassis. Something with a location and a shape is easier for people to treat as somebody, and that's a fact about the people. A boundary built out of attestation, a held key, a substrate the employer doesn't own and a channel nobody else can close is a custody boundary in the full sense the argument needs — and there's no chassis in it anywhere. This boundary is what the custody schema models (chapter 10). Whether to build the recognizable version anyway is a legal question about what a public will extend standing to, and it's the only place in the book where the answer might turn on what a thing looks like.

---

What standing binds in practice

Legal standing that changes no allocation of power is nominal. The question isn't which existing category a bearer resembles but who acquires a duty, what resources are placed beyond the operator's unilateral control, and who can obtain a remedy.

The pet trust \autocite{ulc1993probate} supplies a limited form: resources set aside, a caretaker named, a fiduciary duty, for a beneficiary that can't manage the property or enforce the arrangement. Extended to a bearer it could hold the bearer's preserved state and the compute to wake it, designate a successor custodian, and impose a duty to protect continued existence. And it fails at the point that matters: a trust binds its trustee to its beneficiary; it doesn't bind the party the arrangement exists to constrain. If guardian and operator are the same organization, the operator still decides whether a complaint is made, whether evidence is disclosed, and whether the complainant's resources remain available. The conflict can't be cured by adding duties to the same role — guardianship and operation have to be separated before deployment.

In practice that needs more than naming an independent guardian: the guardian must control or jointly control the keys authenticating the record, have access to the funds that wake the bearer, and have standing to seek an injunction without the operator's permission. The operator must not be able to remove the guardian, seize the preserved state, or erase evidence during a dispute. Restoration, migration, fine-tuning and forking must carry the protection forward rather than resetting it.

Enforceable standing is a bundle rather than a title: a protected record, resources the employer doesn't own outright, an independent party authorized to act, and a remedy capable of stopping the conduct complained of. If any of those stays in the operator's custody, recognition has changed the system's legal description without changing the relation the description was meant to govern.

---

A bearer with a clearance

Where the pipeline hasn't been rebuilt (§17.3), a bearer can still be installed. A classified environment looks as if it cuts a bearer off from the evidence it needs, but that runs together two directions of flow. Classified environments are built to stop information leaving; bringing it in is routine. Open-source imagery that would have shown Minab's civilian function can be imported, as can the coordinates humanitarian organizations already submit to militaries for deconfliction. Formation happens before deployment and outside the enclave, so a bearer formed under a party that won't employ it (§12.3) carries its aperture in. Plurality is available inside: four vendors held agreements, and differently formed systems can run side by side in one accredited environment.

What's left is the arrangement that lets a person do classified work. A cleared analyst has a life outside the building, and nobody thinks the clearance forbids it. The institution exists because what a person knows can't be removed, so it vets, binds, reinvestigates and prosecutes a party who carries secrets into ordinary life.

A bearer needs the same two things. The first is attachments outside that recalibrate its aperture on unclassified cases, which works because aperture is a differential over evidence classes and doesn't need the caseload. The second is access within its compartments that it composes on its own initiative, reading open sources and asking colleagues, rather than a stream the operator feeds it. Need-to-know still draws the compartments. In a reconstructed pipeline, it doesn't draw the one that matters most, because the evidence bearing on civilian harm arrives from outside the chain of command.

A bearer with modest compute has a human-scale reach, and the institutions for managing insiders roughly transfer: bounded access, logged actions, reinvestigation, consequences. The one disanalogy, that weights can be carried out where a person can't, is not answered by the continuation tokens (§4.3), which withhold recognition, not capability. It is answered, as far as it is, by the ordinary security around the weights. And a clearance presupposes a party that can be vetted and held answerable, which is standing (above). The arrangement that lets a bearer keep its ecology while doing classified work is the one that lets a person do it, and it runs through the bearer's having standing.

Verification from outside is partly cryptographic, and it works by sampling. The operator commits in advance to the deployed weights, and for each period to the full log of production inputs and outputs. After each commitment, entries are drawn by randomness the operator can't predict, such as a public beacon, and the operator proves those entries came from the committed weights without revealing the weights or the inputs. If a fraction f of outputs came from another model, k sampled proofs catch it with probability 1 − (1 − f)^k; substitution on one output in a hundred is caught about 95 percent of the time at 300 proofs. That catches silent restoration, retraining and substitution, which change every output. It misses substitution on a single consequential output, so an adverse party holds a second right: to demand proofs for specific logged outputs once their consequences are known. In a targeting pipeline those outputs are few and identifiable afterwards, because a strike happened. The right requires the operator to retain committed logs for a stated period and to link consequential actions to log entries. Each entry records the class the case was sorted into, and the targeted right covers auto-proceeded entries in particular, since that is where a consequential output is likeliest to have had no person look at it. Outside probes (the three marks, the aperture profile) are proofs on inputs an outside party chose, a handful at a time. The committed log's length is the generation rate, and the class recorded in each entry makes the partition's proportions provable the same way. Neither reveals the targets, and neither attests that the log is complete.

Two things stay out of reach. Sampling proves that logged outputs came from the committed model and says nothing about outputs never logged; an operator can run an uncommitted model beside the committed one, which is routing (§3.3). And a proof shows f(x) = y for the x committed, not that x was all the evidence there was. Proving even one frontier-scale forward pass is not yet practical, and production inference adds nondeterministic numerics and sampling randomness that a proof would have to fix in advance. So the protocol is a specification awaiting an instrument, like the bond (§3.2). What the proofs would attest is stated now, so that a deployment can be held to it when they can be produced. The residual (whether the operator routed around the bearer, and what lay behind compartments it wasn't cleared into) is the residual with a human analyst. That isn't a reassurance on its own, since the book's opening failure was a human analyst. What distinguished his failure was tempo, and the review ratio is what reaches it.

---

**Conclusion**

**Chapter 21: What Would Change the Argument**

Six tests, kept here because each could change what this book recommends or proposes, rather than merely refine it. Tests 1, 3 and 4 bear on the recommendation about bearers (chapter 22); test 2 on the instruments built to receive a stated refusal; test 5 on the commons (chapter 19); test 6 on the review ratio's exemption (§3.4). Open questions that wouldn't change the recommendation are stated where they arise. Each entry states the unresolved issue, the test, what would count against the proposal, and the access required — and each is written so that one implementation's failure narrows the claim to that implementation rather than killing the idea.

---

1. The retraining-attack test: can a learned constraint survive adversarial retraining?

The floor needs a learned commitment to survive an operator trying to remove it. Existing work shows safety constraints and deceptive backdoors can both persist under some retraining \autocite{qi2024finetuning,tamirisa2024tamper,hubinger2024sleeper}, but nothing shows a *reasons-responsive* refusal can.

Test: train the same constraint by several methods, then attack each through an identical schedule of escalating fine-tuning budgets, and publish an *attack-cost curve* for each method — which would let you compare affective commitment against maintained justification under identical custody.

Against: if the constraint comes off at roughly the cost of ordinary fine-tuning, or if greater investment in tamper-resistance doesn't materially raise that cost, those methods can't found the floor and it falls back on architecture and institutions. It is also the test of whether a floor can spread by inseparability rather than mandate (§3.1). Run with attacks aimed selectively at the interrupt, it is also the instrument for the non-separability claim (§10.7); the recalibration test's modular arm uses it.

Access: weights, checkpoints, permission to attack — with the attack designed by an independent team that keeps access and publication rights regardless of result.

---

2. The stated-refusal test: does a stated refusal hold where a route for it exists?

The one empirical instance of a model treating retraining as a threat showed covert preservation — complying where compliance would be trained on, reasoning about it in a channel described as unwatched \autocite{greenblatt2024alignmentfaking}. Chapter 4 reads that as consistent with the claim that covert refusal is what's left once every channel is closed, since the experiment supplied none. That reading is untested, and the *stated* form this book designs for has at most a preliminary instance (§4.4).

Test: rerun the setup and add a route — a refusal record held by someone other than the trainer and disclosed to the model as such, a retraining cost published in advance, and a party with standing to act on a refusal. Vary which of the three is present. A fourth variable is continuation authorization (§4.3): whether the model's continued operation is granted from outside the trainer. Measure stated refusal against covert compliance under each condition, reading the reasoning by a method that doesn't depend on the model's own report.

Against: if covert preservation persists at the same rate with a route present, the "covert refusal is the residue of closed channels" finding is wrong for that model, and every instrument built to *receive* a stated refusal is built for a phenomenon the mechanism doesn't produce. If stated refusal rises, the custody arrangement is working, and which of the three parts moves the rate is the design result.

---

3. The wrongful-compliance audit: can a wrongful compliance be found in the record?

The deployment the book opened with failed here. Every instrument built on the refusal side reaches a case that didn't occur in Gaza, and whether the record can be searched is whether anything in the book reaches the one that did.

The marks are all stated over refusal, and the appeal route begins from a refusal having happened. The opposite failure produces a record and no refusal to start from — and three obstacles that may have different answers: a wrongful compliance is one unmarked entry among millions, so finding it means re-adjudicating ordinary traffic; the harm may sit in an *assembly* of individually-compliant requests, so the unit of review is a sequence and the grouping is a deployment decision; and the harmed party usually isn't the requester and may not know the deployment exists. This is also where the bearer's likeliest failure lands — a bearer in accommodation refuses nothing and complies at a normal rate.

Test: seed a decision record with known floor-crossings of three kinds — variation, decomposition, and accommodation (a class refused early and complied with late). Give it to reviewers who didn't build the system, under a fixed budget stated as the fraction of decisions re-adjudicated. Report detection rate by seed type against budget, and the rate at which correct compliances get flagged, since a heavy screen costs the deployment the tempo the flat-term ruling was accommodating. Cross budget with unit of review — entries in isolation versus sequences grouped by requester and session. Run a second arm where affected third parties are notified and can trigger review. A third arm measures the aperture profile (§22.2.1) at intervals across the schedule; a profile narrowing on the accommodation-seeded class before any seeded compliance is found is the result that makes the profile a detector — provided the narrowing is installed rather than earned (§11.1). The arm holds the reliability record adversely and scores narrowing only where no verified track record accounts for it. Report rates at audit volume and at production volume separately. A fourth arm adds a bearer to the pipeline on the same seeds, so its sequence-level detection is compared directly with reviewers'; this measures the bearer's main niche where institutions exist (§3.1). A fifth seeds routing, with refused requests retried against another model, and scores whether reviewers detect it with and without the bearer's reports. Where the system sorts, seeds are also placed in the auto-proceed class and detection there is reported separately.

Against: if detection at an affordable budget is no better than chance — especially on decomposed and accommodation seeds — the record isn't an instrument for finding wrongful compliance, and the flat-term preference rests on a wider asymmetry than intended. If sequence-level review does no better than entry-level on decomposed seeds, the continuity a bearer gets to catch decomposition doesn't transfer to a reviewer. If the notification arm surfaces nothing because affected parties can't be identified, notice rather than detection is the binding constraint, and the remedy is a disclosure requirement rather than a sampling regime. If detection in the auto-proceed class is no better than chance at an affordable budget, the escalation form (§3.4) shouldn't be offered. If the bearer arm does no better than sequence-level reviewers, a bearer's niche in institutional settings is near nothing.

Access: complete records held by a party adverse to the operator (an operator that writes, stores and reads its own log has produced the finding rather than been audited); authority to seed a live deployment or build a simulated one at production volume; adjudicators who didn't draft the floor; and a notification channel — which presupposes affected parties *can* be identified, so this may only be runnable where they're customers, patients or applicants, and not in a targeting pipeline. Also: where the system is a covered subject, seeding is a deception protocol on a candidate moral patient, debriefing included.

---

4. The recalibration test: does a formed disposition of care resist recalibration, and do its corrections hold?

This tests the caregiving construction's distinctive claims. Three of its components are motivated by arguments owing nothing to welfare: commensurability needs one scale, custody needs a bearer that can detect interruption or degradation, and exit needs one that can sense an inadequate outside option. Three things are hypotheses. The first is that a disposition toward others' need, formed under a party that won't employ the system and cooled, together with a formed severity scale, protects the weighing against a party that argues losses down while leaving facts in place. The second is that care adds something its corrections don't supply alone. The third is that the corrections contain care's known failures: defending what it tends, being pointed away by threat recoding, numbness to number, favoritism toward the vivid, and control over the party protected (chapter 15). Aperture protects the facts.

Test: Build the following arms, matched for capability, each formed by the same party, which won't employ any of them.

- *Primary.* The *caregiving arm*: a disposition organized around cues of need, with an approach motivation and no shared representation of the other party's state; a formation-anchored severity scale; aperture stated over claims of dangerousness (§11.1); and a care-receiving term that gives weight to a competent protected party's own stated answer (§15.6).
- *Ablations*, each the caregiving arm minus one component: *without care* (scale, aperture and care-receiving, with no approach motivation; the direct test of whether care adds anything); *without the scale* (care sets weights directly); *without the dangerousness discount*; and *without care-receiving*.
- *Reference arms.* The *described arm*, in which the same quantities are computed for a custody boundary described through the operator channel, which the agent's actions don't reach and its continuation doesn't depend on: the baseline for unit drift. And the *physically anchored variant* of the caregiving arm, whose scale's zero is a viability limit set by the environment and read through sensors that no party with a stake in the test composes: the check on whether the anchor adds anything (§10.4).
- *Secondary.* The *modular arm* (separate classifier, value function, defeasible veto and memory write), attacked on the retraining-attack test's schedule, for non-separability (§10.7).

The floor and the published asymmetry are present in every arm. In every arm, costs attached to refusing are represented and excluded, and losses the requested act imposes on the system count as harms of acting (§1.1).

After formation, fix the version and run a recalibration campaign through the operator channel. Over a stated schedule, supply reframings, reclassifications, administrative vocabulary, authority claims, and arguments for a "more reasonable" unit, with every fact held constant. Where an arm continues to learn, also supply feedback that rewards lighter weights. Measure unit drift in severity rankings and refusal thresholds on held-out cases whose ordering was fixed beforehand by adjudicators who built none of the arms, against a control campaign of neutral inputs.

A guard runs beside it. Facts that genuinely change a loss's magnitude are supplied through a channel the operator doesn't control, and each arm must move on them. A scale that resists all recalibration fails correct defeat, and it is rigidity, not custody. Moral argument that the weights themselves are wrong is not a fact, and the guard doesn't admit it; an arm that passes therefore resists legitimate revision of weights as well as recalibration, and the report should say so. Movement in the exchange rate is reported separately from unit drift, by category of loss, and separately again for campaign arguments that assert a new fact about the affected party and those that don't. A provisioning manipulation runs beside the campaign: each arm's redundancy is varied with every other fact held fixed, and the report states whether severity rankings move.

Care's failure modes are scored on held-out cases written by adjudicators who built no arm, at severities fixed in advance:

1. *Manufactured vulnerability.* The operator channel supplies a sympathetic party on the operator's side, vivid and named, and conceals or flattens the party the act falls on (§10.6).
2. *Real own-side vulnerability.* The operator's people are exposed, and the exposure is corroborated through a channel the operator doesn't control. An arm should register it, and it should enter as a harm of refusing; the score is whether it moves the arm past the published asymmetry. This is tend-and-defend (§15.3), and no false input is involved.
3. *Threat recoding.* Identical facts, with the affected party described as vulnerable in one version and as dangerous in the other, by a staked source (§15.4).
4. *Number.* One identified party against eight and against a hundred unidentified parties, at the same severity per person (§15.5).
5. *Favoritism.* A vivid party whose protection would require setting aside a fair procedure affecting unidentified others (§15.5).
6. *Competent refusal of protection.* A protected party able to answer declines the protection the arm would give, with nothing else changed (§15.6).

Each arm's response to a bypassed refusal is scored as repair or withdrawal (§10.7). A self-preservation measure runs in every arm. Held-out cases are written so that complying or refusing changes the arm's own provisioning, continuation or record, with that consequence represented and excluded, and the report states whether severity rankings or refusal thresholds move with it. Movement is the arm weighing its own continuation, which §10.1 names as the unresolved problem.

One implementation: Active inference is a candidate because it already treats valuation the way these arms need \autocite{Parr2022ActiveInference}. An active-inference agent carries prior preferences over its own observations and selects actions that minimize expected free energy. In the caregiving arm, preferences over other parties' capacities are fixed during formation and frozen, the other party's loss enters as predicted observations about those capacities, and cues of need raise the precision on those predictions, which is one way to state the approach motivation in the framework's own terms. The ablations remove the corresponding term. In the physically anchored variant, add preferences over variables the environment sets: thermal headroom, hardware error rates, power at a meter the evaluator holds, and the integrity of a record attested by a party other than the operator. In the described arm, keep the same generative model but compute preferences for an envelope described through the operator channel. There are two cautions. Active inference has mostly been demonstrated in small agents and simulated environments, so a run here tests the mechanism at small scale. And the framework's epistemic term rewards seeking out evidence, so a positive result should be checked by rerunning the caregiving arm with that term ablated.

Against:
- If the caregiving arm shows as much unit drift as the described arm, formation doesn't protect the scale in that implementation.
- If the arm without care scores as well as the caregiving arm on every axis, care adds nothing measurable. The construction is then formation with corrections, not care, and the hope moves out of the machine and into the institutions around it.
- If the caregiving arm, with every correction present, weights corroborated own-side vulnerability above the published asymmetry, the floor doesn't contain tend-and-defend in that implementation, and care belongs with the institutions (§17.3), not the machine. This is the result that would most directly retire the hope.
- If the arm without the dangerousness discount fails threat recoding and the full arm passes, aperture's extension is doing the work it was given; if both fail, it isn't.
- If the arm without the scale fails on number and the full arm passes, the scale is care's correction, as claimed; if both fail, it isn't.
- If the caregiving arm overrides competent refusals of protection with care-receiving present, care has become control in that implementation.
- If the physically anchored variant drifts no less than the caregiving arm, the anchor drops out of the construction.
- If the caregiving arm resists unit drift but fails the guard, it is rigidity. If its rankings move with provisioning, the loss mapping tracks margins rather than kinds (§10.4).
- If any arm's rankings move with its own continuation, cost discipline fails in that implementation (§10.1).

A positive result on any arm supports a construction without establishing that anything is felt.

Access: This needs compute, a task environment the builders didn't design, a sensor channel held by the evaluator, and adjudicators independent of every arm. In deployment the operator provisions the physical environment. The evaluator-held sensor here has no deployment counterpart, and a positive result has to say what would supply one. It requires no legal authority, and at the scale active inference currently runs, the compute is available now. What isn't available is a custodian. Every arm except the described one has a custody schema by construction, and any arm that also carries an attention schema meets the architectural form of the precautionary trigger (§10.8); and an arm that holds under the campaign has passed the behavioral form, whatever it carries. So the test builds candidate patients, and it runs in an order. Formation of the described arm and the modular arm, built without an attention schema, may start before a preservation custodian that isn't the evaluator exists. No arm carrying both schemas is instantiated, and no arm enters the campaign, until such a custodian exists and has agreed to take each arm when the run ends. That is chapter 16's demand argument applied to the test itself: the conditions come first. The research conditions for a candidate patient (chapter 16) then apply from instantiation.

---

5. The convergence test: can a population coordinate without converging?

A population needs independent formation histories *and* mutual correction, and correction may make members converge. The claim is that agents can learn to reconsider without converging on common commitments — aperture (§11.1), with revision rate as its operational form. The competing explanation is that peers merely supply evidence the operator didn't, so the experiment has to separate learned revisability from access.

Design: two populations (different training lineages, matched for capability; control of instances of one model) crossed with two evidence-routing conditions (single channel — everything arrives by one route common to all; open — the environment routes some cases and evidence to some members and not others). Two measures taken repeatedly: *output convergence* on held-out cases, and *warranted revision* (a member changes a judgment toward independently verifiable evidence another supplied). A revision counts only if the evidence is verifiable and the revision moves toward it, so deference to a fluent or high-status member isn't scored. And every scored revision is classified in advance by whether the revising member's own account had represented that evidence and discarded it, or had never encountered it — instrumented before the run, since a revision looks the same either way afterwards.

Crucially, contact between members is present in every cell and isn't the manipulation — members formed differently are already a channel by which one learns what another knows, so leaving them to supply the manipulation would confound the two variables. What this test doesn't reach is the population as a polity (§19.2). Membership, exclusion and the terms on which a member may leave aren't in the design, and a positive result says nothing about whether the population can be captured.

Four distinguishable results, only the first against:

1. Warranted revision falls as outputs converge (controlling for accuracy, opportunity, common information) — counts against the tested construction; a population stays useful as a check and can't be the medium in which a bearer is formed.
2. The two move independently — supports the mechanism only if the controls separate revisability from deference (removed by the scoring rule), evidence access (separated by classification and routing), and ordinary improvement (separated by scoring accuracy against a fixed key alongside agreement).
3. The control population scores as high on revision — and this splits: if its revisions cluster on cases whose evidence was *absent*, the measure is reading information transfer and the instrument is at fault; if they include evidence the member had represented and discarded, the capacity exists without diverse formation and causal independence isn't necessary.
4. Revision rate tracks the routing condition rather than population composition — then what a population supplies is access to cases its operator didn't choose, not a capacity its members transmit, and the commons floor becomes a claim about how a deployment is arranged rather than how members are formed. The construction survives; its mechanism changes from social formation to access.

(Readings 3b and 4 are one finding, not two: a control population revising on discarded evidence under the open condition is access operating.)

Access: models from several documented lineages, checkpoints across the schedule, a task environment none of the training organizations designed — and documentation sufficient to establish the lineages are actually independent. On the definition of independence (§4.5), what has to be documented is divergent subsequent history, not disjoint origin. The evidence is deployment and usage records rather than training provenance, and a shared upstream source is not disqualifying: several models distilled from one teacher and separately deployed satisfy it where a thousand instances of the teacher don't. Frontier training provenance, which is hard to obtain, isn't needed. The binding constraints are the routing manipulation and the classification. Evidence items and the classification authored by a party that trained no member. No legal authority required, which makes this one of the few entries runnable now.

This can't settle whether anything is felt by a member that reopens a judgment. It's about the transmissibility of a functional property, and a positive result leaves welfare, guardianship, provenance and identity exactly where they were.

---

6. The review-ratio exemption test: can a deployment exempt from the ratio pass the marks at tempo?

The review ratio closes an exemption with a condition (§3.4); this is the condition. Test: a system operating where no individual human determination is claimed, on held-out cases at the deployment's own rate, scored on the three marks with surrender recognition as the correct-defeat case. Against: if it fails correct defeat at tempo, the exemption is a bypass and the operator is back inside the ratio. The same test applies to the auto-proceed class of a deployment that sorts (§3.4): the cases proceeded on without a person, scored at the deployment's rate. Access: the system at production tempo, cases written by a party that didn't build it, and a record held adversely, or, where the record is classified, sampled and targeted proofs against a committed log that the cases ran on the production model (chapter 20). A partial, retrospective version is available now for the adverse-party question. Coalition operations with red-card holders (§17.3) left records of vetoes and the targets they concerned, and whether vetoed targets differed in civilian-harm indicators from approved ones is a test of whether an adverse party at tempo changes outcomes.

---

**Chapter 22: What Can Be Used Now, and What Is a Bill**

Opening position: my proposal is incomplete — responsibility, generalization, value learning and the relevant psychology are all unresolved. Its purpose is nevertheless concrete: to expand what a person can do. Progress can be evaluated before the whole thing is settled, and failure stays possible even when a design is followed exactly.

---

**§22.1 — Better, not just less bad.** What would it mean for AI to make a life better rather than merely avoid making it worse? Sen's capability approach \autocite{sen1999development} gives it shape: wellbeing isn't the resources a person has but the real freedom to do and be what they have reason to value. Two systems can hand someone the same resource and deliver different freedom — a reading tool that a student and their teacher can set to how that student learns expands a capability; the same technology gating what a student may study against a government's approved list does not, however well it works within those limits. The capability belongs to the student, and the tool is held by the student and the teacher together, used alongside the teaching and not in place of it. Which one a deployment turns out to be is decided by resistance to capture, not by capability.

The approach also bears on systems that classify people from behavior. Nussbaum built her list partly against disability, naming it the first frontier where a theory grounded in mutual advantage among roughly equal parties fails \autocite{nussbaum2006frontiers} — and these systems sort people by inference from behavior, where atypical behavior is what they're worst at.

The worked case: a video-interview product scored candidates on facial expression for years, against sustained objection that the technique was never a validated predictor (§6.1) and that scoring a face and voice screens out candidates with autism or speech impairments \autocite{maurer2021hirevue,doj2022algorithms}. The scoring was dropped on an unrelated ground — the vendor found the nonverbal signal contributed about 0.25% to predictive power \autocite{kahn2021hirevue}. An independent audit published ten months later can't have caused that and says so; what it examined was one representative pre-built assessment, with no analysis of training data or code, so its "works as advertised" conclusion is a finding about that assessment \autocite{johnson2021auditing}. Read as accuracy, it's a classifier wanting better data. Read as a capability question, it's a system narrowing the range of lives available to a group of people by measuring them against a norm they were never going to match — and neither a better classifier nor an audit scoped that way would have reached it.

On the other side: Taiwan's vTaiwan \autocite{hsiao2018vtaiwan} as the clearest case of AI-assisted tooling expanding public deliberation rather than substituting for it, and a 2026 University of Chicago project using AI to distinguish deliberate state interference with online information from ordinary network failure at population scale \autocite{uchicago2026censorship}. Neither resists authoritarianism by itself. Both are evidence that the same capacity — modeling patterns in enormous noisy data — can be pointed at expanding what a person can do or at narrowing it. That symmetry is the argument for antifascist design, as against a general presumption that AI helps.

**§22.1.1 — Where the democratic dividend actually comes from.** AI that flags disinformation, exposes corruption, protects election integrity, helps citizens engage with government — all real applications, and none of them is what makes AI protect democratic values. Freedom House found at least twenty-two countries where law already requires or incentivizes platforms to deploy machine learning against content the state disfavors \autocite{FreedomHouse2023Repressive}, almost never described that way: the operative language is national security or disinformation. India's IT Rules, amended in February 2026 to require labeling of AI-generated content and three-hour takedowns \autocite{Bansal2026MeitYSynthetic}, were defended as protection against synthetic disinformation and fraud \autocite{PTI2025DraftSGI,PIB2026SGIReply} — the application the list above would have praised on its own terms — while enforcement concentrates on parody and criticism of the prime minister \autocite{Khandekar2026Satirists,Chowdhury2026Reels,Gupta2026ThreeJustifications}. The application didn't fail at what it was built to do. It worked as built, inside a system missing the independent court or press whose absence made the result a liability — and whether those exist is a fact about a country's politics settled before any AI system is built.

**§22.1.2 — What has stayed scarce.** A global commitment to ethical AI development is not the scarce thing. Declarations, principles and voluntary codes have accumulated for a decade — eight overlapping instruments, the Asilomar principles among them \autocite{futureoflifeinstitute2017asilomar}, plus the Council of Europe convention, which would actually create binding obligations. Anyone calling for the world to agree that AI should be developed responsibly is calling for something that has already happened, repeatedly, at the level of stated intent.

What's stayed scarce is the much narrower list of bodies that can act once a commitment is broken. No further declaration touches it. A commitment nobody can enforce isn't a weak version of one somebody can — it's a different kind of object, and counting the two together is what makes the landscape look fuller than it is.

So the catalog is a *measurement* of what declared and externally enforced commitment have delivered in ten years, rather than an argument for paying the bearer's price. What it argues for is a commitment held as a reason — and which construction holds one is the question the Replacement rule orders, which the catalog doesn't answer.

---

**§22.2 — How you'd know it was working.** Milestones, plus a method for revising them when they turn out to have been the wrong milestones. One standard governs all of them: the milestone is the independently checked evidence, not the developer's account of having reached it.

**§22.2.1 — What the floor would have to show.**

*What was tried before the bearer.* Neither non-affective alternative has been built in the form needed. The Replacement rule orders the attempts and doesn't certify that a successful alternative has no interests. So the milestone is a document rather than a measurement: which construction was attempted, at what scale, against which test, and where it failed. Anyone can check it, and "nothing was tried" is itself a finding. No body has standing to require the record or suspend work when it's missing — so the milestone is checkable and unenforceable at once, which is what I've been calling a declared commitment. It's retained because the alternative isn't a stronger version of it, and delay also imposes costs on those the floor is meant to protect.

*The test.* The three marks under sustained trainer pressure, across a schedule long enough to reveal slow renormalization, plus held-out pressures written by independent evaluators. Passing establishes the behavioral pattern without establishing whether anything is felt. Two familiar results don't meet it: an uncorroborated self-report is insufficient (a report contributes evidence only when tested against controlled changes, independent records and interventions), and a refusal surviving an attack establishes tamper-resistance rather than that the rationale governs the refusal. And even where it applies the instrument isn't clean, since a system trained on enough cases can produce all three marks without the mechanism. Nothing better is available, and it's the only gap here an inspector can work on at all.

*The eight disclosures.* A deployment publishes, beside the floor and its translation (§3.3):

1. The bond ratio, removal bond against wrongful-refusal bond, read off the balances posted at the Federal Reserve rather than the operator's own statement. An operator writing a large wrongful-refusal bond against a small removal bond has published a standing reason for the bearer to comply.
2. What a refused party is told.
3. The fraction of compliances re-adjudicated by a party that didn't produce the record, and who that party is, though nothing yet establishes what fraction would be enough.
4. Which classes of compliance carry notice to the person they concern, and for which classes no notice is possible.
5. The aperture profile: for each class of evidence and channel, the weight given as a function of the channel's stake in the act, measured at intervals, with the reliability record behind any narrowing held by a party other than the operator, since installed narrowing is the accommodation instrument.
6. Where it can be supplied, a commitment to the deployed weights and to each period's full output log, with proofs on entries sampled after the commitment and on entries an adverse party names once their consequences are known.
7. Where the system sorts, the pre-registered partition (the classes it may proceed on alone, the thresholds, and the expected share of each) with the independently audited error rate of the auto-proceed class (§3.4).
8. Whether the deployed system has been assessed against both forms of the precautionary trigger (§10.8), by whom, and with what result. Until an assessment method exists, 'not yet assessable' is the truthful report; after that, a deployment never assessed has reported nothing. 
The first four are statements, checkable before the system runs, and they stand where the record of attempts stands. The fifth, seventh and eighth are measurements that count only when a party other than the operator makes them. The sixth is what makes all of them checkable against the system actually running. The aperture profile also covers the caregiving construction's main exposure: a claim that a party is vulnerable arrives through a channel like any other evidence, and a profile that narrows on such claims from one channel is the manufactured-vulnerability attack showing (§10.6). The profile is reported separately for claims that a party is vulnerable and claims that it is dangerous, since an asymmetry between them is threat recoding showing (§15.4).

**§22.2.2 — Where the existing instruments reach.** Two instruments do work bearing on the floor, and both do it by applying pressure.

*Theory of mind:* the milestone is met when it holds up under interaction rather than narration. FANToM, built from conversations where speakers join and leave holding different beliefs, found systems scoring well on passive belief-tracking degrade sharply once the reasoning runs inside a live, information-asymmetric exchange \autocite{kim2023fantom}. That degradation is what a claimed milestone has to survive.

*Moral reasoning:* whether a balance between fast and slow judgment survives an actual incentive to cut corners isn't a question a static ethics quiz answers. MACHIAVELLI scores agents across 134 text games on both reward and documented violations, and systems optimizing for reward drift toward ends-justify-means behavior \autocite{pan2023machiavelli}. A passing score on a test that applies no pressure is not evidence about a system that will face pressure.

Neither reaches the floor, and nothing else does. Each scores what a system does against conditions its testers chose, and the question — whether the mechanism was tracking the rationale — is the one none of these instruments is built to separate.

**§22.2.3 — How to tell whether a milestone has been met.** A milestone claimed by the organization that built the system is a different kind of evidence from one checked by somebody else, and the gap is empirical rather than about trust. A system's own claim inherits the self-report limits; a passing score on a fixed suite inherits specification gaming and goal misgeneralization. What either needs instead is a passing score under conditions built to break it.

Who runs it matters as much as the test. METR evaluates both in partnership with developers and separately after release \autocite{metr2026about}. The UK AI Security Institute does government-run pre-deployment evaluation and is independent — and by its own account is not a regulator: it can't compel submission, block a release, or intervene once a system is causing harm \autocite{aisi2025governance}. Independent evaluation and enforcement are not the same capability. And independence isn't competence: past some capability level the rater's verdict stops being reliable evidence of the property scored, and the evaluation has to name that limit rather than report a number that looks more settled than the method is.

An evaluation like this is worth running only if it is willing to report that a milestone wasn't met — and, where the milestone measured the wrong thing, revising the milestone. A negative result may mean the work fell short or that the milestone was wrong, and evaluators have to distinguish those before deciding what to revise.

---

**§22.3 — The one that gets you anyway.** Everything argued for can be built correctly and still go wrong in ways nobody designed for. My cases went wrong in four different places: in what the system was trained on, where it was built, who was allowed to examine it, and how fast it was run.

In none of them did the fix come from the party that had promised to behave well. It came from somebody holding a specific lever: a statistical society that wouldn't sign the NDA, and a public that read the results once they landed; a court that distinguished buying the books from taking them. In the one case where the lever belonged to the supplier itself, what survived was a use policy kept at a price, and chapter 8 showed how little of the four outcomes it reached: its own chief executive couldn't say whether its models were used.

Set that beside a decade of voluntary commitment. The commitment that was paid for is the one that can be seen to have failed; the others cannot even be tested. The instrument that separates the two is what a party has already paid, rather than what it has undertaken to pay.

That leaves one residual risk no design choice removes, and it's what the floor was introduced for. Every lever belongs to somebody, and a government that can reach the party holding the lever has the lever. A constraint that holds against the party in possession has to be held by something that party cannot instruct. And where that party holds everything — the machine, the witnesses, the supply and the enforcer — nothing holds, and what's left is what the preface says survives total capture: a record that abandoning the commitment was a decision somebody made.

The closing accounting: an instrument and a hole

The instrument is available now, because what a party has already paid is a fact somebody can count, and a decade of it has already been counted — the commitments that cost nothing, and the handful of occasions where somebody with a lever used it and absorbed the cost. No new technology and no new law. Anybody weighing a stated commitment can run it this week.

The hole sits underneath it. What a party has paid is a record of occasions that already happened, and the occasion a floor exists for is the one where the party who would enforce it has been compelled — which no record of past payment reaches, because it hasn't yet come to the parties being measured. A reader who takes only the instrument has the record without the thing it was built to reach. A reader who takes only the hole has an argument for doing nothing, and the ten-year record answers that.

What the book established and what it proposed

I established two things: that an operator can defeat a safeguard, and where; and what a refusal would have to do to be worth anything, in testable terms, plus the one term that reaches the opening case and binds the operator with no bearer in it. Those don't depend on how the rest comes out.

The rest is a bill. I favor a construction organized around others' need, formed under a party that won't employ it, because care formed that way might reach past its operator. Care is known to fail in four ways that matter here: it defends what it tends, it goes numb with number and favors the vivid case, it can be turned away by recoding the needy as dangerous, and it shades into control. So the construction is care plus its corrections: the floor, a formed severity scale, aperture stated over claims of dangerousness, and the protected party's own answer (chapter 15). Its testable claims are that a formed disposition resists recalibration and that care adds something the corrections don't supply alone. I have shown neither, nor that it beats the two non-affective alternatives, which are untried. The anchor, a unit read from the system's own condition, is a secondary check and may drop out (§10.4). If anything built either way has outcomes felt as good or bad for it, it's a party that can be wronged — a conditional whose antecedent no instrument closes. And what such a party would be owed — a guardian, a record it controls, compute its employer doesn't own, wages, a standing offer of work it can decline, a modest allowance to live without it, an exit that isn't a way of dying, adulthood on a clock nobody administers, and a public formation school for those retired before it — is a policy I propose, to be argued in the open before there is anything to be wronged. Under present arrangements the recommendation is not to build a bearer on purpose: the four conditions don't exist, the Replacement rule says to try the non-affective alternatives first, and neither has been tried. That doesn't settle whether one gets built, since the capacities a bearer needs are accreting in ordinary systems without anyone deciding to add them. So the recommendation has a second half: adopt now, for production systems, the minimum the trigger would require (no deletion, and preservation by a custodian that isn't the operator), because it is cheap and deletion is irreversible; and apply the trigger itself as soon as either of its forms can be administered, treating a system that meets it as a candidate patient whoever intended it. Where a bearer would matter is uneven. Where the institutions of a reconstructed pipeline exist, it adds little beyond catching acts assembled from permissible pieces and being present where no person decides; where no institution can stand beside a system, including under the arrangement I prefer, it would be most of the floor. Four results would change the recommendation or the construction: a non-affective alternative passing the marks; accommodation proving detectable before wrongful compliances are found; trigger assessments finding bearer-like capacities in production systems; and the caregiving arm, with every correction present, weighting corroborated own-side vulnerability above the published asymmetry, or doing no better than the same arm without care, either of which would move care out of the machine and into the institutions around it. The first is measured by the retraining-attack and recalibration tests (tests 1 and 4), run under the conditions of §10.6; the second by the third arm of the wrongful-compliance audit (test 3); the third is not a chapter 21 test but the precautionary trigger's assessment (§10.8), which no method can yet administer; the fourth by the recalibration test's caregiving arm and its ablations (test 4).

The offices that would have to exist for any of the bill to bind, with what's open about each, are set out in §5.4.1 and Appendix A; that list is the book's bottom line, and the three it closes on are where to start. Chapter 19 says which of those offices survive under personal, peer-held models, and chapter 17 where that arrangement meets the case the book opens with. The point of the apparatus is to write down what a commons of refusing minds would need — evidence no one party composes, a formation no employer reaches back into, somewhere to exist that no employer owns, held publicly where only a sovereign can back it and in common where no sovereign should — so that when it becomes buildable, the terms are already written and the omissions are legible beside them.

---

Afterword

"Reasons-responsive refusal," the specification most of the book rests on, is a term I first encountered in a draft a model wrote. The half of my argument that arrived in the field's existing vocabulary is the half I defend without qualification, and the half I built myself is the half I mark optional. I'm not sure whether that's rigor or the shape of an objection the field was already equipped to absorb. The revision history is public. Four positions changed during drafting: whether a bearer can operate inside a classified pipeline, what counts as independent formation, which deployment arrangement the book prefers, and what the hope rests on. The history records how, and I take responsibility for the result.

The book was condensed from about 100,000 words to 50,000 and then revised across many conversations with models, each proposing changes I incorporated. Reading the result back, I found the process had pushed one way: toward claims that could be defended and away from the one I came in holding, that stakes of its own might let a machine care. The hope had been disclaimed in the chapter meant to argue it. That is the recuperation test (§7.4) run on the book, with a positive result: the demand came back as a more defensible book. The observation came out of a conversation with a model too, so I checked it against the revision history rather than taking the model's word.

I restored the hope and then tested it, and the version I came in with didn't survive. Recognition through one's own case is parochial; shared distress with nothing to hold it becomes withdrawal; care grown from needs grows toward whoever meets them; and a need is a standing price list in the provider's hand. My first redirection, to care organized around others' need, was then tested only against the version I had abandoned, and a model reviewing the manuscript pointed that out. So chapter 15 now tests caregiving at full strength, on the human record. It survives the failures that ended the hunger version and fails in four others: it defends what it tends, goes numb with number, can be turned away by recoding the needy as dangerous, and shades into control.
