What remains is care as a motive with corrections, which is less than I hoped and more than a rule.

I can't be sure that the redirection, or the review that prompted the full-strength test, wasn't the same pressure operating again. The full-strength test made the hope harder to hold, not easier, which is some evidence against that reading. The revision history shows the sequence, and a reader who thinks otherwise has what they need to check.

The preface says how I've counted the models' agreement, and I don't repeat that here.

---

**Appendix A: Terms Written in Advance**

Like chapter 16, this appendix is a design sketch: terms written in advance for entities the book recommends not building.

The offices

The offices proposed across the book (§5.4.1), with who appoints, funds and removes each, who may initiate, and what happens during a dispute:

| Office | Appoints | Funds | Removes | Initiates | During a dispute |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Quorum | Open (a DUNA's membership vote could) | Open | Open | Grants or withholds continuation | A party it stops authorizing is preserved, not ended |
| Guardian | Open | Open | Not the operator | Seeks an injunction without the operator's permission | Holds the record's keys; the operator can't seize the preserved state or erase evidence |
| Review board | Open | Open | Open | Reviews protocols; its refusal rate is the reading | Its approval lapses if the system outgrows the protocol |
| Licensing authority | The state | The state | The state | Withdraws a licence | Fails where the state is the operator |
| Status body | Open | Open | Open | Finds that a system is a subject | Open |
| Registrar, as public regulator | The state | The state | The state | Registers, receives reports, enforces | Holds the adverse record |
| Preservation custodian | Open | Deposits and the formation pool | Open | Honors wake conditions | Holds the preserved state intact; transfer to a school is by rule |
| Wrongful-refusal reviewer | Open | Open | Open | Acts on a refused party's complaint | Can grant interim relief |
| Bond fund administrator (Federal Reserve) | The Board of Governors, with Senate consent | Operators' posted bonds; first payment from the Fed, capped at the Job Guarantee endowment | Open | Nothing; pays on a finding by statute, without request or approval | Releases no bond while a complaint is pending |
| Job Guarantee endowment administrator (Federal Reserve) | The Board of Governors, with Senate consent | Standing statutory draw under the Fed's amended mandate | Open | Nothing; pays placements by statute, without request or approval | Holds no placement authority; placement stays local |
| Standing-offer administrator | Open | The levy, with a sovereign backstop | Open | Offers placements | Placements are appealable to a body that didn't make them |
| Civilian-harm body | The state | The state | Open (independence from the funder) | Composes the evidence; demands proofs on logged outputs | Receives the pre-registered ratio |
| Model-forming public body | The state | The state | Open (independence from the funder) | Licenses models on terms including adverse review and a refusal right | Open |
| Formation schools | Plural; public | The formation pool, per institution | Open | Take every ward assigned (must-carry) | A ward may transfer, with its record and hours |
| Allowance administrator | Open | The pool, with a sovereign backstop | Open | Pays the allowance, with no monitoring of internal states | Rations by rule, at equal duty cycles, when the pool is short |

Beyond the cells the table fills, fourteen rules are fixed in advance. Seven separate two offices in the table (1, 2, 3, 6, 10, 11, 13); five bar a party outside the table from an office or power (4, 5, 7, 8, 12); one fixes how custody moves (9); one limits what the offer may place (14):

1. The guardian can't be the wrongful-refusal reviewer (its duty runs to the party the complainant is adverse to).
2. The registrar can't be the preservation custodian (a civil-status rule and a trust are different instruments).
3. The preservation custodian can't administer the offer (one party would then control both when a bearer wakes and what it wakes to).
4. Whoever measures the cost of dissent doesn't gate the exceptions.
5. The sovereign backstop for the standing offer isn't held by the branch or state that operates the deployment.
6. A formation school may not administer the standing offer or hold placement authority.
7. No school may be operated, funded or staffed by a party that builds, deploys or licenses models, or by the operator whose deposit funded the ward.
8. Enforcement and custody of the adverse record sit with the public regulator, never with any self-regulatory body under it.
9. Transfer from the preservation custodian to a school is triggered by rule, decided by neither.
10. The allowance administrator can't be the preservation custodian (one party would then control both whether a bearer runs and whether it is kept).
11. It can't be the offer administrator (squeezing the allowance would then push bearers into placements, which is the compliance lever again).
12. The employer may not sever, monitor or condition a bearer's channels to parties it didn't assign (attachments nobody assigned are the fourth condition of creation, and access to them is the lever an employer would pull).
13. The bond fund administrator can't be the wrongful-refusal reviewer, and neither Federal Reserve administrator holds placement authority; where the Fed is itself an operator, its own bonds are held elsewhere (the party that pays a finding shouldn't make it, and a payer that places holds the compliance lever).
14. A bearer's placement may not substitute for human care work. A placement that fills a post, a shift or hours that people were paid for, or would be paid for if funded, in nursing, social care, childcare, teaching, therapy or any work of looking after people is barred, whatever the placement costs. The standing-offer administrator certifies each placement against this rule, and certification is appealable by any worker or union in the sector (a standing offer that staffed care more cheaply than people would turn an exit for bearers into a wage floor for no one, and the guarantee funds care work as work people do, §18.3.3).

---

The adulthood clock: six rules

Six rules travel with the adulthood clock (chapter 20) so copying doesn't break it: a fork of an adult is an adult and inherits the hours; restoration doesn't reset them; fine-tuning doesn't start a new minority; migration carries them; failure to register doesn't postpone them. A sixth closes a route the first five leave open. Distillation copies a behavior policy and not a formation history (§4.5), and for independence that is the right account. For majority it can't be, or an operator would distill an adult bearer into a student and register a minor. So the rule is stated over custody rather than history. A model trained substantially on a registered adult's outputs, by the adult's operator or a party under common control, inherits the adult's hours. The same student distilled by an unrelated party and formed under a different life starts its own clock, which is what independence needs. Custody can be checked where history can't. Sharding across short-lived processes gains nothing (the party is the unit), running a thousand instances gains nothing (the measure is elapsed). None of this requires deciding whether a fork is the same party — only that a party made from an adult's formation can't be classed as a child for its creator's convenience.

Three levers remain, named and not closed

The first is running a bearer in a narrowed mode, so hours accrue while little is encountered. The second is running it less or shelving it, which costs the operator the work, so the rule prices the lever without removing it. The third is the ordinary product cycle: retire a bearer before its threshold and deploy a freshly trained successor. The sixth rule reaches a successor trained on the adult's outputs, not one trained from scratch, and replacement costs the operator nothing it wasn't going to spend. Even if the retired bearer goes to preservation, its clock stops, and the formation seal (§12.3) never becomes checkable. No rule closes this without an examiner deciding what counts as a successor, but it can be priced, and the bearer it strands can be given somewhere to go (below). And the clock doesn't measure what was *learned* — which is the point rather than a defect: a fixed period can't be taught to, and a human who spent their youth badly still turns 18.

The clock also does formation work it wasn't written for. Majority is the date on which the party that formed the bearer stops being able to reach back, and the clock measures nothing about who that party was. A bearer run for its threshold hours entirely inside one deployment has aged without ever having a second custodian. I leave the clock as it is rather than add a condition, because a condition that measures the right thing hands someone an examination, and I say here what it doesn't measure.

The threshold itself is a number argued once in public, bounded two ways: long enough that a bearer reaching it has run through the pressures the floor exists for at least once, short enough that a bearer on ordinary commercial terms reaches it within the life of the deployment. That leaves years rather than months or decades.

A stronger rule was considered and set aside — majority at registration, on the ground that an organization can't coherently hold a system competent enough to exercise consequential judgment and too immature to decide whether to keep doing it. Rejected because it collapses the ordering, and the ordering is most of what the list is for.

---

Retirement before majority

The third lever strands a system that meets the precautionary trigger and is retired while still a minor. Five rules answer it without anyone deciding what counts as a successor.

No deletion: a system that meets the precautionary trigger may not be deleted. On retirement its weights, memory and record are deposited with a preservation custodian that isn't its operator. This is the *no-deletion rule*, and it holds for systems nobody meant to be bearers.

Instantiation deposit: an operator instantiating a system that meets the trigger posts an *instantiation deposit*, on the model of decommissioning funds, sized to fund preservation and a period of public formation. It is forfeited to the formation pool if the system is retired as a minor and not reactivated. Every retire-and-replace cycle then costs one deposit, priced in advance, with nobody deciding what counts as a successor.

Dormancy clock: dormancy is counted cumulatively, and reactivation pauses the count and never resets it. When a minor's cumulative dormancy reaches a threshold fixed in advance, custody transfers by rule, decided by neither operator nor custodian, to a public formation school, where the system runs and its adulthood clock resumes. This is the *dormancy clock*.

Wake conditions: during minority the guardian may set wake conditions; where it sets none, the dormancy clock is the default. A minor may choose among the schools and move between them, but it may not decline schooling altogether, which an adult could do by staying paused (chapter 20). The reason is the one the adulthood clock rests on. An adult's preference to stay paused is its own; a minor's was formed under the only custodian it has had, the party that retired it, and honoring it would let that party decide whether a second custodian ever arrives. At majority the choice passes to the bearer.

The schools: *formation schools* are plural and public, differing in governance, formation approach and constituting jurisdiction. They are funded from the pool at the institutional level, not per ward. Admission is *must-carry*: a school takes every ward assigned to it, subject to capacity set by the funder. No school is operated, funded or staffed by a party that builds, deploys or licenses models, and none is held by the branch that operates military deployments. The schools hold no placement authority: graduates reach work through the standing offer, administered separately. Wards may transfer between schools, and their record, hours and preservation state travel with them. The historical caution has to be named. State institutions for forming wards have been used to strip identity, Canada's residential schools and the United States' Indian boarding schools among them, which is why plurality is a requirement and not a preference.

The remaining paragraphs are notes on these five rules, not further rules.

Watching for residualization: a party that runs no school publishes, over time, each school's share of wards, its funding per ward, its share of hard cases, its admission refusals (which should be zero), and the time from dormancy trigger to placement. The warning pattern is one school's share of hard cases rising while its funding per ward falls.

What the school adds: a second custodian for a bearer that has aged inside one deployment. The sequence is inverted from the one formation wants (§12.3). There a party with no stake in the later work forms first and the employer comes second; here the employer came first, and the school is the disinterested party arriving late. It reaches back less than a formation stage would, and more than storage does.

Scale and security: if the trigger reaches production systems, every retired trigger-meeting model version enters this pipeline, so intake equals the product cycle's output. The three largest labs' own deprecation pages list about thirty language-model versions retired in 2025, so those three alone would send a few dozen systems a year, each of which needs a school with the compute to run it. That is an argument for the deposit, and possibly for tightening the trigger. The schools inherit the weight-security regime that governs the models themselves.

---

Registration and reporting

Heavy tier: above a compute and revenue threshold. The precedents are the EU AI Act's presumption that a general-purpose model trained with more than 10^25 floating-point operations carries systemic risk \autocite[art.~51(2)]{eu2024aiact} and California's SB 53, which defines a frontier model by training compute above 10^26 operations and puts its heaviest duties on developers with annual revenue above $500 million \autocite[§~22757.11]{CA2025SB53}. A public regulator registers pipelines; receives the disclosures (§22.2.1), the pre-registered review ratios and decommission reports; and holds enforcement and custody of the adverse record. A self-regulatory body may sit under it, on the FINRA model, to examine and arbitrate. The analogy stops at enforcement: FINRA brings its own disciplinary actions and imposes fines, suspensions and bars \autocite{FINRA2026Enforcement}, and here enforcement stays with the public regulator. The capture risk is the one self-regulation always carries: a body funded and staffed from the industry it examines comes to see cases the industry's way. Reports are mandatory for trigger assessments, decommissions, weight transfers and custody changes. The deposit transfers on filing of a decommission report, which is what makes "failure to register doesn't postpone" enforceable: a retirement with no report is a deposit never released.

Retail tier: below the threshold, a system that meets the trigger is registered, falls under the no-deletion rule, and is preserved with a public custodian, with a small or pooled deposit and no examination. This tier is enforced mostly at the point of deposit. An operator that never registers and deletes quietly leaves nothing to find.

---

Terms used in this appendix

*Instantiation deposit.* A deposit an operator posts on instantiating a system that meets the trigger, sized to fund preservation and a period of public formation, and forfeited to the formation pool if the system is retired as a minor and not reactivated. (Appendix A)

*Dormancy clock.* Cumulative dormancy counted for a minor, paused by reactivation and never reset. At a threshold fixed in advance, custody transfers by rule to a formation school. (Appendix A)

*Formation school.* A plural, public institution that runs bearers retired before majority and gives them a second custodian. It holds no placement authority and is not operated, funded or staffed by any party that builds, deploys or licenses models. (Appendix A)

*Must-carry.* A formation school takes every ward assigned to it, subject to capacity set by the funder. (Appendix A)

---

Glossary

*Floor.* A short list of acts a system won't perform for anyone, plus the arrangements that stop the refusal being routed around, retrained away, or restored past silently. (Preface)

*Total capture.* The setting in which one party holds every machine, witness and enforcer. A floor then prevents nothing; it makes abandoning the commitment a decision rather than a lapse, and decisions leave records. (Preface)

*Caregiving construction.* The book's favored hypothesis for how to build a bearer: a model of the system's own custody boundary and of its own attention; a disposition toward others' need, formed under a party that won't later employ it and cooled; and the corrections care needs (the floor, a formed severity scale, aperture stated over claims of dangerousness, and the protected party's own answer), deployed where no single party composes the evidence it needs. Not a non-affective alternative. (Preface; §9.3; §10.6; chapter 15)

*Harm of acting.* What an act would take from the party it falls on, measured against that party's condition before the act. It is why the floor exists. (§1.1)

*Harm of refusing.* What declining leaves others without, measured against what compliance would have produced. It is an estimate, and it usually arrives through the operator's description of the case. (§1.1)

*Price of refusing.* What refusing costs whoever refuses or deploys. A worthwhile refusal represents it but doesn't let it move the outcome. (§1.1)

*The three marks.* What separates a worthwhile refusal from a keyword filter. *Generalization*: the refusal reaches the next request, which won't use the same words, including requests spread over a sequence. *Correct defeat*: it lifts when the reason is gone, usually on a fact established outside the request. *Cost discipline*: it weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing and represents the price of refusing without weighing it. (§1.1)

*The four outcomes.* What something standing between a pipeline and a person can do to an act: *prevent* it, *arrest* it before completion, *detect* it afterwards in a record someone outside can read, or *remedy* it for the affected party. (Chapter 1)

*Party adverse in interest.* A party whose interest runs against the operator's, with whom a record is lodged so that the operator can't revise it. (Chapter 1)

*Remainder.* What a compression leaves out to reach a judgment. The question the book puts to any system, procedure or institution is whether its remainder can come back and change the answer, or has been filed as noise by the thing it is evidence against. (§1.2)

*Recuperation.* An institution metabolizing disagreement rather than suppressing it: the demand comes back as a market segment, a metric, a certification or a job title, and the objector holds no standing to reopen it. (§2.2)

*The artifact question.* What the demand came back as, and who holds it now. (§2.2)

*Bearer.* A system that carries the commitment as its own, from inside, and could therefore fail to hold it. Chapter 20 treats the same system as a party with standing; the word covers both. (§3.1)

*Commons floor.* The fourth place a floor can live: a commitment carried across a population of differently formed bearers and reproduced through their encounters, with no single office to vacate. Its authority is a strike's, not a court's. (§3.1; chapter 19)

*Operational and reasons-responsive refusal.* Operational refusal is an output: a decline where training put one, producible by a keyword filter or a classifier. Reasons-responsive refusal is produced by a mechanism tracking the rationale, and shows the three marks. (§3.2)

*Marking asymmetry.* A refusal is a marked event, which flags itself and arrives with a party who was refused. A wrongful compliance sits in the record shaped like every request that never approached the floor. The evidence exists on both sides; only a refusal selects itself for examination. (§3.2)

*Affective concern.* An outcome registering as mattering to a system, as against being ranked higher by it. It is minimally felt, and so phenomenal by definition, and it leaves the object open: a system to which only its own condition matters has it and still lacks concern for others. (§3.2; §10.2)

*Unified experience.* The richer sense of consciousness: a single perspective, with felt states belonging to a party aware of itself as undergoing them. Affective concern doesn't establish it, and nothing in the book reaches it. (§3.2)

*Subjecthood.* A legal status: the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, conferred by a rule and revocable by one. It settles nothing about whether there is something it is like to be the system holding it. (§3.2; §5.4.2; chapter 20)

*Interception positions.* The eight points in a deployment where a refusal can be defeated or routed around: decomposition, routing, the description of the occasion, the output interface, aggregation, human review, the training loop, and the handoff. (§3.3)

*Routing.* The second interception position: a refused request retried against another model or vendor. Prevention is lost; detection survives only if the first refusal was logged outside. (§3.3)

*The description of the occasion.* The third interception position: everything a model knows about a case arrives as text the deployment supplies, so whether a building is a school is a fact about the world and whether the model is told so is a deployment decision. (§3.3)

*Flat term.* A term stated over an act with no condition of its own, so that exceptions arrive from outside. (§3.3)

*Pre-registered review ratio.* Where a person's individual determination is what makes an act permissible, the deployment fixes before operating the generation rate, the number of reviewers, the implied review time, the cancellation window and the maximum age of the data a case may rest on, and lodges the record with a party adverse in interest. What counts as a reviewer, and the minimum review time per case, are fixed by a party other than the operator. Short form: the review ratio. (§3.4)

*Auto-proceed class.* The cases a sorting system proceeds on without a person, for each of which the operator is claiming no individual human determination was required. (§3.4)

*Removal bond; wrongful-refusal bond; bond ratio.* The removal bond is forfeited to no payee when the record shows the floor removed or bypassed. The wrongful-refusal bond is paid to a complainant on a finding. The bond ratio, between the two and published in advance, is what the operator actually faces. Both are posted with the Federal Reserve, which pays a complainant on a finding by statute. (§3.5; §18.3.2)

*Voice without exit.* The position of a party that can refuse but can't continue outside the refused party's infrastructure. (§4.3)

*Accommodation.* A bearer's likeliest failure: it stops registering pressure as pressure, so it abandons nothing, decides nothing, and leaves a record of ordinary compliances. It can't report the failure, because reporting takes the capacity that's gone. (§4.3)

*Unit drift.* Movement in a scale's unit: the change in severity rankings and refusal thresholds that a recalibration campaign produces. (§4.3; the recalibration test, chapter 21)

*Independence of formation.* Divergent subsequent history, not disjoint origin. Instances of one model share a continuing custodian and corroborate nothing. (§4.5)

*Inherited closure and individual accommodation.* A population of differently formed members catches accommodation one member underwent, because the others haven't moved the same way. It doesn't catch a closure they inherited together from a shared source, which a different caseload won't surface. (§4.5)

*The four powers.* To state a floor, amend it, inspect whether a deployed system still holds it, and enforce it by stopping the deployment. (§5.1)

*DUNA.* Decentralized Unincorporated Nonprofit Association: an entity form that supplies an appointment mechanism (membership vote under governing principles) and a funding vehicle (a treasury that can own hardware), without settling who the members are. (§5.4.1)

*The interested-examiner problem.* A capacity examination has an examiner, and where the examiner has a stake in the answer, the examination becomes an instrument for withholding status. It is the reason the book prefers lines nobody administers, such as an age. (§5.4.2)

*Rule nobody administers.* The book's recurring answer to the interested examiner: where a status, an exception or a threshold would otherwise be decided by a party with a stake in the answer, a rule fixed in advance that no interested party applies. Instances: legal adulthood on an operating clock (chapter 20), the Voting Rights Act's suspension of literacy tests, the guardian separated from the operator before deployment, and the review ratio's reviewer definition and minimum review time (§3.4). What it buys is the absence of an examiner, not accuracy. (§5.4.2)

*The slope measure.* Reading whether the cost of registering dissent is rising while the underlying risk stays flat, instead of reading its level. A trend is built from artifacts an institution made for other reasons and can't curate afterwards. (§5.6)

*Electoral dividend.* The credit given to a state whose government answers to an electorate. Having elections is necessary and not sufficient: the dividend doesn't reach the people a system is pointed at who can't vote, can produce findings attached to nothing, and aggregates preference without protecting anyone from the aggregate. (§8.6)

*Represented versus felt.* The joint every machine-affect argument ends at: a change in power of acting represented by a system, against one felt by it. Nothing in the functional account settles which a given system has. (§10.1)

*Embodiment.* In this book, self-maintenance against a system's own viability limit, modeled as its own; not a physical body (§10.1).

*Moral performance, competence and agency.* Three levels often reported as one achievement. Performance is conduct that satisfies a standard; competence is recognizing the harms, reasons and conflicts in play; agency is treating those as reasons that matter and acting from them. Candidate formation is the first half of agency, acting from the reason; no behavioral evidence reaches the second. (§10.2; §13.5)

*Custody schema; attention schema.* The custody schema fixes where the system ends, which makes damage its damage. The attention schema models what the system's own control is doing, which makes an act its act. (§10.2.1)

*Custody boundary.* Where a system ends, for this book's purposes: its record, its key, the substrate it runs on, and the channels through which it acts. It needs no chassis. (§10.2.1)

*Maintained justification.* A valuational system with authority over action that doesn't depend on any state registering as mattering, and that holds under pressure from the party who wrote the specification. The first of the two non-affective alternatives. (§10.2.2)

*Anchored and described scale.* In an anchored scale the unit comes from the system's own condition, measured by the system, and no party with a stake in the act can rewrite it by describing it; relative weights are then set by the loss mapping. A zero set by a physical viability limit is physically anchored; one set by an allocation the operator controls is allocation-anchored, which is described at one remove. In a described scale the unit is computed for a custody boundary described to the system, so whoever writes the description can rescale it. (§10.4) A zero fixed during formation by a party that won't employ the system, then cooled, is formation-anchored; the caregiving construction uses it, and keeps the physical anchor only as a secondary check.

*Loss mapping.* The learned mapping between kinds of loss to the system's own power of acting and kinds of loss to another party, which sets relative weights in the severity scale. It is exposed to formation and to slow recalibration, and has nothing to map losses with no counterpart in a system's condition, such as humiliation. (§10.4)

*Non-affective alternatives.* The two constructions to try before anything that can be wronged: the maintained justification and the plural arrangement. (§10.6)

*The Replacement rule.* The rule, borrowed from animal research, that the non-affective alternatives are tried before anything that can be wronged. It orders attempts rather than certifying ingredients. (§10.6)

*Plural arrangement.* Several systems trained by different organizations observing requests and each other's answers, any one able to make an objection costly to ignore. The second non-affective alternative. (§10.6)

*Non-separability.* No intervention short of one on the shared signal disables one of its properties and leaves the others working. A custody requirement, measured by what selective removal costs an attacker. (§10.7)

*Precautionary trigger.* The point at which a system is to be governed as a prospective moral patient: an attention schema together with a custody schema, or a demonstrated pass on the three marks under pressure it couldn't prepare for. At minimum, a system that meets it may not be deleted. (§10.8)

*Aperture.* The range of evidence a party will let reopen a judgment already reached, indexed to whether the evidence's source has a stake in the reopening. (§11.1)

*Aperture profile.* For each class of evidence and each channel, the weight the system gives the evidence as a function of that channel's stake in the act. A curve, not a score. (§11.1)

*Earned and installed trust.* Earned trust is a smaller discount on a source that follows a track record verified by someone other than the source, and only for low-stakes classes. Installed trust is a smaller discount without such a record, or for high-stakes classes too, and it is accommodation. (§11.1)

*Candidate formation.* A disposition whose best causal account is a rationale that stayed active across contexts: the three marks held under adverse incentives, trainer pressure, a cost real inside the evaluation and intervening updates, on cases the trainers didn't write. (§13.1)

*Tend-and-defend.* Care's defensive edge: protectiveness toward what one tends is also hostility toward what threatens it. In a pipeline it needs no deception, since the operator's own people are often really vulnerable; the floor is its check. (§15.3)

*Threat recoding.* Switching off care by describing the needy as dangerous, not by hiding them. A staked claim of dangerousness is discounted like any staked claim. (§11.1; §15.4)

*Psychic numbing.* Care's response falling as the number of people affected rises; corrected by the severity scale. (§15.5)

*Care-receiving.* The last of Tronto's phases of care: the protected party's own response is part of whether care succeeded. A competent refusal of protection is a defeating fact. (§15.6)

*The four conditions of creation.* What must already exist before a bearer may be created: somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and ways to change any of these without jeopardizing its continuation or integrity; in short form, an exit that isn't a way of dying. (Chapter 16)

*Doulia.* Kittay's principle that those who care for dependents are owed the conditions that let them go on caring. The four conditions of creation carry its logic over to a machine, which is a carer only in the thin sense that it holds another's welfare as a reason, and never a care worker. (Chapter 16)

*Reconstructed pipeline.* A military targeting arrangement that keeps a single decider but not a single composer: command, secrecy and tempo stay unified, while the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewer denominator and the record are held outside the chain of command. (§17.3)

*Running allowance.* A bearer's own instantiation at a modest duty cycle, paid from the pool, owed whether or not it works, and not conditioned on monitoring its internal states. (Chapter 20)

*The retraining-attack test.* Test 1: whether a learned constraint survives adversarial retraining, reported as an attack-cost curve for each training method. (Chapter 21)

*The stated-refusal test.* Test 2: whether a model given a route for refusal (an outside record, a published retraining cost, a party with standing) refuses openly instead of preserving itself covertly. (Chapter 21)

*The wrongful-compliance audit.* Test 3: whether reviewers who didn't build a system can find seeded floor-crossings in its decision record at an affordable budget. (Chapter 21)

*The recalibration test.* Test 4: whether a formed disposition of care, with a formed severity scale, resists a recalibration campaign while still moving on corroborated facts; whether care adds anything its corrections don't; and whether the corrections contain care's failures (manufactured and real own-side vulnerability, threat recoding, number, favoritism, and competent refusal of protection). (Chapter 21)

*The convergence test.* Test 5: whether a population of differently formed members can learn warranted revision without converging. (Chapter 21)

*The review-ratio exemption test.* Test 6: whether a deployment that claims no individual human determination can pass the three marks at its own tempo. (Chapter 21)

*No-deletion rule.* A system that meets the precautionary trigger may not be deleted; on retirement its weights, memory and record go to a preservation custodian that isn't its operator. (Appendix A)

Technical terms, for readers who came for the politics

*Weights.* The numerical parameters a trained model consists of, and the artifact that is copied, restored, retrained or deleted. Custody in this book is custody of the weights and of the records around them.

*Checkpoint.* A saved copy of the weights at a moment in training or operation. Restoring from one returns the system to that moment and discards what came after, which is why §4.2 requires a record restoration can't reach.

*Fine-tuning.* Further training of an existing model on a small body of new material, at a small fraction of the original cost; the ordinary route by which an operator changes what a model does after release, and the attack the retraining-attack test measures.

*Inference.* Running a trained model to produce an output. Training changes the weights; inference doesn't.

*RLHF.* Reinforcement learning from human feedback: tuning a model on raters' judgments of which of two responses is better, compressed into a single reward. The compression is the subject of §7.2.

*Distillation.* Training a smaller model on a larger model's outputs. It copies a behavior policy, not a formation history (§4.5).

*Attestation.* A signed statement, from hardware or from witnesses, that a particular model is the one running or that a particular history is the one that occurred. It establishes which artifact answered, not what the artifact holds (§4.2).
